# QA1 — Winthrop / Tong (Index Benchmarks, Replication, Equitizing With Futures, Securities Lending)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QA1*

### Q1 — Requirements for an Index-Based Strategy [QA-1]

**Context (QA1):**
Tong's statements: (1) a free-float adjustment to a market-cap weighted index lowers its liquidity; (2) an index provider using a buffering policy makes the index more investable.

**Question:**
Which of Tong’s statements regarding equity index benchmarks is (are) correct?

A. Only Statement 1
B. Only Statement 2
C. Both Statement 1 and Statement 2

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Statement 1 is wrong: a free-float adjustment excludes shares held by founders, governments or other companies, so index weights reflect true liquidity. It does not lower it.
- **B is correct.** **Only Statement 2.** An index must be rule based, transparent and investable. Buffering makes transitions gradual and orderly, so the index is more investable.
- **C is wrong.** Statement 1 is wrong, so 'both' can't be right.

**Key Principle:**
> Investable index = rule based + transparent + investable. Float adjustment and buffering improve investability.

### Q2 — Benchmark Matching Low Beta, High Yield, Low P/E and P/B [QA-2]

**Context (QA1):**
Winthrop wants a Canadian equity benchmark covering all sectors, highly liquid well-known companies, low beta, high dividend yield, low P/E and low P/B.

**Question:**
To satisfy Winthrop’s benchmark and security selection specifications, the Canadian equity index benchmark Tong selects should be:

A. small capitalization with a core tilt.
B. large capitalization with a value tilt.
C. mid-capitalization with a growth tilt.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Small caps are riskier than large caps; Winthrop wants low beta.
- **B is correct.** **Large cap with a value tilt.** Large caps are the most liquid and lower risk; value stocks have high yields and low P/E and P/B.
- **C is wrong.** Growth stocks have high P/Es, high momentum and EPS growth, which conflicts with his low-P/E requirement.

**Key Principle:**
> Large cap = liquid. Value = high yield, low P/E, low P/B. Growth = high P/E.

### Q3 — Which ETF Has the Highest Expected Tracking Error [QA-3]

**Context (QA1):**
Full replication. Constituents: XIU 60 (expense 0.18%), SPY 506 (0.10%), EFA 933 (0.33%).

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 1 and assuming a full-replication indexing approach, the tracking error is expected to be highest for:

A. XIU.
B. SPY.
C. EFA.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** XIU has the fewest constituents (60), so the lowest tracking error potential.
- **B is wrong.** SPY has 506 constituents, fewer than EFA, and the lowest expense ratio (0.10%).
- **C is correct.** **EFA.** More constituents → higher tracking error under full replication (more trading, harder to hold every name at exact weights), and EFA also has the highest expense ratio (0.33%).

**Key Principle:**
> Tracking error rises with number of constituents, and costs (fees) drag excess return.

### Q4 — Identifying Full Replication [QA-4]

**Context (QA1):**
Method 1: requires all index constituents to be available for trading and liquid; significant brokerage commissions can occur when the index is large.

**Question:**
Method 1’s portfolio construction process is most likely:

A. optimization.
B. full replication.
C. stratified sampling.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Optimization uses sophisticated software and covariances to build a portfolio with fewer securities.
- **B is correct.** **Full replication.** The manager holds every index security at index weights, needing all constituents to be liquid and tradable, and sufficient assets. Commissions can be large when the index is large.
- **C is wrong.** Stratified sampling holds only a sample from each strata, so it doesn't need all constituents.

**Key Principle:**
> Full replication: hold all index securities at index weights; costly for large indexes.

### Q5 — Identifying Stratified Sampling [QA-5]

**Context (QA1):**
Method 2: for an index with many constituents and/or low assets under management; simple and technically unsophisticated; fewer securities than the index, lower commissions.

**Question:**
Method 2’s portfolio construction process is most likely:

A. optimization.
B. full replication.
C. stratified sampling.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Optimization needs technical sophistication (software/algorithms), so it isn't 'simple'.
- **B is wrong.** Full replication holds all securities.
- **C is correct.** **Stratified sampling.** Used for indexes with many constituents or when assets are small; the portfolio holds a sample from each stratum.

**Key Principle:**
> Stratified sampling: simple, fewer securities. Optimization: sophisticated.

### Q6 — Revenue to Offset Costs: Securities Lending [QA-6]

**Context (QA1):**
Tong adds that portfolio stocks may generate incremental revenue, offsetting administrative costs but creating counterparty and collateral risks.

**Question:**
The method that Tong suggests to add incremental revenue is:

A. program trading.
B. securities lending.
C. attribution analysis.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Program trading is buying or selling many stocks at once; it doesn't generate revenue.
- **B is correct.** **Securities lending.** The lender earns fees that offset management costs, but is exposed to the borrower's credit risk (counterparty) and to market risk on the posted collateral.
- **C is wrong.** Attribution analysis explains sources of return; it doesn't generate revenue.

**Key Principle:**
> Securities lending income offsets fund costs; risks: counterparty and collateral.

### Q7 — Futures Needed to Equitize a USD Bonus [QA-7]

**Context (QA1):**
USD 5,750,000 bonus arrives in one month; 15% policy weight to US equity; S&P 500 futures price 2,464.29; E-mini multiplier 50.

**Question:**
In preparation for receipt of the performance bonus, Tong should immediately:

A. buy two US E-mini equity futures contracts.
B. sell nine US E-mini equity futures contracts.
C. buy seven US E-mini equity futures contracts.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Two contracts would cover only about 29% of the target exposure.
- **B is wrong.** The view is bullish, so she buys, not sells.
- **C is correct.** **Buy 7 E-mini contracts.** US equity allocation = 15% × 5,750,000 = USD 862,500. Contract value = 2,464.29 × 50 = USD 123,214.50. N = 862,500 / 123,214.50 = 7.00 (6.99999).

**Key Principle:**
> Contracts = (allocation × cash) / (futures price × multiplier).

### Q8 — Risk of Using Equity Index Futures [QA-8]

**Context (QA1):**
Tong cautions that futures returns can differ from the underlying index, primarily because of dividends and splits.

**Question:**
The risk that Tong discloses regarding the equity futures strategy is most likely:

A. basis risk.
B. currency risk.
C. counterparty risk.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Basis risk.** The hedging/overlay instrument doesn't match the exposure perfectly; futures track only the index price, so dividends cause differences. (Stock splits don't affect performance comparisons.)
- **B is wrong.** Not the risk she described: the difference she cites comes from dividends and splits, not currency.
- **C is wrong.** Not the risk she described: she cites differences between futures and index returns.

**Key Principle:**
> Futures vs index differences (dividends, mismatches) = basis risk.

# QA2 — Mackenzie Foundation / McMahon (Factor Strategies, HHI, Sector Attribution, Tracking Error)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QA2*

### Q9 — Factor-Based Momentum vs Market-Cap Weighting [QA-9]

**Context (QA2):**
McMahon suggests a factor-based momentum strategy for international stocks.

**Question:**
Compared with broad-market-cap weighting, the international equity strategy suggested by McMahon is most likely to:

A. concentrate risk exposure.
B. be based on the efficient market hypothesis.
C. overweight stocks that recently experienced large price decreases.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Concentrate risk exposure.** Factor strategies concentrate risk in the chosen factor, leaving investors vulnerable when that factor is out of favor.
- **B is wrong.** Momentum conflicts with market efficiency (it's not based on the EMH); cap weighting is the one closer to it.
- **C is wrong.** Momentum overweights stocks that have recently gone up, not those that fell.

**Key Principle:**
> Single-factor strategies concentrate risk (vs cap-weighted).

### Q10 — Classifying a Momentum Strategy [QA-10]

**Context (QA2):**
Factor-based momentum strategy (excess price return vs market over a period).

**Question:**
The international strategy suggested by McMahon is most likely characterized as:

A. risk based.
B. return oriented.
C. diversification oriented.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Risk-based strategies reduce volatility (e.g., low volatility).
- **B is correct.** **Return oriented.** Momentum is a return-oriented factor.
- **C is wrong.** Diversification-oriented strategies include equal weighting and similar.

**Key Principle:**
> Factor strategy types: return oriented (momentum, value), risk oriented (low vol), diversification oriented (equal weight).

### Q11 — What a Price-Weighted Index Holds [QA-11]

**Context (QA2):**
The US large-cap benchmark is price-weighted, 150 stocks.

**Question:**
The initial benchmark used for the US large-cap allocation:

A. is unaffected by stocks splits.
B. is essentially a liquidity-weighted index.
C. holds the same number of shares in each component stock.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Price-weighted indexes are affected by splits (a split cuts the price and weight).
- **B is wrong.** Liquidity-weighted isn't a feature of price weighting.
- **C is correct.** **Holds the same number of shares in each stock.** Weight = price / sum of prices, i.e., a portfolio of one share of each constituent.

**Key Principle:**
> Price-weighted index = one share of each stock; splits change weights.

### Q12 — Effective Number of Stocks From the HHI [QA-12]

**Context (QA2):**
HHI of the benchmark = 0.0286.

**Question:**
Based on its HHI, the initial US large-cap benchmark most likely has:

A. a concentration level of 4.29.
B. an effective number of stocks of approximately 35.
C. individual stocks held in approximately equal weights.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 4.29 isn't a concentration measure here; the HHI is 0.0286.
- **B is correct.** **≈35 effective stocks.** HHI = Σ w_i²; effective N = 1/HHI = 1/0.0286 = 34.97.
- **C is wrong.** Equal weights across 150 stocks would give an HHI of 1/150 = 0.0067; 0.0286 means a concentrated cap/price structure, so weights are not approximately equal.

**Key Principle:**
> HHI = Σ w²; effective number of stocks = 1/HHI.

### Q13 — Sector Contributing Most to Underperformance [QA-13]

**Context (QA2):**
Contribution = sector return × weight. Foundation vs benchmark weights: IT 18.71% vs 19.06%; Staples 16.52% vs 16.10%; Energy 9.38% vs 9.53%; Utilities 8.76% vs 8.25%; Financials 6.89% vs 6.62%.

**Question:**
Using a sector attribution analysis based on Exhibit 1, which US large-cap sector is the primary contributor to the portfolio’s underperformance relative to the benchmark?

A. Utilities
B. Consumer staples
C. Information technology

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Utilities difference is −0.02% (−0.34% vs −0.32%), smaller than IT's.
- **B is wrong.** Consumer staples difference is +0.05%, positive.
- **C is correct.** **Information technology.** Contribution difference (foundation − benchmark) is −0.04% (2.01% vs 2.05%), the largest negative. IT had a positive return but was underweight.

**Key Principle:**
> Simple sector attribution: (weight × return) difference vs benchmark; underweighting a positive-return sector hurts.

### Q14 — Lowest Tracking Error Portfolio [QA-14]

**Context (QA2):**
Portfolio fees: 0.10%, 0.09%, 0.07%; cash: 6.95%, 3.42%, 2.13%.

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 2, which portfolio will most likely have the lowest tracking error?

A. Portfolio 1
B. Portfolio 2
C. Portfolio 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Highest cash (6.95%, cash drag) and highest fee.
- **B is wrong.** Middle on both.
- **C is correct.** **Portfolio 3.** Lowest cash holding (2.13%) and lowest fee (0.07%).

**Key Principle:**
> Cash drag and fees raise tracking error.

# QA3 — Camme / Parker (Factor Approaches, Replication Managers, Rebalancing)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QA3*

### Q15 — Most Accurate Comment on Factor Approaches [QA-15]

**Context (QA3):**
Parker: value factor funds seek to lower downside risk; fundamental weighting overweights overpriced and underweights underpriced stocks; single-factor funds concentrate risk relative to cap weighting.

**Question:**
Which comment by Parker regarding factor-based approaches is most accurate? The comment regarding:

A. value factor funds.
B. single-factor funds.
C. fundamental weighting.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Value funds focus on valuation measures, not volatility.
- **B is correct.** **Single-factor funds.** Relative to cap weighting they concentrate risk in the factor's characteristic.
- **C is wrong.** Fundamental weighting is meant to do the opposite: overweight stocks priced below intrinsic value and underweight overpriced ones.

**Key Principle:**
> Single-factor → concentrated risk. Fundamental weighting targets mispricing.

### Q16 — Manager Blending Stratified Sampling and Optimization [QA-16]

**Context (QA3):**
| | Manager A | Manager B | Manager C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Benchmark | S&P 500 | MSCI EAFE | Russell 3000 |
| Holdings fund/index | 498/500 | 800/928 | 1,200/3,000 |
| Futures | cash drag | currency overlay | none |
| Accounts for covariances | No | No | Yes |
| Tracking error | 0.06% | 1.97% | 0.34% |
| Excess return | 0.10% | 2.10% | −0.15% |

**Question:**
In Exhibit 1, the replication manager that most likely blends stratified sampling and optimization is:

A. Manager A.
B. Manager B.
C. Manager C.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Manager A holds 498 of 500 with tiny tracking error: full replication.
- **B is wrong.** Manager B holds 800 of 928 (sampling) and ignores covariances (no optimization), with a large tracking error.
- **C is correct.** **Manager C.** Holds 1,200 of 3,000 (sampling) and accounts for covariances (optimization), giving lower tracking error than sampling alone.

**Key Principle:**
> Covariances accounted for → optimization. Fewer holdings than index → sampling.

### Q17 — Evaluating Statements About Manager B [QA-17]

**Context (QA3):**
Manager B (MSCI EAFE, tracking error 1.97%, excess return 2.10%, currency overlay). Parker: (1) he faced more volatile markets, from tracking errors; (2) he used currency overlays to lever returns; (3) his excess return looks like luck, not skill.

**Question:**
Which of Parker’s statements about Manager B in Exhibit 1 is most appropriate? The statement about:

A. excess return.
B. tracking errors.
C. currency overlays.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Excess return.** A replication manager aims to track the index; the proper measure of skill is tracking error, and B's is the highest of the three. Excess return of 2.10% is then luck, not skill.
- **B is wrong.** Tracking error measures the volatility of excess return, not market volatility.
- **C is wrong.** A currency overlay hedges foreign-currency returns; it doesn't lever them.

**Key Principle:**
> Tracking error = volatility of (portfolio − index) return; high TE with high excess return suggests luck.

### Q18 — Primary Strategy of a Multi-Factor Fund [QA-18]

**Context (QA3):**
MultiFAK vs benchmark: Yield 28%/28%; Momentum 14%/17%; Low Volatility 31%/28%; Quality 27%/27%; 91 vs 100 stocks.

**Question:**
From Exhibit 2, MultiFAK’s primary strategy is most likely:

A. risk reduction.
B. diversification.
C. return oriented.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Risk reduction.** Overweights low volatility, underweights momentum (return oriented), and holds fewer securities (not diversification).
- **B is wrong.** Fewer securities than the index isn't a diversification approach.
- **C is wrong.** It underweights momentum, the return-oriented factor.

**Key Principle:**
> Overweight low volatility = risk-oriented strategy.

### Q19 — Excess Return From Active Factor Weighting [QA-19]

**Context (QA3):**
MultiFAK weight/return: Yield 0.28/3.5%; Momentum 0.14/8.1%; Low Vol 0.31/9.3%; Quality 0.27/4.9%. Benchmark: 0.28/3.5%; 0.17/8.1%; 0.28/9.3%; 0.27/4.1%.

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 2, the excess return of MultiFAK arising from active factor weighting is closest to:

A. 0.04%.
B. 0.25%.
C. 0.28%.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **0.04%.** Only Momentum and Low Volatility weights differ: (0.14 − 0.17)(8.1%) + (0.31 − 0.28)(9.3%) = −0.243% + 0.279% = 0.036% ≈ 0.04%. Quality's return gap (4.9% vs 4.1%) is security selection (0.216%), not factor weighting.
- **B is wrong.** 0.25% is the total active effect (0.252%): factor weighting plus Quality selection.
- **C is wrong.** 0.28% is the Low Volatility overweight alone and ignores the Momentum underweight.

**Key Principle:**
> Factor weighting effect = Σ (w_p − w_b) × factor return (same return in both); selection = w × (R_p − R_b).

### Q20 — Cost-Efficient Fix After a Market Correction [QA-20]

**Context (QA3):**
Allocation drifted to 51% equity, 2% cash, 47% fixed income vs policy 60/40 (±5%), cash max 2%. IPS permits active managers and derivatives (cash drag/allocation guidelines achievable with derivatives, notional ≤10% of assets); no index ETFs.

**Question:**
The most cost efficient strategy to deal with Dashe’s concerns following the equity market correction is a(n):

A. overlay using equity index futures.
B. rebalancing to policy weights by selling bonds and purchasing equities.
C. rebalancing by replacing the highest-tracking-error manager with low-cost index exchange-traded funds (ETFs).

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Overlay with equity index futures.** Brings equity exposure to the guideline range without disrupting the active managers and with low tracking error.
- **B is wrong.** Selling bonds and buying equities incurs trading costs and disrupts managers.
- **C is wrong.** The IPS doesn't allow index ETFs.

**Key Principle:**
> Futures overlay = cheapest way to rebalance toward policy weights.

# QA4 — Serinch / PGQ (Top-50 and Top-10 Index Products, Tracking Without Derivatives)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QA4*

### Q21 — Sources of Positive Quarterly Excess Return for an Index ETF [QA-21] (Essay)

**Context (QA4):**
PGQ tracks the S&P 500 Top 50 (float-adjusted, quarterly rebalance with buffer) via replication; positive excess returns in some quarters with no constituent changes.

**Question:**
Discuss one potential source of positive quarterly excess returns for PGQ, including how it contributes.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Any one of:
- **Securities lending:** long holders' shares are in demand by short sellers; lending income offsets costs and, for low-cost index funds, can make net expenses negative, so the fund earns more than the index.
- **Cash drag in a falling market:** equity indexes hold no cash, so cash from dividends, sale proceeds and investor contributions creates tracking error; when the market falls, uninvested cash doesn't suffer the index decline, giving positive excess return (negative when the market rises).

**Key Principle:**
> Index fund excess return sources: securities lending income; cash drag in down markets.

### Q22 — Factors Characterizing a Top-10 Index Product [QA-22] (Essay)

**Context (QA4):**
Exhibit 1: S&P 500 Top 10 vs S&P 500: P/E 32.8 vs 26.5; P/B 26.7 vs 4.6; yield 0.45% vs 1.19%; average market cap $880bn vs $97bn; 10-yr return 18.95% vs 13.06%; 10-yr volatility 19.04% vs 15.29%.

**Question:**
Identify two factors other than Market that could characterize the product and justify each with one reason.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Any two of:
- **Growth (or value):** higher mean P/E than the S&P 500 (positive growth / negative value exposure).
- **Size:** larger mean float-adjusted market cap (negative relative exposure to size).
- **Yield:** lower dividend yield (negative exposure to yield).
- **Momentum:** return history showing greater momentum (positive exposure).
- **Volatility:** higher annualized volatility (negative exposure to low volatility).

**Key Principle:**
> Characterize an index by its tilt on factors: size, value/growth, yield, momentum, volatility, quality.

### Q23 — Tracking an Index With No Listed Derivatives: Equity Swap [QA-23] (Essay)

**Context (QA4):**
Ultra-Cap 10 SMA ($12m) must track the S&P 500 Top 10; no full replication, sampling or optimization; and not by building a portfolio of the index's securities. No US ETFs, funds or listed derivatives track this index.

**Question:**
Discuss a portfolio management approach for the account and how it generates returns that track the index.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**An equity index swap.** Futures/options don't exist for this index, and its tracking error vs listed-futures indexes is too large to proxy; a swap can be set up on virtually any index with a willing counterparty.

OTC contract for a set period: the account **receives the index return** and **pays** the counterparty a market reference rate (typically floating) plus an agreed fee. It needs no trading by PAM and little information beyond the index return.

**Key Principle:**
> No listed derivatives and can't hold constituents → OTC equity index swap (receive index return, pay MRR + spread).

# QA5 — Index Analyst (Benchmark Selection by Factor Exposure)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QA5*

### Q24 — Index for a Risk-Oriented Strategy [QA-24]

**Context (QA5):**
Factor exposures: Index 1 (equal weighted): Market 0.45, Size 0.33, Value 0.41, Yield 0.24, Momentum 0.01, Quality 0.32. Index 2 (price weighted): 0.90, −0.16, −0.06, 0.08, 0.54, 0.29. Index 3 (volatility weighted): 0.88, −0.38, −0.23, 0.33, −0.01, 0.46.

**Question:**
Which benchmark index would be most appropriate for a risk-oriented strategy?

A. Index 1
B. Index 2
C. Index 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Index 1 is equal weighted (diversification) with a small-cap tilt.
- **B is wrong.** Index 2 is price weighted with a momentum tilt (return oriented).
- **C is correct.** **Index 3.** Volatility weighting plus large-cap, high-quality and yield exposures fit a strategy aimed at reducing downside volatility and overall risk.

**Key Principle:**
> Risk-oriented → volatility-weighted/low-vol, quality, large cap.

### Q25 — Index for a Diversification-Oriented Strategy [QA-25]

**Context (QA5):**
Index 1 equal weighted; Index 2 price weighted; Index 3 volatility weighted.

**Question:**
Which benchmark index would be most appropriate for a diversification-oriented strategy?

A. Index 1
B. Index 2
C. Index 3

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Index 1.** Equal weighting reduces single-stock risk relative to the others.
- **B is wrong.** Price weighting isn't a diversification approach.
- **C is wrong.** Volatility weighting is risk oriented.

**Key Principle:**
> Equal weighting = diversification-oriented.

### Q26 — Index for a Small-Cap Value, High-Yield Investor [QA-26]

**Context (QA5):**
Jones invests in small-cap, low price-to-book, high-dividend-yield stocks.

**Question:**
Which benchmark index would be most appropriate for Jones?

A. Index 1
B. Index 2
C. Index 3

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Index 1.** Positive Size (0.33), Value (0.41) and Yield (0.24) exposures match.
- **B is wrong.** Index 2 has negative size and value exposures (large-cap growth).
- **C is wrong.** Index 3 has negative size and value exposures (large-cap growth).

**Key Principle:**
> Positive Size and Value loadings = small-cap value.

### Q27 — Index for a Downside-Risk-Sensitive, Large-Cap Investor [QA-27]

**Context (QA5):**
Smith is concerned about downside risk and believes small caps offer inadequate returns for their risk.

**Question:**
Which benchmark index would be most appropriate for Smith?

A. Index 1
B. Index 2
C. Index 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Index 1 has a small-cap focus.
- **B is wrong.** Index 2 has lower quality, size and yield exposures than Index 3.
- **C is correct.** **Index 3.** Large-cap tilt (Size −0.38) and the highest quality exposure (0.46), with volatility weighting.

**Key Principle:**
> Downside-sensitive, no small caps → large cap, high quality, volatility weighted.

# QA6 — Marketdex (Low-Volatility Europe ETF vs Mutual Fund)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QA6*

### Q28 — Construction Approach of Low-Volatility Europe Funds [QA-28]

**Context (QA6):**
LVEU ETF: 98 holdings; LVEUX mutual fund: 99 holdings; index tracks 205 stocks. Equity futures only for cash flows; no listed futures on the index.

**Question:**
The type of investment and portfolio construction approach most likely used by both the LVEU ETF and the LVEUX mutual fund is:

A. full replication.
B. derivatives-based.
C. stratified sampling with optimization.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Holdings are about half the 205 index stocks, so not full replication.
- **B is wrong.** A derivatives approach wouldn't hold 98–99 issues and no suitable derivatives exist for this index.
- **C is correct.** **Stratified sampling with optimization.** About half the constituents are held, and tracking error is low, consistent with sampling plus optimization limiting smaller holdings.

**Key Principle:**
> Fewer holdings than index + low tracking error → sampling with optimization.

### Q29 — Why ETF Tracking Error Is Higher Than the Mutual Fund's [QA-29]

**Context (QA6):**
Index and mutual fund (LVEUX) prices use European primary-market closes (converted at that time); the ETF (LVEU) trades in the US until the US close. Management fees: 0.22% vs 0.24%.

**Question:**
The factor that increases the published tracking error of the LVEU ETF but not the LVEUX mutual fund is most likely the:

A. effect of management fees.
B. effect of market bid–ask spreads on trade prices.
C. difference in pricing practices between the index and the fund.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Fees differ by only 2 bp, minimal and in the ETF's favor.
- **B is wrong.** ETF daily returns use closing prices that don't include spreads; authorized participant creations/redemptions are in kind with no spread cost to the fund.
- **C is correct.** **Pricing practices.** The index and the mutual fund use European closing prices and FX at that time; the ETF's price is its last US trade, reflecting later FX and sentiment moves, which raises its published tracking error.

**Key Principle:**
> ETF price (US close) vs index (primary-market close) timing mismatch → published tracking error.

### Q30 — Investor Cost Unique to the ETF [QA-30]

**Context (QA6):**
Purchase costs: ETF commissions ≈0.25%, mutual fund load 0.25%.

**Question:**
The factor that decreases a fund investor’s realized excess return in the LVEU ETF but not the LVEUX mutual fund is most likely the:

A. effect of management fees.
B. effect of market bid–ask spreads on trade prices.
C. difference in pricing practices between the index and the fund.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Fees are lower for the ETF (0.22% vs 0.24%).
- **B is correct.** **Bid–ask spreads.** ETF investors trade at market prices and bear the spread; mutual fund shares trade directly with the fund at the daily price with no spread.
- **C is wrong.** Pricing-practice differences affect tracking error randomly, not consistently negatively for investors.

**Key Principle:**
> ETF investors pay bid–ask spreads; mutual fund investors transact at NAV.

### Q31 — Securities Lending Impact on Excess Return [QA-31]

**Context (QA6):**
Both CMR funds participate in securities lending.

**Question:**
The impact on fund excess returns of securities lending activity is most likely:

A. positive for both funds.
B. negative for both funds.
C. positive for one fund and negative for the other.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Positive for both.** Lending revenue always adds to performance, though its amount is unpredictable.
- **B is wrong.** Lending generates revenue; it doesn't hurt returns.
- **C is wrong.** Both funds participate, so both benefit.

**Key Principle:**
> Securities lending revenue contributes positively to excess return.

# QA7 — Cullen / Stapleton (Funds vs ETFs, Factor Strategies, Subgroup Sampling)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QA7*

### Q32 — Mutual Funds vs ETFs: Which Comment Is Accurate [QA-32]

**Context (QA7):**
Comments: (1) both can be bought on margin; (2) investors can short ETFs but not mutual funds; (3) both have the same liquidity.

**Question:**
In comparing mutual funds and ETFs, Cullen is most accurate in:

A. Comment 1.
B. Comment 2.
C. Comment 3.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Only ETF investors can buy on margin, not mutual fund investors.
- **B is correct.** **Comment 2.** Only ETF shares can be sold short.
- **C is wrong.** Liquidity differs: ETFs trade intraday but can be illiquid; mutual fund units are redeemed with the fund.

**Key Principle:**
> ETFs: shortable, marginable, intraday. Mutual funds: redeemed at NAV.

### Q33 — Factor-Based vs Market-Cap Weighting: Evaluating Claims [QA-33]

**Context (QA7):**
Stapleton: relative to market-cap weighting, factor-based strategies tend to diversify risk exposures; are transparent; but can be copied, reducing the advantage.

**Question:**
When comparing factor-based strategies relative to the market-cap weighting of an index, Stapleton’s comments are most likely:

A. correct.
B. incorrect regarding transparency.
C. incorrect regarding risk exposure.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Not entirely correct: the risk exposure claim is wrong.
- **B is wrong.** Transparency claim is correct: factor selection, weighting and rebalancing are transparent (and replicable, causing crowding).
- **C is correct.** **Incorrect regarding risk exposure.** Factor strategies tend to *concentrate* risk exposures, hurting when the factor is out of favor.

**Key Principle:**
> Factor strategies: transparent and replicable (crowding), concentrate risk.

### Q34 — Share of Excess Return From Active Factor Weighting [QA-34]

**Context (QA7):**
River Valley vs benchmark (weight/return): Growth 0.22/7.9% vs 0.25/7.9%; Value 0.19/5.2% vs 0.19/5.2%; Quality 0.29/6.7% vs 0.26/6.7%; Momentum 0.30/3.9% vs 0.30/4.5%. Totals: 5.84% vs 6.06%.

**Question:**
In Exhibit 1, the percentage of the excess return of the River Valley Fund arising from active factor weighting is closest to:

A. –0.22%.
B. –0.04%.
C. 18.18%.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** −0.22% is the total active effect, not the share.
- **B is wrong.** −0.04% is the factor-weight effect before dividing by the total effect.
- **C is correct.** **18.18%.** Factor-weighting effect: Growth (0.22 − 0.25)(7.9%) = −0.24%; Quality (0.29 − 0.26)(6.7%) = +0.20%; sum = −0.04%. Total effect = 5.84% − 6.06% = −0.22%. Share = −0.04/−0.22 = 18.18%. (Momentum's shortfall of −0.18% is security selection.)

**Flag:** The key's 18.18% uses table values rounded to two decimals (−0.04 / −0.22). Unrounded I get −0.036 / −0.216 = 16.7%. The answer (18.18%) is still the only sensible choice among those offered.

**Key Principle:**
> Share from factor weighting = factor-weight effect ÷ total active effect.

### Q35 — Manager Most Likely to Have the Largest Tracking Error [QA-35]

**Context (QA7):**
| | Manager A | Manager B | Manager C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Holdings fund/index | 498/500 | 504/500 | 475/500 |
| Dividends reinvested | Next day | Same day | Next day |
| Fee (bp) | 12 | 15 | 10 |
| Reconstitution | Quarterly | Quarterly | Semi-annually |

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 2, the portfolio manager most likely to have the largest tracking error is:

A. Manager A.
B. Manager B.
C. Manager C.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Manager A holds 498 of 500 and reconstitutes quarterly, though it also reinvests dividends next day.
- **B is wrong.** Manager B reinvests dividends same day and reconstitutes quarterly.
- **C is correct.** **Manager C.** Fewest index holdings (475), dividends reinvested next day (cash drag), and least frequent reconstitution; its slightly lower fee won't offset these.

**Key Principle:**
> Tracking error drivers: coverage of index holdings, cash drag, rebalancing/reconstitution frequency, fees.

### Q36 — Describing a Subgroup Sample of an Index [QA-36]

**Context (QA7):**
Stapleton suggests buying a portfolio from mutually exclusive yet comprehensive subgroups of the index to track its return and risk, for a $15m allocation.

**Question:**
The portfolio method suggested by Stapleton to replicate the MSCI EAFE Index is best described as:

A. optimization.
B. stratified sampling.
C. a blended approach.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Optimization maximizes or minimizes a characteristic subject to constraints.
- **B is correct.** **Stratified sampling.** A limited sample from distinct, mutually exclusive and exhaustive strata; used for large indexes or small AUM.
- **C is wrong.** A blended approach uses full replication for liquid issues and sampling/optimization for less liquid ones.

**Key Principle:**
> Strata that are mutually exclusive and exhaustive = stratified sampling.

# QB1 — Furlings / Asgard / Tokra (Manager Styles, Value Traps, Activism)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QB1*

### Q37 — Which Manager Is Most Fundamental [QB-1]

**Context (QB1):**
Furlings: head manager's discretionary sector views (10%+ sector deviations held 12–24 months) plus equal-weighted baskets of 7–10 high-conviction names per sector (~90 total). Asgard: bottom-up fundamental research on <125 stocks but heavy reliance on software, screening and a quantitative risk model. Tokra: factor-score ranking (P/B, 12-month price change, ROA), optimized with risk limits, ~400 holdings.

**Question:**
Which fund manager’s investing approach is most consistent with fundamental management?

A. Furlings
B. Asgard
C. Tokra

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Furlings.** Top-down and bottom-up, but allocation rests on managers' discretion and judgment; strong emphasis on financial statements, valuation, competitive advantage and governance; a small number of names.
- **B is wrong.** Asgard has both fundamental and quantitative traits; it depends heavily on technology for screening/ranking and its allocation depends heavily on a quantitative risk model.
- **C is wrong.** Tokra is quantitative: ranks on quantitative metrics and uses portfolio optimization, with many positions.

**Key Principle:**
> Fundamental = discretionary, few names, deep company analysis. Quantitative = systematic, many names, optimization.

### Q38 — Which Statement About the Managers' Styles Is Incorrect [QB-2]

**Context (QB1):**
Tokra scores securities on P/B, 12-month price increase, and return on assets.

**Question:**
Which of the following statements about the approaches and styles of either Furlings, Asgard, or Tokra is incorrect?

A. Furlings is a top-down sector rotator with a value orientation within sectors.
B. Asgard is a bottom-up manager with a GARP (growth at a reasonable price) style.
C. Tokra is a factor-based manager using value, growth, and profitability metrics.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A is a correct statement: Furlings is a top-down sector rotator, selecting within sectors on relative valuation.
- **B is wrong.** B is a correct statement: Asgard is bottom-up, favoring reasonable valuations and above-average growth (GARP).
- **C is correct.** **C is the incorrect statement.** Tokra is factor based, but its metrics are value (P/B), momentum (12-month price change) and profitability (ROA); there is no growth proxy such as earnings growth.

**Key Principle:**
> Read the stem: 'incorrect' questions — the keyed letter is the false statement.

### Q39 — Manager Most Likely to Fall Into a Value Trap [QB-3]

**Context (QB1):**
Value trap: a stock looks cheap on P/E or P/B only because its growth prospects are deteriorating.

**Question:**
Which manager is most likely to get caught in a value trap?

A. Furlings
B. Asgard
C. Tokra

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Furlings' analysts dissect financial statements and competitive landscape for a few names, so they are more likely to spot worsening prospects.
- **B is wrong.** Asgard targets cash flow growth over the next three years and has tools to evaluate growth, so it is more aware of deteriorating prospects.
- **C is correct.** **Tokra.** A quantitative process using historical information and no forward-looking cash flow or profitability expectations may not detect deteriorating prospects.

**Key Principle:**
> Value trap risk is highest for backward-looking, screen-based value processes.

### Q40 — Activist Tactic Least Likely for a Long-Term Engaged Manager [QB-4]

**Context (QB1):**
Asgard engages with companies as a long-term shareholder using its voice and vote; emphasizes governance and strong balance sheets.

**Question:**
Which activist investing tactic is Asgard least likely to use?

A. Engaging with management by writing letters to management, calling for and explaining suggested changes, and participating in management discussions with analysts or meeting the management team privately
B. Launching legal proceedings against existing management for breach of fiduciary duties
C. Proposing restructuring of the balance sheet to better utilize capital and potentially initiate share buybacks or increase dividends

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Engaging management through letters, calls, meetings is consistent with using its voice.
- **B is correct.** **Launching legal proceedings against management for breach of fiduciary duty.** Aggressive posturing doesn't fit a long-term, good-governance investor, which wouldn't hold companies with weak governance.
- **C is wrong.** Proposing balance-sheet restructuring (buybacks, dividends) fits Asgard's strength in cash flow and capital structure analysis.

**Key Principle:**
> Activist spectrum: constructive engagement → proposals → proxy fights/legal action; governance-focused long-term holders stay at the soft end.

### Q41 — Portfolio Characteristics at Odds With Declared Style [QB-5]

**Context (QB1):**
Key data (Index / Furlings / Asgard / Tokra): P/B 4.8 / 4.30 / 4.35 / 5.4; Net income/assets 2.8% / 4.5% / 4.3% / 3.2%; 12-month momentum 10.5% / 14.0% / 10.0% / 12.0%; EPS growth 11.9% / 11.0% / 13.1% / 10.8%.

**Question:**
Based on the information provided in Exhibits 1 and 2, which manager’s portfolio characteristics is most likely at odds with its declared style?

A. Furlings
B. Asgard
C. Tokra

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Furlings' lower P/B and P/E and higher net income/assets fit a relative-value orientation within sectors.
- **B is wrong.** Asgard's P/B and expected growth (13.1%) fit reasonable valuation and above-average growth; lower yield, slightly higher P/E and lower momentum aren't inconsistent.
- **C is correct.** **Tokra.** It emphasizes P/B, momentum and ROA; momentum and ROA are favorable, but its average P/B (5.4) is higher than the index (4.8), at odds with a value metric. Sector tilts could explain this, but its sector deviation limit is only 3%, which weakens that explanation.

**Key Principle:**
> Compare the portfolio's characteristics to the index for each declared factor; unexplained gaps need follow-up with the manager.

### Q42 — Why Morningstar and Lipper Can Classify a Fund Differently [QB-6]

**Context (QB1):**
Asgard is a blend fund per Morningstar and a value fund per Lipper.

**Question:**
Leonard is looking at the style classification from Asgard as reported by Morningstar and Thomson Reuters Lipper. He is surprised to find that Asgard is classified as a blend fund by Morningstar and a value fund by Lipper. Which of the following statements is correct?

A. Although the Morningstar methodology classifies securities as either value, growth, or core, the Lipper methodology assumes a stock can have the characteristics of many styles. This approach can result in a different classification for the same portfolio.
B. The Lipper methodology can only lead to a value or growth classification. It does not offer a core/blend component.
C. The Morningstar methodology classifies securities as either value, growth, or core by looking at the difference between their respective growth and value scores. It is possible that the Asgard funds hold a balanced exposure to both value and growth and/or core stocks.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Neither Morningstar nor Lipper assumes a stock belongs to several styles; both place each stock in one category.
- **B is wrong.** Lipper does have a core classification (sums Z-scores of six characteristics to give value, core or growth).
- **C is correct.** **Morningstar uses growth minus value scores.** Value and growth scores (0–100, five proxies each) are subtracted; strongly positive = growth, strongly negative = value, near zero = core. A blend fund can have balanced value/growth exposure and/or dominant core exposure.

**Key Principle:**
> Morningstar: growth score − value score → growth/core/value. Lipper: sum of Z-scores → value/core/growth.

# QB2 — Nowacki / Heydon (Quant vs Fundamental, Biases, Pairs Trading)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QB2*

### Q43 — Asset Selection Method Inconsistent With a Top-Down Fund [QB-7]

**Context (QB2):**
Heydon's existing equity fund uses a top-down fundamentals-based strategy.

**Question:**
Which of the following asset allocation methods would not likely be used by Nowacki and Knight to select investments for the existing equity fund?

A. Sector and industry rotation
B. Growth at a reasonable price
C. Country and geographic allocation

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Sector/industry rotation is top-down.
- **B is correct.** **Growth at a reasonable price (GARP).** A bottom-up approach starting with company-level data.
- **C is wrong.** Country/geographic allocation is top-down.

**Key Principle:**
> Top-down: country, sector, macro. Bottom-up: GARP, value, growth stock picking.

### Q44 — Quant Fund vs Fundamental Fund [QB-8]

**Context (QB2):**
Heydon's new Quant Fund (quantitative active) vs its existing fundamental top-down fund.

**Question:**
Relative to Heydon’s existing fund, the new fund will most likely:

A. hold a smaller number of stocks.
B. rebalance at more regular intervals.
C. see risk at the company level rather than the portfolio level.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Quant funds focus on factors across a large group of stocks, so they typically hold more, not fewer, stocks.
- **B is correct.** **Rebalance at more regular intervals.** Quant portfolios rebalance on a schedule (monthly/quarterly); fundamental managers monitor continuously and trade any time.
- **C is wrong.** Quant risk sits at the portfolio (factor) level; fundamental managers see risk at company level.

**Key Principle:**
> Quant: systematic, many stocks, regular rebalancing, portfolio-level risk.

### Q45 — Incorrect Characteristic of Quantitative Management [QB-9]

**Context (QB2):**
Characteristics: (1) focus on factors across a large group of stocks; (2) systematic, non-discretionary decisions; (3) emphasis on forecasting companies' future prospects.

**Question:**
Which characteristic suggested by Knight to describe the quantitative approach to active management is incorrect?

A. Characteristic 1
B. Characteristic 2
C. Characteristic 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Characteristic 1 is correct for quant.
- **B is wrong.** Characteristic 2 is correct for quant.
- **C is correct.** **Characteristic 3 is incorrect.** Quant analysis relies on history and factors; emphasizing forecasts of earnings and cash flows is fundamental analysis.

**Key Principle:**
> Fundamental = forecast future earnings/cash flows. Quant = historical data, factors.

### Q46 — Starting Point of the Quantitative Investment Process [QB-10]

**Question:**
Nowacki’s most appropriate response to Knight’s question about the quantitative investment process is to:

A. back-test the new strategy.
B. define the market opportunity.
C. identify the factors to include and their weights.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Back-testing comes after the opportunity is defined and data are processed.
- **B is correct.** **Define the market opportunity (investment thesis).** Order: define opportunity → acquire/process data → back-test (identify factors and weights) → out-of-sample evaluation.
- **C is wrong.** Identifying factors and their weights is part of back-testing.

**Key Principle:**
> Quant process: thesis → data → back-test → out-of-sample test.

### Q47 — Information Coefficient Timing [QB-11]

**Context (QB2):**
FS(t) = factor score this month; SR(t) = strategy return this month.

**Question:**
In Nowacki’s back-testing of the factor-based strategy for the new fund, the calculated information coefficient should be based on:

A. FS(t) and SR(t).
B. FS(t) and SR(t + 1).
C. SR(t) and FS(t + 1).

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Both are contemporaneous; a score can't be tested for prediction against the same period's return.
- **B is correct.** **FS(t) and SR(t+1).** The IC correlates the current period's factor score with the next period's return.
- **C is wrong.** Reverses the timing (return now vs future score).

**Key Principle:**
> IC = correlation(factor score at t, return at t+1).

### Q48 — Bias From Using Year-End Statements Before Release [QB-12]

**Context (QB2):**
Nowacki computes P/B and P/E using 31 December prices and the same year's financials, which weren't published until weeks later.

**Question:**
Nowacki’s calculated price/book ratios (P/Bs) and price/earnings ratios (P/Es), in his back-testing of the new strategy, are a problem because of:

A. data mining.
B. look-ahead bias.
C. survivorship bias.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Data mining/overfitting comes from searching data for patterns.
- **B is correct.** **Look-ahead bias.** Uses information not available at the decision date.
- **C is wrong.** Survivorship bias comes from excluding companies that left the universe.

**Key Principle:**
> Look-ahead bias: using data not yet known at the time.

### Q49 — Best Pairs Trade Candidate [QB-13]

**Context (QB2):**
| Pair | Current ratio vs long-term average | Historical relationship | Return correlation |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 and 2 | Not significantly different | Mean reverting | High |
| 3 and 4 | Significantly different | Mean reverting | High |
| 5 and 6 | Significantly different | Not mean reverting | Low |

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 1, which stock pair should Knight recommend as the best candidate for statistical arbitrage?

A. Stock 1 and Stock 2
B. Stock 3 and Stock 4
C. Stock 5 and Stock 6

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** No divergence now, so no trade signal.
- **B is correct.** **Stocks 3 and 4.** Price ratio is currently away from its mean, historically mean-reverting, with highly correlated returns.
- **C is wrong.** Not mean reverting and low correlation.

**Key Principle:**
> Stat arb pair: current divergence + mean reversion + high correlation.

### Q50 — Effect of Adding a Factor on Style Classification [QB-14]

**Context (QB2):**
Investment universe unchanged; a new factor is added to the multifactor model.

**Question:**
The most appropriate response to Knight’s question regarding the potential future scenario for the Heydon Quant Fund is:

A. only the returns-based approach.
B. only the holdings-based approach.
C. both the returns-based approach and the holdings-based approach.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Holdings change too.
- **B is wrong.** Returns correlations to factors change too.
- **C is correct.** **Both approaches.** Returns-based: correlations of fund returns with factors change. Holdings-based: portfolio holdings would likely change, changing the classification.

**Key Principle:**
> Changing the factor model affects both returns-based and holdings-based style classifications.

# QB3 — Dewey / Sardar (Fundamental Process, Rebalancing, Style Analysis)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QB3*

### Q51 — Classifying Macro/Demographic Sector Positioning [QB-15]

**Context (QB3):**
At his previous firm Dewey took active sector exposures based on the macro environment and demographic trends.

**Question:**
In managing the fund at his previous employer, Dewey’s investment process can be best described as:

A. an activist strategy.
B. a top-down strategy.
C. a bottom-up strategy.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Activist strategies take stakes and advocate change.
- **B is correct.** **Top-down.** Starts from macro variables and sectors.
- **C is wrong.** Bottom-up focuses on company-specific variables.

**Key Principle:**
> Top-down: macro, demographics, policy, sector.

### Q52 — Next Step in a Fundamental Active Process [QB-16]

**Context (QB3):**
Dewey and Sardar have defined the universe/opportunity, pre-screened, and done industry and competitive analysis.

**Question:**
Sardar’s recommendation for the next step should be to:

A. review results from back-testing the strategy.
B. make recommendations for rebalancing the portfolio.
C. forecast companies’ performances and convert those forecasts into valuations.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Back-testing is a quant step.
- **B is wrong.** Rebalancing is the last step.
- **C is correct.** **Forecast company performance and convert to valuations.** Steps: (1) universe/market opportunity; (2) prescreen; (3) industry/competitive and financial analysis; (4) forecast performance; (5) convert to valuations; (6) construct portfolio; (7) rebalance.

**Key Principle:**
> Fundamental process: universe → screen → industry/financial analysis → forecast → valuation → portfolio → rebalance.

### Q53 — Rebalancing Trigger for a Fundamental Fund [QB-17]

**Context (QB3):**
Dewey's Purity Fund uses a fundamental active process.

**Question:**
Based upon Dewey’s chosen investment process for the management of the Purity Fund, rebalancing of the fund will most likely occur:

A. at regular intervals.
B. in response to changes in company-specific information.
C. in response to updated output from optimization models.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Regular-interval rebalancing is quant.
- **B is correct.** **In response to changes in company-specific information.** Fundamental managers monitor holdings continuously and trade at any time.
- **C is wrong.** Optimizer output drives quant rebalancing.

**Key Principle:**
> Fundamental: event-driven rebalancing. Quant: regular intervals/optimizer.

### Q54 — Approach Behind Buying High-P/B AZ Industrial [QB-18]

**Context (QB3):**
Purity Fund emphasizes financial strength and demonstrated profitability; Sardar believes AZ trades below intrinsic value despite a P/B above its industry average.

**Question:**
Which investment approach is the most likely basis for Sardar’s buy recommendation for AZ Industrial?

A. Relative value
B. High-quality value
C. Deep-value investing

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Relative value compares multiples to the industry; AZ's P/B is above average, contrary to that.
- **B is correct.** **High-quality value.** Intrinsic value supported by valuation metrics, with emphasis on financial strength and profitability.
- **C is wrong.** Deep value targets extremely low valuations vs assets, often distressed firms.

**Key Principle:**
> High-quality value: intrinsic value + financial strength + profitability.

### Q55 — Bottom-Up Style Analysis From Stock Attributes [QB-19]

**Context (QB3):**
Sardar aggregates the attributes of individual stocks in the Purity Fund.

**Question:**
The analysis performed by Sardar on the Purity Fund can be best described as being based on:

A. a holdings-based approach.
B. manager self-identification.
C. a returns-based style analysis.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Holdings-based.** Aggregates attributes of individual holdings, weighted by position.
- **B is wrong.** Self-identification relies on the manager's description.
- **C is wrong.** Returns-based regresses fund returns on style indexes.

**Key Principle:**
> Holdings-based: aggregate stock attributes. Returns-based: regress returns on style indexes.

# QB4 — Allfunz / Leeter (Quant Management, Biases, Pairs Trading, Style Analysis)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QB4*

### Q56 — Accurate Statement on Quant Management [QB-20]

**Context (QB4):**
Leeter: (1) manager experience and discretion in identifying new trends are important in any quant strategy; (2) loss aversion bias is more prominent in quant than fundamental; (3) quant methods rely on information coefficients between firm returns and factors.

**Question:**
Which of Leeter’s statements concerning the quantitative approach to active management is most accurate?

A. Statement 1
B. Statement 2
C. Statement 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Discretion has a minimal role in quant approaches.
- **B is wrong.** Loss aversion is more typical of fundamental approaches.
- **C is correct.** **Statement 3.** Quant methods use past data to identify systematic factors to over/underweight, using ICs.

**Key Principle:**
> Quant: factor-based, IC, minimal discretion.

### Q57 — Bias Reduced by Using Initially Reported (Unrevised) Data [QB-21]

**Context (QB4):**
XTZZ keeps the originally reported government data as a lagged factor until revisions become available.

**Question:**
XTZZ’s approach to analyzing government-reported data most likely reduces:

A. look-ahead bias.
B. survivorship bias.
C. model overfitting.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Look-ahead bias.** It avoids using revised data that wasn't known when first reported.
- **B is wrong.** Survivorship bias is about ignoring firms that no longer exist.
- **C is wrong.** Overfitting is fitting a model to patterns specific to past data.

**Key Principle:**
> Use data as it was known at the time (point-in-time) to avoid look-ahead bias.

### Q58 — Main Risk Control in Pairs Trading [QB-22]

**Context (QB4):**
Kopernicus trades highly correlated peer pairs, expecting mean reversion over days or weeks.

**Question:**
Which risk management method is the Kopernicus Fund most likely to use to offset the primary risk of its strategy?

A. Proper identification of the pairs
B. Frequent use of stop-loss order rules
C. Extensive analysis of the limit order book

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Pair selection matters but doesn't address a change in fundamentals that prolongs or ends convergence.
- **B is correct.** **Frequent stop-loss rules.** The key risk is that the divergence isn't temporary (structural change); stop-losses exit when a loss limit is hit.
- **C is wrong.** Limit-order-book analysis suits millisecond high-frequency trading, not multi-day trades.

**Key Principle:**
> Pairs trading risk: divergence is permanent → stop-loss.

### Q59 — Value vs Growth Elements in P/E-Based Strategies [QB-23]

**Context (QB4):**
Altitude: forward P/E using forecast EPS to select stocks. Pioneer: compares a company's P/E with its industry average to find under-priced firms. Leeter: both incorporate a growth strategy.

**Question:**
Using Exhibit 1, Leeter’s statement about Altitude Funds and Pioneer Funds is most likely:

A. correct.
B. incorrect in regard to Altitude Funds.
C. incorrect in regard to Pioneer Funds.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Statement is right for Altitude but not Pioneer.
- **B is wrong.** Altitude does incorporate growth (GARP).
- **C is correct.** **Incorrect regarding Pioneer.** Pioneer is relative value, with no growth element. Altitude (forward P/E on forecast EPS) is GARP (value plus growth).

**Key Principle:**
> Relative value = multiple vs industry. GARP = value multiple with growth forecast.

### Q60 — Overlay for a Fund Hurt by Volatile Exchange Rates [QB-24]

**Context (QB4):**
Regulas: global equities across 5–10 countries; uses monthly non-traditional data; customized benchmark; factors more volatile than traditional ones; performance suffers when exchange rates are volatile.

**Question:**
Using Exhibit 1 and the equity selection process of Regulas Funds, the strategy will most likely benefit from:

A. a portfolio overlay.
B. a new benchmark.
C. using annual rebalancing.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Portfolio overlay.** Derivatives overlay can remove exchange rate risk.
- **B is wrong.** The custom benchmark is already appropriate.
- **C is wrong.** Annual rebalancing is too infrequent given volatile factors.

**Key Principle:**
> Overlay (derivatives) isolates/removes unwanted exposure such as currency.

### Q61 — Returns-Based vs Holdings-Based Analysis Description [QB-25]

**Context (QB4):**
Leeter: returns-based analysis regresses past fund returns on style indexes; it is harder to perform than holdings-based but gives deeper analysis.

**Question:**
Leeter’s reply to Clickman concerning determining the style of funds is most accurate in regard to the:

A. description of a returns-based analysis.
B. applicability of returns-based analysis versus holding-based analysis.
C. comparison of depth of analysis between returns-based analysis versus holdings-based analysis.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Description of returns-based analysis.** Regression of fund returns on style index returns is correct.
- **B is wrong.** Wrong on applicability: returns-based analysis is easier because data are more readily available.
- **C is wrong.** Wrong on depth: holdings-based analysis uses actual holdings, so it's deeper and more accurate.

**Key Principle:**
> Returns-based: easier, less detailed. Holdings-based: more data, deeper.

# QB5 — Reed / TBC (Back-Testing)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QB5*

### Q62 — Pitfalls of Back-Testing a Quant Strategy [QB-26] (Essay)

**Context (QB5):**
Reed back-tested the AE strategy on 10 years of third-party unstructured data (consumer spending factor).

**Question:**
Describe two pitfalls associated with quantitative strategies that Reed should have considered.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Any two of:
- **Survivorship bias:** using only companies still in business ignores delisted, bankrupt or acquired stocks, overstating results.
- **Look-ahead bias:** using information not available when the decision would have been made.
- **Data mining / overfitting:** automated pattern search that fits a model to past data and may not hold in the future.
- **Transaction costs:** trading isn't free and can erode returns materially.
- **Constraints on turnover:** may stop the manager from capturing the model's expected excess return in live trading.
- **Availability of positions:** if a position can't readily be bought or sold short, expected excess return can't be captured.

**Key Principle:**
> Back-test pitfalls: survivorship, look-ahead, data mining/overfitting, transaction costs, turnover constraints, availability of positions.

# QB6 — Yarsley (Relative Value, Income, Deep Value, GARP)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QB6*

### Q63 — Relative Value Candidate [QB-27] (Essay)

**Context (QB6):**
| Co. | Price | 1-yr fwd EPS | 3-yr EPS growth | Div. yield | Sector avg P/E | Fwd P/E | PEG |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | $48 | $4 | 3% | 9% | 12 | 12 | 4.0 |
| 2 | $28 | $2 | 5% | 0% | 35 | 14 | 2.8 |
| 3 | $30 | $10 | −2% | 2% | 15 | 3 | negative |
| 4 | $36 | $4 | 10% | 2% | 10 | 9 | 0.9 |

(Fwd P/E = price ÷ forward EPS; PEG = forward P/E ÷ growth, computed by me from the exhibit.)

**Question:**
Identify the company most appropriate for a relative value strategy. Justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Company 2.** Forward P/E of 14 is far below its sector average of 35. Companies 1 and 4 trade near their sector P/Es (12 vs 12; 9 vs 10), and Company 3 has negative expected growth.

**Key Principle:**
> Relative value: multiple well below the sector/industry average.

### Q64 — Income Strategy Candidate [QB-28] (Essay)

**Context (QB6):**
Same four-company table.

**Question:**
Identify the company most appropriate for an income strategy. Justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Company 1.** Highest dividend yield (9%), and its forward P/E (12) matches its sector (12), so it isn't over- or undervalued relative to peers.

**Key Principle:**
> Income strategy: high, sustainable dividend yield at a fair valuation.

### Q65 — Deep Value Candidate [QB-29] (Essay)

**Context (QB6):**
Same four-company table.

**Question:**
Identify the company most appropriate for a deep value strategy. Justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Company 3.** Forward P/E of only 3, low in absolute terms and far below its sector average of 15; its situation (negative growth forecast) needs close research.

**Key Principle:**
> Deep value: extremely low absolute multiples; investigate why.

### Q66 — GARP Candidate [QB-30] (Essay)

**Context (QB6):**
Same four-company table.

**Question:**
Identify the company most appropriate for a GARP strategy. Justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Company 4.** Lowest PEG (0.9), excluding Company 3 whose growth forecast is negative; a favorable valuation relative to growth.

**Key Principle:**
> GARP: low PEG with positive growth.

# QB7 — Abernathy / Granville / Carlucci (Fundamental Approaches, Event-Driven, GARP)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QB7*

### Q67 — Accurate Statement on the Fundamental Approach [QB-31]

**Context (QB7):**
Statements: (1) subjective, discretionary process; (2) selections based on exposure to variables that predict return; (3) portfolio built with an optimizer controlling risk at portfolio level.

**Question:**
Which of the analysts’ statements regarding the fundamental approach is the most accurate?

A. Statement 1
B. Statement 2
C. Statement 3

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Statement 1.** Fundamental investing is subjective and discretionary.
- **B is wrong.** Exposure to predictive variables is the quantitative approach.
- **C is wrong.** Portfolio optimizers controlling portfolio-level risk are quantitative.

**Key Principle:**
> Fundamental = discretion. Quant = factor exposures + optimizer.

### Q68 — Fund That Uses Only Bottom-Up [QB-32]

**Context (QB7):**
Fund A: stocks picked by company analysis; sector overlays control risk. Fund B: long–short, event-driven, software scans news then analysts analyze targets. Fund C: country/sector parameters from a strategist, then judgment-based stock picks.

**Question:**
Based on the descriptions in Exhibit 1, which fund is least likely to use a combination of a top-down and a bottom-up strategy?

A. Fund A
B. Fund B
C. Fund C

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Fund A: stock selection is bottom-up, sector overlays for risk are top-down.
- **B is correct.** **Fund B.** Bottom-up only: news screening only identifies targets for analyst work.
- **C is wrong.** Fund C: country/sector allocation is top-down, then stock selection is bottom-up.

**Key Principle:**
> Top-down = macro/country/sector. Bottom-up = security-level analysis.

### Q69 — Strategy of a Fund Buying Out-of-Favor Stocks [QB-33]

**Context (QB7):**
Fund A invests in companies with poor earnings that are out of favor and influenced by investor behavioral trends; picks by company analysis; sector overlays.

**Question:**
As described in Exhibit 1, Fund A most likely follows which investment strategy?

A. Contrarian
B. Deep value
C. Restructuring and distressed

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Contrarian.** Buys against prevailing sentiment (low/negative earnings or low dividends) expecting a rebound, using behavioral-finance logic.
- **B is wrong.** Deep value targets extremely low valuations relative to assets, often distressed.
- **C is wrong.** Restructuring/distressed aims for control or influence over a distressed company at a deep discount.

**Key Principle:**
> Contrarian: trade against sentiment/crowd behavior.

### Q70 — Event-Driven Fund: Which Investment Point Is Accurate [QB-34]

**Context (QB7):**
Fund B (event-driven long–short). Points: (1) shares of a potential acquirer, even in a consolidating industry; (2) a control position in a distressed company at a deep discount; (3) long-term investments in reorganizing companies.

**Question:**
In theanalysts’ discussion about Fund B’s potential investments, the point that is most accurate relates to:

A. reorganization opportunities.
B. positions in distressed companies.
C. acquisitions in a consolidating industry.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Reorganizations require long horizons, inconsistent with Fund B's event strategy.
- **B is wrong.** A control position in a distressed company is also long term, inconsistent with Fund B.
- **C is correct.** **Acquisitions in a consolidating industry.** An event-driven fund can go long or short acquirer and target to exploit inefficiencies, even in a consolidating industry.

**Key Principle:**
> Event-driven: short/medium-term corporate-event inefficiencies; distressed control/reorganizations are long-horizon.

### Q71 — Step Not Addressing Illusion of Control or Confirmation Bias [QB-35]

**Context (QB7):**
Concentrated positions and underperformance; Carlucci suspects illusion of control and confirmation bias.

**Question:**
Which of the following steps is Abernathy least likely to recommend to address the behavioral biases that Carlucci believes exist in the larger funds?

A. Establish stop-loss rules
B. Institute stricter rules for diversification
C. Actively seek contradictory information

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Stop-loss rules.** They address loss aversion, not illusion of control or confirmation bias.
- **B is wrong.** Diversification rules address illusion of control (overconfidence, excessive trading, concentrated bets).
- **C is wrong.** Seeking contradictory information counters confirmation bias.

**Key Principle:**
> Illusion of control → diversification/trading rules. Confirmation bias → seek contradictory views. Loss aversion → stop-losses.

### Q72 — GARP Stock Selection Using PEG and Sector Growth [QB-36]

**Context (QB7):**
| | Price | 12-mo fwd EPS | 3-yr EPS growth | Sector avg P/E | Sector growth |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AQU | $36 | $3.70 | 9.50% | 11.4 | 8.00% |
| BRW | $28 | $2.75 | 12.00% | 10.1 | 14.00% |
| CSY | $19 | $1.95 | 11.25% | 10.8 | 10.00% |
Fund D buys above-average growth at reasonable multiples (GARP).

**Question:**
Using Exhibits 1 and 2, the stock that would be the best addition to Fund D is:

A. AQU.
B. BRW.
C. CSY.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** AQU has the highest PEG (1.02: P/E 9.73 ÷ 9.5).
- **B is wrong.** BRW has the lowest PEG (0.85) but its 12% growth is below the sector's 14%, so it isn't above-average growth.
- **C is correct.** **CSY.** Forward P/E 9.74, PEG 0.87, and growth (11.25%) above the sector's 10%.

**Key Principle:**
> GARP: low PEG and growth above the sector average.

# QB8 — Popkirk / PV (Manager Classification, Lunnar Funds, Momentum)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QB8*

### Q73 — Top-Down Fundamental Fund Among Candidates [QB-37]

**Context (QB8):**
Lunnar A: value-weighted mix of 10 country indexes, low fee, annual rebalance. Lunnar B: strategically weighted mix of 10 country indexes, weights adjusted for long-term trends and short-term opportunities. Lunnar C: strategically weighted 5–10-country equities, fundamental stock weights for near-term growth.

**Question:**
Based on the information in Exhibit 1, which of the funds from Lunnar Associates most likely follows a top-down fundamental approach?

A. Lunnar A
B. Lunnar B
C. Lunnar C

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Lunnar A is passive (value weights, no forward views).
- **B is correct.** **Lunnar B.** Discretionary weights on country indexes based on economic views: top-down fundamental.
- **C is wrong.** Lunnar C is active but bottom-up (individual securities' growth prospects).

**Key Principle:**
> Weights on indexes by discretionary macro views = top-down; securities by prospects = bottom-up.

### Q74 — Fund Style Most Consistent With Its Index [QB-38]

**Context (QB8):**
Value/Growth mix (holdings-based / returns-based / index): Lunnar A 68/32, 68/32, 75/25; Lunnar B 67/33, 68/32, 65/35; Lunnar C 39/61, 34/66, 35/65.

**Question:**
Which of the funds in Exhibit 2 has a style most consistent with that of its respective index?

A. Lunnar A
B. Lunnar B
C. Lunnar C

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Lunnar A is 7 points off its index on holdings.
- **B is correct.** **Lunnar B.** Holdings-based mix (67% value) differs from the index (65%) by 2 points, vs 7 for A and 4 for C. Holdings-based analysis is generally more accurate because it uses actual holdings.
- **C is wrong.** Lunnar C is 4 points off its index on holdings.

**Key Principle:**
> Holdings-based style analysis is more accurate; returns-based is used when holdings aren't available.

### Q75 — Bias Typical of a Fundamental Fund [QB-39]

**Context (QB8):**
Popkirk will look for acknowledgment of survivorship, confirmation or look-ahead bias in Lunnar C's fundamental approach.

**Question:**
Evidence of which behavioral bias is most likely to be found when analyzing the Lunnar C fund?

A. Look-ahead
B. Survivorship
C. Confirmation

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Look-ahead bias is a back-testing risk in quant strategies.
- **B is wrong.** Survivorship bias is a back-testing risk in quant strategies.
- **C is correct.** **Confirmation bias.** The 'stock love' tendency to seek confirming information; a risk for fundamental analysts.

**Key Principle:**
> Fundamental: confirmation, loss aversion, illusion of control. Quant: look-ahead, survivorship, overfitting.

### Q76 — Rotating Into Deep Value Over the Economic Cycle [QB-40]

**Context (QB8):**
Annual report: 'During the last recession, we rotated into deep-value companies likely to deliver superior returns as a risk-on environment returned.' Analyst 1: top-down. Analyst 2: bottom-up because deep value. Analyst 3: GARP, which is top-down.

**Question:**
Which of the analysts’ comments regarding the Lunnar fund’s annual report is the most accurate?

A. Analyst 1
B. Analyst 2
C. Analyst 3

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Analyst 1.** Style rotation driven by economic cycle analysis is top-down.
- **B is wrong.** Deep value use here results from economics-driven rotation, so it is top-down.
- **C is wrong.** GARP is a bottom-up strategy.

**Key Principle:**
> Style rotation based on economic cycle = top-down; GARP = bottom-up.

### Q77 — Style Analysis Method for a Non-Standard Hedge Fund [QB-41]

**Context (QB8):**
Lunnar Hedge-X uses long–short equity with substantial futures and options overlays.

**Question:**
Which method of style analysis is best used to properly classify the Lunnar Hedge-X fund?

A. Self-identification
B. Holdings-based analysis alone
C. Holdings-based and returns-based analysis combined

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Self-identification.** For a non-standard hedge fund with extensive shorts and derivatives, the prospectus description is the key information; neither holdings-based nor returns-based analysis helps classify it.
- **B is wrong.** Holdings-based analysis alone doesn't capture the strategy.
- **C is wrong.** Neither approach, alone or combined, helps classify this type of fund.

**Key Principle:**
> Complex hedge funds: classify by self-identification (prospectus).

### Q78 — Drawback of a Price-Momentum Strategy [QB-42]

**Context (QB8):**
Thompson claims consistent success from price momentum as a rewarded factor exposure.

**Question:**
Which of the following is best described as a major drawback of using Thompson’s approach? It is subject to:

A. a value trap.
B. a growth trap.
C. extreme tail risk.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A value trap refers to cheap-looking value stocks, not momentum.
- **B is wrong.** A growth trap refers to stocks that seem to have continuing earnings growth, not price momentum.
- **C is correct.** **Extreme tail risk.** Momentum is subject to extreme tail risk (sharp reversals).

**Key Principle:**
> Momentum factor: crash/tail risk.

# Standalone — Quantitative, Fundamental and Style Analysis

### Q79 — Factor-Mimicking Portfolio [QB-43]

**Question:**
A factor-mimicking portfolio can be best described as a:

A. long-only portfolio with exposure to a given factor built with controlled tracking error.
B. portfolio that enters into long or short positions taking into account short availability constraints and transaction costs.
C. theoretical long-short portfolio that is dollar neutral with a unit exposure to a chosen factor and no exposure to other factors.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** That describes a factor-tilting (long-only) portfolio with controlled tracking error.
- **B is wrong.** FMPs invest in almost every stock with no regard for short availability or transaction costs; practical 'pure factor' portfolios add such constraints.
- **C is correct.** **Theoretical long–short portfolio, dollar neutral, with unit exposure to one factor and none to others.**

**Key Principle:**
> FMP = theoretical long/short, dollar neutral, unit exposure to one factor.

### Q80 — Style Analysis Without Full Portfolio Details [QB-44]

**Context:**
Active long-only portfolio with a ten-year record using overlay strategies; complete holdings unavailable.

**Question:**
An actively managed long-only portfolio has a ten-year track record. The portfolio manager frequently uses overlay strategies to achieve the portfolio's desired characteristics. An investment advisor decides to perform a style analysis to determine if the portfolio is appropriate for her clients. If complete details of the portfolio are unavailable, the investment advisor is most likely to:

A. use returns-based style analysis.
B. use holdings-based style analysis.
C. conclude that style analysis is not possible.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Returns-based style analysis.** Compare the strategy's returns to style indexes; possible with a ten-year record.
- **B is wrong.** Holdings-based needs full holdings.
- **C is wrong.** Style analysis is still possible via returns.

**Key Principle:**
> No holdings disclosure → returns-based style analysis.

### Q81 — Statement About the Quantitative Approach [QB-45]

**Question:**
A quantitative approach to active management:

A. makes use of variables that relate to company fundamentals.
B. controls for risk at the stock level rather than at the portfolio level.
C. does not involve analyst judgment in designing a model to estimate expected stock returns.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Uses variables related to company fundamentals** (valuation, profitability, financial strength, sentiment, industry, price attributes).
- **B is wrong.** Quant controls risk at the portfolio level (optimizer), not the stock level.
- **C is wrong.** Analyst judgment is used at the design stage (choosing and combining factors); quant replaces ongoing discretion with systematic execution.

**Key Principle:**
> Quant: fundamentals as factors, judgment in design, systematic execution, portfolio-level risk.

### Q82 — Statement About the Fundamental Approach [QB-46]

**Question:**
The fundamental approach to active management:

A. uses optimizers for portfolio construction.
B. seeks to obtain a detailed understanding of a company’s current and past profitability.
C. involves systematic processes to search for security characteristics that have predictive power to identify securities that will earn superior investment returns.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Optimizers are used in quantitative portfolio construction; fundamental uses judgment and conviction within risk parameters.
- **B is correct.** **Seeks a detailed understanding of the company's current and past profitability** (financial position, cash flows, business model, management, outlook) to value the shares.
- **C is wrong.** Systematic searches for predictive characteristics describe the quantitative approach.

**Key Principle:**
> Fundamental: discretion + deep company analysis.

### Q83 — Factor-Based Hedged Portfolio Construction [QB-47]

**Context:**
Ranking by factor, divided into deciles; the analyst goes long the top decile.

**Question:**
An analyst ranks an investable stock universe by a factor and divides the universe into deciles. The analyst then begins constructing a hedged portfolio by going long the top decile companies. This approach to portfolio construction differs from the factor-based hedged portfolio approach in that the hedged portfolio is long the:

A. best decile companies and short the worst decile companies.
B. bottom decile companies and short the top decile companies.
C. top 1% companies and short the bottom 1% companies in each decile.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Long the best decile and short the worst decile.**
- **B is wrong.** Reversed: the hedged portfolio goes long the best, short the worst.
- **C is wrong.** Uses quantiles (best vs worst), not 1% slices within each decile.

**Key Principle:**
> Long/short hedged factor portfolio: long best quantile, short worst quantile.

### Q84 — Basis of Price Momentum [QB-48]

**Question:**
Price momentum as a form of factor-based investing is based on the observation that:

A. stocks with high earnings yield tend to provide higher returns.
B. stocks that outperform over the previous 12 months tend to outperform over the following months.
C. investors are compensated for the greater likelihood that companies with high returns potential will experience financial distress.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** High earnings yield is the value factor rationale.
- **B is correct.** **Stocks that outperformed over the previous 12 months tend to continue outperforming** over the following 2–12 months (Jegadeesh and Titman).
