- **C is wrong.** The distress-compensation explanation (Fama and French) is for the value premium.

**Key Principle:**
> Momentum = winners keep winning over 2–12 months; value = cheap stocks outperform.

### Q85 — Best Statistical Arbitrage Pair [QB-49]

**Context:**
Pair 1: car maker and supplier, strong 3-year price correlation, prices diverged in opposite directions recently. Pair 2: two similar banks; one changed strategy, one reported strong growth; ratio deviated significantly from its moving average. Pair 3: two large-cap cement stocks, similar P/E, price ratio mean-reverting for ten years, now >1 standard deviation above its moving average.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager is currently evaluating three pairs of stocks for her statistical arbitrage fund. Which pair is the  portfolio manager most likely to choose for her fund?

| Pair 1 | Consists of car manufacturer and its supplier. Despite different business models, statistical analysis shows strong correlation has persisted between their share prices for the last three years. The stock prices have moved significantly in opposite direction in recent days. |
|---|---|
| Pair 2 | Consists of two mature banks of similar size. One bank recently shifted its focus to retail banking and saw significant decline in its stock price. The other bank reported significant increase in business and saw a sharp increase in its stock price. This has led to the price ratio of the two banks to deviate significantly from their long-run moving average. |
| Pair 3 | Consists of two large-cap stocks in the cement sector with similar price earnings multiple. The ratio of their share prices shows mean reversion over the last ten years. The ratio is currently more than one standard deviation above its moving average. |

A. Pair 1
B. Pair 2
C. Pair 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Past correlation may be spurious and mean reversion isn't described.
- **B is wrong.** Business changes may explain the deviation, so it may not revert.
- **C is correct.** **Pair 3.** Correlated, mean-reverting ratio with no business change, currently stretched.

**Key Principle:**
> Stat arb: similar businesses, mean-reverting ratio, current divergence, no fundamental change.

### Q86 — Relative Value Position After a Rerating [QB-50]

**Context:**
Target price $45 a year ago; peer group rerated up 10%; stock now $52.

**Question:**
A stock was assigned a target price of $45 a year ago ​​​​​​based on a relative value framework. The market subsequently rerated the peer group including the stock by 10% based on a significant positive shift in the sector's future prospects. The stock currently trades at $52. The best course of action for a portfolio manager holding the stock is to:

A. reduce the size of the position.
B. keep the position unchanged.
C. increase the size of the position.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Reduce the position.** New target = 45 × 1.10 = $49.50; at $52 the stock is above target (now overvalued), limiting upside.
- **B is wrong.** Stock is above its adjusted target.
- **C is wrong.** Adding to an overvalued position isn't supported.

**Key Principle:**
> Price above target → overvalued → reduce.

### Q87 — Portfolio Construction Step in a Quant Process [QB-51]

**Context:**
Return model chosen; now constructing the portfolio.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager is tasked with the responsibility of creating and managing a fund based on a quantitative strategy. After choosing an appropriate model to estimate stock returns, the manager turns to the portfolio construction process. The portfolio manager is most likely to:

A. select and weight each factor applying a systematic process.
B. create a standard monthly back-test to assess the effectiveness of the chosen strategy over time.
C. rely on commercial risk model vendors to estimate the variance–covariance matrix of stock returns.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Selecting and weighting factors is part of building the multifactor model.
- **B is wrong.** Back-testing is the strategy evaluation step.
- **C is correct.** **Rely on commercial risk model vendors to estimate the variance–covariance matrix.** Directly estimating it from sample returns is infeasible and error-prone.

**Key Principle:**
> Portfolio construction: risk models (vendor covariance estimates), optimizer.

### Q88 — When Survivorship Bias Arises [QB-52]

**Question:**
Survivorship bias becomes an issue relating to quantitative decision making when:

A. back-tests use only those companies that are currently in business.
B. the chosen model estimates the variance–covariance matrix using only the sample return data.
C. the selected factors may be effective only individually but not add material value to a factor model because they are correlated with other factors.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Back-tests use only companies currently in business**, ignoring those that went bankrupt, delisted or were acquired.
- **B is wrong.** That is a portfolio construction estimation issue.
- **C is wrong.** That is a multifactor model design issue (correlated factors).

**Key Principle:**
> Survivorship bias: back-test universe excludes failed firms.

### Q89 — Relative Value Pick From Three Stocks [QB-53]

**Context:**
| | Div. yield | Price | Fwd EPS | Sector P/E | EPS growth |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stock 1 | 2% | $72 | $6 | 12 | 2% |
| Stock 2 | 0% | $80 | $4 | 25 | 5% |
| Stock 3 | 7% | $54 | $3 | 18 | 4% |

**Question:**
An analyst gathers the following information about three stocks:

|  | Dividend Yield (%) | Stock Price ($) | Forward EPS ($) | Sector Average P/E | Forecasted EPS Growth (%) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stock 1 | 2 | 72 | 6 | 12 | 2 |
| Stock 2 | 0 | 80 | 4 | 25 | 5 |
| Stock 3 | 7 | 54 | 3 | 18 | 4 |

Which stock should the analyst recommend based on the relative value approach?

A. Stock 1
B. Stock 2
C. Stock 3

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Stock 1's P/E equals its sector's, and its PEG (6) is the highest.
- **B is correct.** **Stock 2.** P/E = 80/4 = 20, below its sector's 25. Stocks 1 and 3 trade at their sector P/Es (12 and 18).
- **C is wrong.** Stock 3 fits income investing (7% yield), not relative value.

**Key Principle:**
> Relative value: P/E below the sector average.

### Q90 — GARP Pick From Three Stocks [QB-54]

**Context:**
| | Div. yield | Price | Fwd EPS | Sector P/E | EPS growth |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stock 1 | 6% | $51 | $3 | 17 | 5% |
| Stock 2 | 0% | $72 | $2 | 20 | 18% |
| Stock 3 | 2% | $84 | $7 | 12 | 15% |

**Question:**
An analyst gathers the following information on three stocks:

|  | Dividend Yield (%) | Stock Price ($) | Forward EPS ($) | Sector Average P/E | Forecasted EPS Growth (%) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stock 1 | 6 | 51 | 3 | 17 | 5 |
| Stock 2 | 0 | 72 | 2 | 20 | 18 |
| Stock 3 | 2 | 84 | 7 | 12 | 15 |

Which stock should the analyst recommend based on the growth at a reasonable price approach?

A. Stock 1
B. Stock 2
C. Stock 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Stock 1 fits income investing (6% yield).
- **B is wrong.** Stock 2 is a traditional growth pick: P/E 36 vs sector 20, high growth (18%) but expensive.
- **C is correct.** **Stock 3.** PEG = (84/7)/15 = 0.8, the lowest (Stock 1: 3.4; Stock 2: 2.0).

**Key Principle:**
> GARP: lowest PEG with strong growth at a reasonable multiple.

### Q91 — Derivatives Overlay on a Relative Value Portfolio [QB-55]

**Context:**
A relative value investor uses derivative positions to add active returns uncorrelated with the underlying strategy.

**Question:**
An investor with a relative value approach decides to use an array of derivative positions to add active returns that are not correlated with those generated by their underlying portfolio strategy. The add-on strategy created by these derivative positions is an example of:

A. top-down investing.
B. contrarian investing.
C. bottom-up investing.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Top-down investing.** A portfolio overlay (derivatives managed separately) is a top-down strategy that helps bottom-up investors control risk exposures or add uncorrelated active returns.
- **B is wrong.** Contrarian investing is a bottom-up strategy; the overlay isn't that.
- **C is wrong.** The underlying relative value strategy is bottom-up, but the derivative add-on is the top-down overlay.

**Key Principle:**
> Portfolio overlay = top-down add-on to a bottom-up portfolio.

### Q92 — Activist Target (Growth and Governance) [QB-56]

**Context:**
| | EPS growth vs market | Governance |
|---|---|---|
| Company 1 | Faster | Weaker |
| Company 2 | Slower | Weaker |
| Company 3 | Slower | Stronger |

**Question:**
An analyst gathers the following information about the characteristics of three companies relative to the market average:

|  | EPS Growth | Corporate Governance |
|---|---|---|
| Company 1 | Faster | Weaker |
| Company 2 | Slower | Weaker |
| Company 3 | Slower | Stronger |

Which company is a suitable target for an activist investor?

A. Company 1
B. Company 2
C. Company 3

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Faster growth makes it a less likely target.
- **B is correct.** **Company 2.** Activists target slower growth and weaker governance.
- **C is wrong.** Stronger governance makes it a less likely target.

**Key Principle:**
> Activist targets: slow growth, negative momentum, weak governance, single-class shares.

### Q93 — Activist Target Characteristic [QB-57]

**Question:**
Which of the following companies is most likely a suitable target for an activist investor? A company with:

A. negative price momentum.
B. multi-class share structure.
C. stronger-than-average corporate governance.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Negative price momentum.** Targets feature slower growth, negative momentum and weaker governance.
- **B is wrong.** Multi-class share structures hinder activism; activists prefer single-class.
- **C is wrong.** Stronger governance makes a firm a less likely target.

**Key Principle:**
> Activist targets: negative momentum, weak governance, slow growth, single-class shares.

# QC1 — Ap (Fund Construction, Trading Costs, Factor Variance, Active Share)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QC1*

### Q94 — Main Portfolio Construction Building Block for a Factor-Timing Fund [QC-1]

**Context (QC1):**
Fund 1 times exposures to rewarded and unrewarded factors and to other asset classes (country, asset class, sector), preferring large trades.

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 1, the main building block of portfolio construction on which Fund 1 focuses is most likely:

A. alpha skills.
B. position sizing.
C. rewarded factor weightings.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Alpha skills.** The three building blocks are alpha skills, position sizing and rewarded factor weightings. Timing factors and asset classes is an alpha skill.
- **B is wrong.** Position sizing is about how large each position is.
- **C is wrong.** Rewarded factor weightings are strategic factor tilts, not timing.

**Key Principle:**
> Building blocks: alpha skills, position sizing, rewarded factor weightings.

### Q95 — Bottom-Up Fund Among Three [QC-2]

**Context (QC1):**
Fund 1: factor/asset-class timing. Fund 2: diversified by securities, concentrated in factors (growth over value). Fund 3: evaluates risk/return of individual securities and builds from stock-specific forecasts.

**Question:**
Which fund in Exhibit 1 most likely follows a bottom-up approach?

A. Fund 1
B. Fund 2
C. Fund 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Fund 1 times factors and asset classes (top-down).
- **B is wrong.** Fund 2 expresses a growth-over-value factor view.
- **C is correct.** **Fund 3.** Bottom-up builds from individual security forecasts.

**Key Principle:**
> Bottom-up = security-specific forecasts. Top-down = factor/asset-class/sector views.

### Q96 — Fund With the Highest Implicit Trading Costs [QC-3]

**Context (QC1):**
Fund 1: small caps, large AUM, few securities, large trades. Fund 2: large caps, large AUM, many securities, builds slowly via unlit venues. Fund 3: small caps, small AUM, many securities, large trades.

**Question:**
Which fund in Exhibit 1 most likely has the greatest implicit costs to implement its strategy?

A. Fund 1
B. Fund 2
C. Fund 3

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Fund 1.** Large AUM in small caps, few holdings and big trades each raise market impact (trade size relative to average daily volume).
- **B is wrong.** Fund 2 trades liquid large caps slowly in unlit venues.
- **C is wrong.** Fund 3 has small AUM spread across many securities.

**Key Principle:**
> Market impact rises with trade size vs average daily volume; large AUM in small caps is hardest.

### Q97 — Share of Variance Explained by the Market Factor [QC-4]

**Context (QC1):**
Fund 1 factor coefficients: Market 1.080, Size 0.098, Value −0.401, Momentum 0.034. Covariances with the market factor: 0.00109 (market variance), 0.00053, 0.00022, −0.00025. Portfolio monthly standard deviation 3.74%.

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 2, the portion of total portfolio variance that is explained by the market factor in Fund 1’s existing portfolio is closest to:

A. 3%.
B. 81%.
C. 87%.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 3% is far too low; the market beta dominates.
- **B is wrong.** 81% doesn't match the calculation.
- **C is correct.** **87%.** Contribution of market factor to variance = x_m × Σ x_j Cov(m, j) = 1.080 × (1.080×0.00109 + 0.098×0.00053 − 0.401×0.00022 + 0.034×(−0.00025)) = 0.001223. Portfolio variance = 0.0374² = 0.001399. Ratio = 87%.

**Flag:** The key's formula image did not paste; the calculation shown is the standard one and I reproduced the key's 0.001223 and 87%.

**Key Principle:**
> Factor's variance contribution = x_f × Σ x_j Cov(f, j); divide by portfolio variance.

### Q98 — Effect of Doubling Active Weights With Short-Selling Constraints [QC-5]

**Context (QC1):**
New fund doubles all active weights vs Fund 1 but can't scale up short positions as much as long positions (borrowing cost and difficulty).

**Question:**
Relative to Fund 1, Chiyodasenko’s new equity fund will most likely exhibit a lower:

A. information ratio.
B. idiosyncratic risk.
C. collateral requirement.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Lower information ratio.** IR = active return / active risk. Without constraints, active return scales linearly with active risk and IR is unchanged; constraints on shorts stop active return from scaling, so IR falls.
- **B is wrong.** Doubling active weights raises, not lowers, idiosyncratic risk.
- **C is wrong.** The new fund has larger positions, so more collateral/borrowing needs, not lower.

**Key Principle:**
> Constraints → diminishing returns to scaling active risk → lower IR.

### Q99 — Effect of Switching a Same-Sector Pair for a Cross-Sector Pair on Active Risk [QC-6]

**Context (QC1):**
Fund 3 moved from an auto overweight/auto underweight pair (1pp each) to benchmark weights, and from benchmark to an energy overweight/financial underweight pair (1pp each).

**Question:**
As a result of Fund 3’s two trades, the portfolio’s active risk most likely:

A. decreased.
B. remained unchanged.
C. increased.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The new pair is less correlated, not more.
- **B is wrong.** The change in correlation changes active risk.
- **C is correct.** **Increased.** Same-sector pairs are more highly correlated (positions offset), so they contribute little active risk; energy vs financial are less correlated, so the long/short pair adds more active risk.

**Key Principle:**
> Long/short pair in the same sector offsets risk; cross-sector pair adds active risk.

### Q100 — Effect of the Same Trades on Active Share [QC-7]

**Context (QC1):**
Same two trades as above.

**Question:**
What was the effect of Fund 3’s two trades on its active share? Fund 3’s active share:

A. decreased.
B. remained unchanged.
C. increased.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Total absolute active weight didn't fall.
- **B is correct.** **Unchanged.** Active share = ½ Σ|w_p − w_b|. Before and after, the absolute active weights summed to 2pp (1pp over, 1pp under), so it's unchanged.
- **C is wrong.** Total absolute active weight didn't rise.

**Key Principle:**
> Active share depends only on total absolute active weights.

### Q101 — Risk Measure Constrained by a 'No More Than 60% Negative Deviations' Rule [QC-8]

**Context (QC1):**
Fund 3 constrains predicted returns so that no more than 60% of deviations from the mean are negative.

**Question:**
Which risk measure does Fund 3’s new risk control explicitly constrain?

A. Volatility
B. Skewness
C. Drawdown

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Volatility measures dispersion, not asymmetry.
- **B is correct.** **Skewness.** Positive skew means more than half of deviations are negative with larger positive ones; the rule constrains the asymmetry of the distribution.
- **C is wrong.** Drawdown measures peak-to-trough loss.

**Key Principle:**
> Share of deviations above/below the mean → skewness.

# QC2 — Chen / Garcia (Fundamental Law, Position Constraints, Multi-Factor Funds)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QC2*

### Q102 — Breadth Needed for a Target Active Return [QC-9]

**Context (QC2):**
Manager A: AUM $250m, active risk 5%, IC 0.15, TC 0.40, expected active return 2.5%.

**Question:**
The number of truly independent decisions Manager A would need to make in order to earn her expected active portfolio return this year is closest to:

A. 8.
B. 11.
C. 69.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 8 is roughly √BR rather than BR.
- **B is wrong.** 11 doesn't satisfy the equation.
- **C is correct.** **69.** E(R_A) = IC × √BR × σ_A × TC → 2.5% = 0.15 × √BR × 5% × 0.40 → √BR = 8.33 → BR = 69.4.

**Flag:** The key's formula image did not paste; BR = (2.5 / (0.15 × 0.40 × 5))² = 69.4 is my calculation and matches the key's 69.

**Key Principle:**
> Fundamental law: E(R_A) = TC × IC × √BR × σ_A.

### Q103 — Most Restrictive Position-Size Constraint [QC-10]

**Context (QC2):**
Pasliant: market cap $3.0bn, index weight 0.20%, ADV 1% of cap. Constraints: ≤3% of AUM; ≤10% of dollar ADV; ≤10× index weight. AUM $250m.

**Question:**
Which of the following position size policy constraints is the most restrictive in setting Manager A’s maximum position size in shares of Pasliant Corporation?

A. Liquidity
B. Allocation
C. Index weight

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Liquidity.** ADV = 1% × $3bn = $30m; 10% × 30m = **$3.0m**. Allocation: 3% × 250m = $7.5m. Index weight: 250m × (0.20% × 10) = $5.0m.
- **B is wrong.** Allocation limit is $7.5m, not binding.
- **C is wrong.** Index weight limit is $5.0m, not binding.

**Key Principle:**
> Binding constraint = the one giving the smallest dollar position.

### Q104 — Identifying a Multi-Factor Manager [QC-11]

**Context (QC2):**
Manager B: highly diversified, balanced exposures to rewarded factors, high active share, relatively low active risk target.

**Question:**
Manager B’s portfolio is most likely consistent with the characteristics of a:

A. pure indexer.
B. sector rotator.
C. multi-factor manager.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A pure indexer would have very low active share.
- **B is wrong.** A sector rotator is concentrated in sector bets.
- **C is correct.** **Multi-factor manager.** Diversified across factors and securities, high active share, low tracking error (often ~3%).

**Key Principle:**
> Multi-factor: diversified, high active share, low active risk.

### Q105 — Asset Contribution to Portfolio Variance [QC-12]

**Context (QC2):**
Weights: A1 30%, A2 45%, A3 25%. Covariances of Asset 2: with A1 0.01050, with A2 0.01960 (variance), with A3 0.00224.

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 1, the contribution of Asset 2 to Manager C’s portfolio variance is closest to:

A. 0.0025.
B. 0.0056.
C. 0.0088.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 0.0025 doesn't match.
- **B is correct.** **0.0056.** CV₂ = 0.45 × (0.30×0.01050 + 0.45×0.01960 + 0.25×0.00224) = 0.45 × 0.01253 = 0.005639.
- **C is wrong.** 0.0088 doesn't match.

**Flag:** The key's formula image did not paste; the calculation shown matches the key's 0.005639.

**Key Principle:**
> Asset i's variance contribution = w_i × Σ_j w_j Cov(i, j).

### Q106 — Best-Constructed Portfolio From Risk Characteristics [QC-13]

**Context (QC2):**
| | X | Y | Z |
|---|---|---|---|
| Unexplained risk contribution | 1% | 17% | 3% |
| Annualized volatility | 10.50% | 13.15% | 15.20% |
| Active risk | 2.90% | 8.40% | 4.20% |
| Active share | 0.71 | 0.74 | 0.63 |
(Similar costs, fees, alpha skills; X and Z have similar factor exposures.)

**Question:**
Based on Exhibits 2 and 3, which portfolio best exhibits the risk characteristics of a well-constructed portfolio?

A. Portfolio X
B. Portfolio Y
C. Portfolio Z

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Portfolio X.** Low unexplained (idiosyncratic) risk; vs Z (comparable exposures) it has lower volatility, lower active risk, and higher active share.
- **B is wrong.** Y has very high unexplained risk relative to total risk.
- **C is wrong.** Z has higher volatility and active risk, and lower active share, than X.

**Key Principle:**
> Well-constructed: low idiosyncratic risk share, lower volatility/active risk, higher active share.

### Q107 — Long–Short vs Long-Only Statements [QC-14]

**Context (QC2):**
Statement 1: a long–short portfolio can have gross exposure of 100%. Statement 2: long-only generally offers greater capacity, especially in large caps.

**Question:**
Which of Garcia’s statements regarding investing with long–short and long-only managers is correct?

A. Only Statement 1
B. Only Statement 2
C. Both Statement 1 and Statement 2

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Statement 2 is also correct.
- **B is wrong.** Statement 1 is also correct.
- **C is correct.** **Both correct.** Gross exposure = long + |short|: 50% long and 50% short gives 100%. Long-only has greater capacity, particularly in large caps.

**Key Principle:**
> Gross exposure = longs + |shorts|. Long-only = most capacity.

### Q108 — Classifying Chen's Preferred Approach [QC-15]

**Context (QC2):**
Chen prefers security-specific factors, factor timing, and generally more concentrated portfolios than a systematic approach.

**Question:**
Chen’s preferred portfolio management approach would be best described as:

A. top down.
B. systematic.
C. discretionary.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Top-down emphasizes macro factors.
- **B is wrong.** Systematic approaches are typically more diversified.
- **C is correct.** **Discretionary** (bottom-up).

**Key Principle:**
> Discretionary bottom-up: concentrated, security-specific; systematic top-down: diversified.

# QC3 — Voyager (Fundamental Law, Active Share, Capacity)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QC3*

### Q109 — Expected Active Return From the Fundamental Law [QC-16] (Essay)

**Context (QC3):**
IC 0.40, breadth 49, TC 0.20, active risk 4.0%.

**Question:**
Calculate the expected active return at inception.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

E(R_A) = IC × √BR × σ_A × TC = 0.40 × √49 × 4.0% × 0.20 = **2.24%**.

**Key Principle:**
> E(R_A) = TC × IC × √BR × σ_A.

### Q110 — Active Share [QC-17] (Essay)

**Context (QC3):**
Portfolio vs benchmark weights: Stock A 65% vs 60%; Stock B 20% vs 40%; Stock C 15% vs 0%.

**Question:**
Calculate active share and interpret.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Active share = ½ × (|0.65−0.60| + |0.20−0.40| + |0.15−0|) = ½ × 0.40 = **0.20**.

0 means the portfolio is identical to the benchmark; 1 means no overlap. 0.20 is close to a benchmark-like portfolio.

**Key Principle:**
> Active share = ½ Σ|w_p − w_b|.

### Q111 — Capacity Concerns After Large New Contributions [QC-18] (Essay)

**Context (QC3):**
New contributions $1.1bn vs AUM $100m. Holdings: A ($500m cap, ADV 1,000 shares of 50m outstanding), B ($200m; 1,000 of 20m), C ($100m; 500 of 10m).

**Question:**
Discuss two concerns regarding the effect of the new contributions on the ability to outperform.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

1. **Size vs market cap:** the three stocks' total market cap is $800m, less than the $1.1bn of contributions; even buying all of the three companies wouldn't invest the cash.
2. **Liquidity:** each stock trades less than 0.1% of shares outstanding daily, so buying large amounts would move prices and incur high trading costs, eroding alpha.

**Key Principle:**
> Capacity: position size vs market cap and daily volume; market impact erodes alpha.

### Q112 — Top-Down Systematic vs Bottom-Up Discretionary [QC-19] (Essay)

**Context (QC3):**
Voyager's current approach is bottom-up, discretionary; analysts prefer top-down, systematic for reconstituting.

**Question:**
Contrast the top-down systematic approach with Voyager's current approach.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

- **Top-down, systematic:** emphasizes macro factors and factor timing; typically results in a diversified portfolio.
- **Bottom-up, discretionary (current):** emphasizes firm-specific characteristics, may include factor timing; the portfolio may be concentrated or diversified depending on the manager.

**Key Principle:**
> Top-down systematic: macro, factors, diversified. Bottom-up discretionary: stock-specific, often concentrated.

# QC4 — Reed / TBC (Active Share, Position Limits, AUM Growth)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QC4*

### Q113 — Inferring Strategy Differences From Active Share and R-squared [QC-20] (Essay)

**Context (QC4):**
| | AE | CC |
|---|---|---|
| Active share | 0.92 | 0.71 |
| Active risk | 15.1% | 7.2% |
| Positions | 42 | 124 |
| R-squared | 0.72 | 0.98 |

**Question:**
Discuss one difference between the strategies of AE and CC from Exhibit 1.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

AE has higher active share (0.92 vs 0.71) and higher active risk (15.1% vs 7.2%) with fewer holdings (42 vs 124), and a lower R-squared (0.72 vs 0.98): its holdings differ more from the benchmark and factors explain less of its return. AE looks like a **concentrated stock-picker** (high concentration, high idiosyncratic risk). CC, with a much higher R-squared, appears to be a **concentrated factor bettor** (factor selection is very important).

**Key Principle:**
> High active share + high idiosyncratic risk + low R² → stock-picking. High R² → factor betting.

### Q114 — Days to Build a Maximum Position [QC-21] (Essay)

**Context (QC4):**
AE AUM $2bn; max position 2% of AUM; trading limit 5% of daily volume; average small-cap: market cap $1bn, 2% of cap trades daily.

**Question:**
Calculate the number of days to purchase the maximum allowable position.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

- Max position = $2bn × 2% = $40m
- Daily volume = $1bn × 2% = $20m
- Daily purchase limit = $20m × 5% = $1m
- Days = 40 / 1 = **40 days**

**Key Principle:**
> Days to trade = max position ÷ (ADV × participation limit).

### Q115 — Impact of AUM Growth on Portfolio Construction [QC-22] (Essay)

**Context (QC4):**
Exhibit 2: first 2 years vs past 3 years: AUM $235m→$1,770m; positions 18→40; turnover 40%→20%; betas: Market 0.84→0.96, Size 0.05→0.20, Value 0.20→0.10, Quality 0.30→0.00, Momentum 0.00→−0.20.

**Question:**
Discuss one way AUM growth has impacted portfolio construction.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Any one of:
- **More positions and small-cap tilt:** positions rose from 18 to 40 and Size beta from 0.05 to 0.20; small caps take longer to buy, which lowered turnover (40% → 20%), and higher implicit costs may limit alpha.
- **Factor drift:** market beta rose (0.84 → 0.96) and exposure to Quality (0.30 → 0), Momentum (0 → −0.20) and Value (0.20 → 0.10) fell, i.e., style drift as it became harder to find names that fit the strategy; more positions also lowered idiosyncratic risk.

**Key Principle:**
> Growing AUM → more names, smaller-cap tilt, lower turnover, style drift, lower alpha.

# QC5 — Winters / Treadstone (Active Return, Active Share, Fundamental Law)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QC5*

### Q116 — Active Return of a Five-Asset Portfolio [QC-23] (Essay)

**Context (QC5):**
| Asset | Portfolio wt | Return | Benchmark wt |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | 0.10 | 8.0% | 0.20 |
| B | 0.15 | 7.9% | 0.20 |
| C | 0.20 | 3.7% | 0.20 |
| D | 0.25 | 3.3% | 0.20 |
| E | 0.30 | −11.2% | 0.20 |

**Question:**
Calculate Winters's active return and interpret with reference to under/overweights.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Portfolio return = 0.10(8.0) + 0.15(7.9) + 0.20(3.7) + 0.25(3.3) + 0.30(−11.2) = **0.19%**.
Benchmark return = 0.20 × (8.0 + 7.9 + 3.7 + 3.3 − 11.2) = **2.34%**.
**Active return = −2.15%** (= Σ (w_p − w_b) × R).

He underweighted the two best performers (A, B) and overweighted the two worst (D, E, with E losing 11.2%).

**Key Principle:**
> Active return = Σ (w_p − w_b) × R_i.

### Q117 — Active Share of the Five-Asset Portfolio [QC-24] (Essay)

**Context (QC5):**
Portfolio weights 10/15/20/25/30%; benchmark 20% each.

**Question:**
Calculate the active share.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Absolute differences: 0.10 + 0.05 + 0.00 + 0.05 + 0.10 = 0.30. Active share = ½ × 0.30 = **0.15 (15%)**.

**Key Principle:**
> Active share = ½ Σ|w_p − w_b|.

### Q118 — Higher Expected Active Return: Webb vs Daniels [QC-25] (Essay)

**Context (QC5):**
Webb: TC 0.40, active risk 3%, IC 0.25, breadth 225. Daniels: TC 0.60, active risk 5%, IC 0.30, breadth 100.

**Question:**
Determine which manager has a higher expected active return.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

E(R_A) = IC × √BR × σ_A × TC.
- Webb: 0.25 × √225 × 3% × 0.40 = **4.50%**
- Daniels: 0.30 × √100 × 5% × 0.60 = **9.00%**

**Daniels**, twice Webb's.

**Key Principle:**
> E(R_A) = TC × IC × √BR × σ_A.

### Q119 — Breadth Needed to Match the Higher Active Return [QC-26] (Essay)

**Context (QC5):**
Webb (breadth 225) vs Daniels (expected 9.00%).

**Question:**
How many more independent decisions would Webb need to match Daniels?

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Doubling the return requires √BR to double: √BR = 2 × √225 = 30, so BR = 900. Additional decisions = 900 − 225 = **675**.

**Key Principle:**
> Active return scales with √BR: doubling it needs four times the breadth.

# QC6 — Anderson (LCGR and LSME Factor Attribution)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QC6*

### Q120 — Share of LCGR's Monthly Return Attributable to Alpha [QC-27]

**Context (QC6):**
LCGR annualized excess return 16.48%. Betas: Market 1.11, Size −0.04, Value −0.39, Momentum 0.07. Monthly factor returns: Market 0.94%, Size −0.37%, Value 0.48%, Momentum 0.07%.

**Question:**
The proportion of LCGR’s monthly returns attributable to alpha was closest to:

A. 9%.
B. 32%.
C. 46%.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 9% computes alpha as the active return vs the benchmark: (16.48% − 14.96%) / 16.48% = 9.2%.
- **B is correct.** **32%.** Monthly return = (1.1648)^(1/12) − 1 = 1.2794%. Factor return = 1.11(0.94%) + (−0.04)(−0.37%) + (−0.39)(0.48%) + 0.07(0.07%) = 0.8759%. Alpha = 1.2794% − 0.8759% = 0.4035%. Alpha ÷ total return = 31.5%.
- **C is wrong.** 46% divides alpha by the total factor return (0.4035% / 0.8759%) instead of by total return.

**Flag:** Exhibit 1 lists LSME active share as 982.3%, which is outside the 0–100% range of active share; I can't verify the intended value. It isn't needed to answer QC-27 to QC-29.

**Key Principle:**
> Alpha = actual return − Σ(beta × factor return); share = alpha ÷ total return (monthly).

### Q121 — Share of LSME's Variance Explained by the Market Factor [QC-28]

**Context (QC6):**
LSME volatility 12.22% (annual). Betas: Market 0.29, Size −0.44, Value 0.55, Momentum −0.10. Covariances with Market (monthly): Market 0.00233, Size 0.00042, Value −0.00031, Momentum −0.00063.

**Question:**
The proportion of LSME’s total monthly variance explained by the Market factor was closest to:

A. 9%.
B. 16%.
C. 31%.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **9%.** Monthly variance = (12.22%)² / 12 = 0.0012444. Σ beta_j × Cov(Market, j) = 0.29(0.00233) − 0.44(0.00042) + 0.55(−0.00031) − 0.10(−0.00063) = 0.0003834. Market variance contribution = 0.29 × 0.0003834 = 0.0001112. Ratio = 8.9%.
- **B is wrong.** 16% uses only the market-market term (0.29 × 0.00233), omitting the cross-factor covariances: 0.29 × 0.0006757 / 0.0012444 = 15.7%.
- **C is wrong.** 31% uses the total of the covariance terms (0.0003834) without multiplying by the market beta: 0.0003834 / 0.0012444 = 30.8%.

**Key Principle:**
> Factor's variance contribution = β_f × Σ β_j Cov(f, j); divide by total variance.

### Q122 — Fundamental-Law Element Behind Inconsistent Returns [QC-29]

**Context (QC6):**
Anderson observes LCGR's inconsistent performance (the constraints on its portfolio construction process).

**Question:**
Based on Anderson’s observations, which element of the fundamental law of active management is the most likely cause of LCGR’s inconsistent return performance?

A. Breadth
B. Transfer coefficient
C. Information coefficient

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Breadth is the number of truly independent decisions per year.
- **B is correct.** **Transfer coefficient.** TC is the ability to translate insights into decisions without constraint; constraints relate to portfolio construction. E(R_A) = IC × √BR × σ_A × TC.
- **C is wrong.** IC is how well forecast active returns match realized ones, which relates to the research process, not portfolio construction.

**Key Principle:**
> IC → research process. TC → portfolio construction. BR → independent decisions.

# QC7 — Langham (Alternative Beta, Active Share, Risk Efficiency)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QC7*

### Q123 — Most Accurate Comment on the MFC Value Fund [QC-30]

**Context (QC7):**
Exhibit 1 (monthly, 15 yrs): Market 0.71%; Russell 1000 Value benchmark: Market 0.59%, Size −0.04%, Value 0.08%, Momentum 0.08%, Alpha −0.05%, Total 0.66%. MFC Value: Market 0.50%, Size 0.02%, Value 0.11%, Momentum 0.05%, Alpha −0.05%, Total 0.63%. Comments: (1) small-cap tilt helped; (2) value out of favor; (3) fund must have lower alpha because it did 0.03pp worse than the benchmark.

**Question:**
Which of Shaw’s comments about the MFC Value Fund in Exhibit 1 is most accurate? The comment concerning:

A. alpha.
B. small-cap tilt.
C. value being out of favor.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Alpha is −0.05% for both the fund and the benchmark, so alpha doesn't explain the shortfall.
- **B is correct.** **Small-cap tilt.** The Size factor contributed +0.02%.
- **C is wrong.** Value did look out of favor (0.66% vs the market's 0.71%), but the Value factor itself contributed a positive 0.08%, so the comment isn't accurate.

**Key Principle:**
> Factor contribution = beta × factor return; compare alpha separately.

### Q124 — Classifying the Soar Fund's Process [QC-31]

**Context (QC7):**
Soar emphasizes security-specific factors, keeps security concentration low to hold idiosyncratic risk down, favors quality and value, and only occasionally considers macro conditions.

**Question:**
From the description of the Soar Fund, the most appropriate classification of its portfolio construction process is:

A. top-down systematic.
B. bottom-up systematic.
C. bottom-up discretionary.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Soar sometimes considers macro data but that is not its primary driver.
- **B is correct.** **Bottom-up systematic.** Security-specific factors are bottom-up; low idiosyncratic risk and low concentration indicate a systematic approach.
- **C is wrong.** Low idiosyncratic risk and low concentration point to systematic, not discretionary.

**Key Principle:**
> Security-specific factors = bottom-up; diversified/low idiosyncratic risk = systematic.

### Q125 — Fund A vs Fund B: Most Accurate Comment [QC-32]

**Context (QC7):**
Same index, similar active share and number of positions; Fund A's active risk is 7%, almost three times Fund B's. Comments: (1) Fund B has more sector bets; (2) Fund A has higher fees; (3) Fund A has greater dispersion about the benchmark.

**Question:**
In regard to Shaw’s comments about Fund A and Fund B, the one that is most accurate concerns:

A. Fund A’s fees.
B. Fund A’s dispersion.
C. Fund B’s sector bets.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Fees are more likely to be tied to active share, which is the same for both funds.
- **B is correct.** **Fund A's dispersion.** Higher active risk means returns are more widely dispersed (up and down) around the benchmark.
- **C is wrong.** Sector bets raise active risk, so Fund A, not Fund B, is the more likely sector bettor.

**Key Principle:**
> Same active share but different active risk → the higher-risk fund likely makes sector/factor bets.

### Q126 — Diversified Multi-Factor Fund From Risk Targets [QC-33]

**Context (QC7):**
| | Fund X | Fund Y | Fund Z |
|---|---|---|---|
| Target active risk | 8% | 7% | 4% |
| Max sector deviation | 1% | 15% | 10% |
| Max single-security risk contribution | 4% | 2% | 1% |

**Question:**
Among the funds shown in Exhibit 2, which one is most likely managed using a diversified multi-factor investor approach?

A. Fund X
B. Fund Y
C. Fund Z

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Fund X: high active risk, high single-security risk, tight sector limits → stock picking.
- **B is wrong.** Fund Y: high active risk and high sector tolerance → sector rotation.
- **C is correct.** **Fund Z.** Low single-security risk (1%) and modest active risk (4%), with flexibility on sectors, indicate a diversified portfolio emphasizing factor exposures.

**Key Principle:**
> Stock picker: high security risk, tight sector limits. Sector rotator: loose sector limits. Multi-factor: low security risk, low active risk.

### Q127 — Fund Most Risk-Efficient for Amity [QC-34]

**Context (QC7):**
| | Blue | Ash | March |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sharpe | 1.11 | 0.90 | 0.92 |
| Active risk | 5.5% | 6.0% | 3.2% |
| Active share | 0.41 | 0.48 | 0.75 |
| Securities | 340 | 290 | 140 |
| Volatility | 11.5% | 14.7% | 14.9% |
| Covariance with Amity fund | Low | High | Low |
(Similar performance histories, benchmarks and fees.)

**Question:**
The fund in Exhibit 3 that is most consistent with Quint’s requirements is:

A. Ash.
B. Blue.
C. March.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Ash has the highest active risk, with more securities and lower active share than March.
- **B is wrong.** Blue has the most securities and a low active share; it has greater active risk than March despite more holdings.
- **C is correct.** **March.** Lowest active risk (3.2%) with the fewest securities (140) and the highest active share (0.75).

**Key Principle:**
> Risk-efficient: high active share for low active risk.

### Q128 — Replacement Fund That Minimizes Total Active Risk [QC-35]

**Context (QC7):**
Same Exhibit 3. Funds with low covariance to the existing Amity portfolio: Blue, March; high covariance: Ash.

**Question:**
From Exhibit 3, the replacement candidate fund that, if included, will most likely minimize the active risk of the final Amity equity fund is:

A. Ash.
B. Blue.
C. March.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Ash.** Replacing part of the portfolio with a fund of high covariance to the existing portfolio keeps the total's active risk lowest.
- **B is wrong.** Low covariance lowers overall volatility, not active risk (active risk is volatility relative to the benchmark).
- **C is wrong.** Same reasoning as Blue.

**Flag:** The key's reasoning (that a high-covariance fund minimizes active risk of the combined portfolio) is stated without a calculation; I haven't verified it independently.

**Key Principle:**
> Low covariance lowers absolute volatility; the key says high covariance minimizes active risk.

# QC8 — Epsilon Institute (Fraser, Mattley, Western and Herrick Funds)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QC8*

### Q129 — Investment Process of a Discretionary Fund [QC-36]

**Context (QC8):**
Fraser's fund is built with a discretionary approach using the four Fama–French factors.

**Question:**
The investment process indicated for the Fraser Fund is most likely designed around which of the following?

A. A balanced exposure to known rewarded factors
B. Research-based rules across a broad universe of securities
C. The inclusion of non-financial variables, such as pricing power

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Balanced exposure to known rewarded factors is systematic.
- **B is wrong.** Research-based rules across a broad universe is systematic.
- **C is correct.** **Non-financial variables, such as pricing power.** Discretionary processes seek active returns from firm-specific factors and give more concentrated portfolios.

**Key Principle:**
> Discretionary: firm-specific insight, concentrated. Systematic: rules, broad universe, balanced factors.

### Q130 — Fraser Fund Return Unexplained by Rewarded Factors [QC-37]

**Context (QC8):**
Fund excess return 0.55%/month. Betas: Market 0.91, Size 0.15, Value 0.60, Momentum 0.08. Factor returns: 0.61%, 0.17%, 0.18%, 0.72%. Benchmark 0.72%. Risk-free 0.33%.

**Question:**
Using Exhibit 1, the average monthly return of the Fraser Fund that is unexplained by rewarded factors is closest to:

A. –0.20%.
B. –0.17%.
C. 0.13%.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **−0.20%.** Factor return = 0.91(0.61) + 0.15(0.17) + 0.60(0.18) + 0.08(0.72) = 0.75%. Alpha = 0.55% − 0.75% = −0.20%.
- **B is wrong.** −0.17% is the active return: 0.55% − 0.72%.
- **C is wrong.** 0.13% adds the risk-free rate back: 0.33% − 0.20%.

**Key Principle:**
> Alpha = actual − Σ(beta × factor return); use excess returns.

### Q131 — Investment Style: High Active Share, Low Active Risk [QC-38]

**Context (QC8):**
Mattley Fund: relatively high active share, relatively low active risk.

**Question:**
The Mattley Fund manager is most likely following which investment style?

A. Sector rotation
B. Closet indexing
C. Diversified stock picking

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A sector rotator has high active risk.
- **B is wrong.** A closet index has both low active share and low active risk.
- **C is correct.** **Diversified stock picking.** Holdings differ substantially from the benchmark (high active share) with low idiosyncratic risk from diversification.

**Key Principle:**
> High AS + low AR = diversified stock picker. Low AS + low AR = closet indexer. High AR = concentrated/sector/factor bets.

### Q132 — Least Accurate Comment on Active Share and Active Risk [QC-39]

**Context (QC8):**
Single-factor model comments: (1) active risk rises with idiosyncratic volatility; (2) the active risk attributed to active share is smaller in more diversified portfolios; (3) if factor exposure is fully neutralized, active share will be entirely attributed to active risk.

**Question:**
In Lazare and Warrack’s comments about Active Share and active risk, the comment that is least accurate is the one concerning:

A. portfolio diversification.
B. neutralizing factor exposure.
C. increasing idiosyncratic volatility.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Accurate: active risk attributed to active share is smaller for more diversified portfolios with lower idiosyncratic risk.
- **B is correct.** **Neutralizing factor exposure is the false comment.** Keyed B is the least accurate statement. The key says that with factor exposure neutralized, active risk is entirely attributable to active share.
- **C is wrong.** Accurate: active risk rises with factor and idiosyncratic volatility.

**Flag:** The key calls the neutralizing comment incorrect, but its explanation's corrected statement is a reordering of the same wording. The key's direction (active risk attributable to active share, not the other way around) is what I used.

**Key Principle:**
> Neutralize factor exposure → active risk comes entirely from active share (not the reverse).

### Q133 — Most Restrictive Constraint for the Western Fund [QC-40]

**Context (QC8):**
Average company cap $1.8bn, 0.90% traded daily; positions ≤ 7% of ADV; ≤ 1.75% of AUM; ≥ 60 securities; AUM $100m.

**Question:**
If the Western Fund manager is hired, which constraint is most restrictive with respect to average position size? The constraint related to:

A. liquidity.
B. diversification.
C. asset allocation.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Liquidity.** ADV = 0.90% × $1.8bn = $16.2m; 7% = **$1.13m**. Allocation: 1.75% × $100m = $1.75m. Diversification: $100m / 60 = $1.67m. Smallest binds.
- **B is wrong.** Diversification allows up to $1.67m.
- **C is wrong.** Allocation allows $1.75m.

**Key Principle:**
> Binding constraint = smallest dollar position.

### Q134 — Country A's Share of Herrick Fund Variance [QC-41]

**Context (QC8):**
Weights A 30%, B 50%, C 20%. Portfolio std dev 13.2%. Covariances: A–A 0.0625, A–B 0.015, A–C 0.00675; Cov(A, portfolio) = 0.0276.

**Question:**
The proportion of the Herrick Fund’s portfolio variance contributed by Country A is closest to:

A. 20.9%.
B. 47.5%.
C. 56.8%.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 20.9% divides Cov(A, P) by the standard deviation instead of the variance (0.0276 / 0.132).
- **B is correct.** **47.5%.** Contribution = 0.30 × 0.30 × 0.0625 + 0.30 × 0.50 × 0.015 + 0.30 × 0.20 × 0.00675 = 0.00828 (= 0.30 × 0.0276). Portfolio variance = 0.132² = 0.01742. Ratio = 47.5%.
- **C is wrong.** 56.8% uses w_A × σ_A / σ_P = 0.30 × 25 / 13.2.

**Key Principle:**
> Asset contribution to variance = w_i × Cov(i, P); ÷ portfolio variance.

# Standalone — Portfolio Construction

### Q135 — Reliability of Historical Performance: Single-Factor vs Multi-Factor [QC-42]

**Context:**
Manager A: value style based on high dividend yield. Manager B: multidimensional factors (momentum, volatility, quality, growth). Similar AUM and alpha.

**Question:**
Portfolio Manager A follows value style investing and buys securities based on high dividend yield. Portfolio Manager B follows a multidimensional, factor-based approach including momentum, volatility, quality and growth. An investment advisor wants to determine if the historical performance of either manager is a reliable indicator of their expected future outperformance. If the assets under management and alpha returns for the past three years are similar for both portfolio managers, the investment advisor is most likely to conclude that:

A. Portfolio Manager A’s historical performance is more reliable.
B. Portfolio Manager B’s historical performance is more reliable.
C. the historical performance of both portfolio managers are equally reliable.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A depends on one narrowly defined value bias.
- **B is correct.** **Manager B is more reliable.** Per the fundamental law, confidence rises when performance comes from a larger sample of independent decisions; A's performance rests on a single narrow dimension.
- **C is wrong.** They are not equally reliable.

**Key Principle:**
> More independent decisions (breadth) → performance more reliable as evidence of skill.

### Q136 — Greatest Alpha Potential With Unmatched Systematic Risk [QC-43]

**Context:**
Long/short (% of capital): A 120/0; B 130/30; C 0/100.

**Question:**
The following are long and short positions as a percentage of capital for three portfolios:

|  | Long Positions | Short Positions |
|---|---|---|
| Portfolio A | 120 | 0 |
| Portfolio B | 130 | 30 |
| Portfolio C | 0 | 100 |

If the systematic risk of long and short positions are not matched, which portfolio offers the potential to generate the greatest alpha?

A. Portfolio A
B. Portfolio B
C. Portfolio C

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A is a leveraged long-only portfolio (only long insights).
- **B is correct.** **Portfolio B.** Gross exposure 160% (130 + 30); it can exploit both long and short insights.
- **C is wrong.** C exploits only short insights.

**Key Principle:**
> Long–short portfolios can use both long and short insights; gross exposure = long + |short|.

### Q137 — Within-Sector vs Across-Sector Bets and Active Risk [QC-44]

**Context:**
Portfolio 1 over/underweights within sectors; Portfolio 2 across sectors; similar active share; goal: lower active risk.

**Question:**
An investor is evaluating two portfolios. Portfolio 1 typically overweights and underweights stocks within each sector. Portfolio 2 overweights and underweights stocks across sectors. Both portfolios are benchmarked to the same index and have similar Active Share. The objective is to choose a portfolio with lower active risk. The investor is most likely to:

A. choose Portfolio 1.
B. choose Portfolio 2.
C. be indifferent between Portfolio 1 and Portfolio 2.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Portfolio 1.** Within-sector bets generate little active risk because of high correlation; cross-sector bets add more.
- **B is wrong.** Across-sector bets generate more active risk.
- **C is wrong.** Similar active share can come with quite different active risk; active risk depends on cross-correlation, active share doesn't.

**Key Principle:**
> Active share ignores correlation; active risk reflects it.

### Q138 — Inference From Active Share and Active Risk [QC-45]

**Context:**
| | Manager A | Manager B |
|---|---|---|
| Active share | 0.73 | 0.77 |
| Active risk | 6.5% | 2.7% |
| Positions | 40 | 36 |
(50-stock large-cap index.)

**Question:**
The following details relate to two active equity managers benchmarked to a 50-stock large-cap index:

|  | Manager A | Manager B |
|---|---|---|
| Active Share | 0.73 | 0.77 |
| Active risk | 6.5% | 2.7% |
| Number of positions | 40 | 36 |

Based only on this information, an analyst is most likely to infer that:

A. Manager B's returns is more dispersed about the benchmark than Manager A's returns.
B. Manager A’s active decisions are driven at the sector level rather than at the security level.
C. Manager B's portfolio is less correlated with its benchmark compared to Manager A's portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A, not B, has returns more dispersed about the benchmark (higher active risk).
- **B is correct.** **A's active decisions are driven at the sector level.** Similar active share and position counts but much higher active risk suggests sector-level bets.
- **C is wrong.** Lower active risk means B is more, not less, correlated with its benchmark.

**Key Principle:**
> Same active share, higher active risk → sector/factor bets rather than security-level bets.

### Q139 — Closet Indexer Raising Cash [QC-46]

**Context:**
A closet indexer sells its largest holding and holds the proceeds as cash.

**Question:**
A closet indexer benchmarked to a large-cap index sells the stock with the highest weight in the portfolio and maintains the sale proceeds as cash. This significant allocation to cash most likely lowers the portfolio's:

A. active risk.
B. absolute risk.
C. Active Share.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Cash has a low correlation with the benchmark's assets, which increases active risk.
- **B is correct.** **Absolute risk.** Cash has low volatility and low correlation with other assets.
- **C is wrong.** Active share rises as weights move away from the index.

**Key Principle:**
> Cash lowers absolute risk but raises active risk and active share.

### Q140 — Concentrated Fund With Large Cash Position [QC-47]

**Context:**
Active fund; high security concentration; large cash position.

**Question:**
An active fund benchmarked to a large-cap index has a high security concentration and a large position in cash. The fund is most likely to have a:

A. low active risk because of the large position in cash.
B. high active risk because of high security concentration.
C. low Active Share because of high security concentration.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Cash lowers absolute risk but, being uncorrelated with other assets, adds active risk.
- **B is correct.** **High active risk because of high security concentration.**
- **C is wrong.** Concentrated portfolios generally have high active share.

**Key Principle:**
> Concentration → higher active risk and higher active share.

### Q141 — Drawdown Definition [QC-48]

**Question:**
Drawdown measures a portfolio's:

A. loss from its high point until it begins to recover.
B. average loss incurred if the value at risk cutoff is exceeded.
C. minimum loss that would be expected a certain percentage of the time over a specific period of time.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Loss from its high point until it begins to recover.**
- **B is wrong.** That describes CVaR.
- **C is wrong.** That describes VaR.

**Key Principle:**
> Drawdown = peak-to-trough loss before recovery.

### Q142 — Breadth of Expertise [QC-49]

**Question:**
Which of the following best describes the breadth of expertise as an element of a manager's portfolio construction?

A. The number of truly independent decisions made by the manager each year
B. The ability to translate portfolio insights into investment decisions without constraint
C. The extent to which the manager’s forecasted active returns correspond to the realized active returns

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Number of truly independent decisions per year.**
- **B is wrong.** That is the transfer coefficient.
- **C is wrong.** That is the information coefficient.

**Key Principle:**
> BR = independent decisions; TC = constraint-free implementation; IC = forecast accuracy.

### Q143 — Breadth Implied by Expected Active Return [QC-50]

**Context:**
Active risk 3.0%, IC 0.15, TC 0.6, expected active return 1.6%.

**Question:**
An analyst gathers the following information about a fund manager:

- **Active risk (%):** 3.0
- **Information coefficient:** 0.15
- **Transfer coefficient:** 0.6
- **Expected active return (%):** 1.6

The number of truly independent decisions that the manager would need to make to generate their expected active portfolio return is closest to:

A. 6.
B. 13.
C. 35.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 6 omits the square root (it's √BR).
- **B is wrong.** 13 omits the transfer coefficient.
- **C is correct.** **35.** 1.6% = 0.15 × √BR × 3% × 0.6 → √BR = 0.016 / 0.0027 = 5.93 → BR = 35.1.

**Key Principle:**
> E(R_A) = TC × IC × √BR × σ_A.

### Q144 — Systematic Bottom-Up Manager [QC-51]

**Question:**
A portfolio manager following a systematic bottom-up approach will most likely:

A. implement factor timing.
B. run a concentrated portfolio.
C. emphasize security-specific factors.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Factor timing is more likely among discretionary managers, especially top-down; systematic top-down managers emphasize macro factors and factor timing.
- **B is wrong.** Systematic managers are unlikely to run concentrated portfolios.
- **C is correct.** **Emphasizes security-specific factors.**

**Key Principle:**
> Bottom-up = security-specific factors. Systematic = diversified.

### Q145 — Supporting a Higher AUM [QC-52]

**Question:**
To support a higher level of assets under management (AUM), a portfolio manager is more likely to have:

A. fewer securities in her portfolio.
B. lower level of idiosyncratic risk in her portfolio.
C. stocks with lower average market capitalization in her portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** More, not fewer, securities support higher AUM.
- **B is correct.** **Lower idiosyncratic risk.** It implies more securities and smaller positions, supporting more AUM.
- **C is wrong.** Small-cap funds have greater AUM-related challenges.

**Key Principle:**
> Capacity: more holdings, larger/more liquid stocks.

### Q146 — Fund With the Advantage in Managing Slippage [QC-53]

**Context:**
Options: large-cap $2bn; large-cap $500m; small-cap $500m.

**Question:**
Which of the following funds has the greatest advantage in managing slippage costs?

A. A large-cap fund with $2 billion assets under management
B. A large-cap fund with $500 million assets under management
C. A small-cap fund with $500 million assets under management

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Larger AUM than B.
- **B is correct.** **Large-cap, $500m.** Smaller AUM is easier to implement strategically, and slippage is greater in smaller-cap securities.
- **C is wrong.** Small caps have higher slippage.

**Key Principle:**
> Slippage rises with AUM and falls with market cap/liquidity.

### Q147 — Greatest Risk Efficiency [QC-54]

**Context:**
| | Active share | Volatility | Expected return |
|---|---|---|---|
| Product 1 | 0.78 | 19.2% | 6.3% |
| Product 2 | 0.77 | 14.2% | 6.2% |
| Product 3 | 0.42 | 14.1% | 5.6% |
(Similar active risks, costs, factor exposures.)

**Question:**
An analyst gathers the following information about three investment products with similar mandates:

|  | Active Share | Annualized Volatility (%) | Expected Annualized Return (%) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Product 1 | 0.78 | 19.2 | 6.3 |
| Product 2 | 0.77 | 14.2 | 6.2 |
| Product 3 | 0.42 | 14.1 | 5.6 |

If all three products have similar active risks, costs, and factor exposures, the product with the greatest risk efficiency is:

A. Product 1.
B. Product 2.
C. Product 3.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Product 1 has higher volatility than Product 2.
- **B is correct.** **Product 2.** Higher active share than Product 3 at similar absolute risk, and lower absolute volatility than Product 1 at similar active share.
- **C is wrong.** Product 3 has a lower active share than Product 2 at similar volatility.

**Key Principle:**
> Similar costs/factors: prefer lower volatility; if similar risk, higher active share.

### Q148 — Long–Short Portfolio With Greatest Alpha Potential [QC-55]

**Context:**
Long/short/cash (% of capital): P1 130/30/0; P2 90/90/100; P3 40/100/160.

**Question:**
An analyst gathers the following information (as a percentage of capital) about three long-short portfolios:

|  | Long Position | Short Position | Cash |
|---|---|---|---|
| Portfolio 1 | 130 | 30 | 0 |
| Portfolio 2 | 90 | 90 | 100 |
| Portfolio 3 | 40 | 100 | 160 |

If an investor is interested in maximizing the potential for obtaining greater alpha, which portfolio should be recommended?

A. Portfolio 1
B. Portfolio 2
C. Portfolio 3

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** P1 has the highest net exposure (130 − 30 = 100), not gross.
- **B is correct.** **Portfolio 2.** Gross exposure = long + |short|: P1 160, **P2 180**, P3 140. Shorts fund the longs, giving the most gross leverage.
- **C is wrong.** The key's rejection adds cash into gross exposure (P3 300 vs P2 280); that isn't the gross-exposure definition used for B.

**Flag:** The key's explanation for C adds cash to gross exposure (P2 280, P3 300), which conflicts with the definition used in the B explanation; I followed the B definition.

**Key Principle:**
> Gross exposure = |long| + |short|; net = long − |short|.

# QD1 — Serena / Kiest (Liability Classification, Immunization Structure, Benchmarks, Replication)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QD1*

### Q149 — Classifying Kiest's Liabilities [QD-1]

**Context (QD1):**
Bonds pay fixed amounts at the end of years 1, 2 and 3 ($9.572m, $8.392m, $8.2m); no call or put provisions.

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 1, Kiest’s liabilities would be classified as:

A. Type I.
B. Type II.
C. Type III.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Type I.** Type I liabilities have known amounts and known timing.
- **B is wrong.** Type II has known amounts but uncertain timing.
- **C is wrong.** Type III has uncertain amounts but known timing.

**Key Principle:**
> Type I: known amount, known date. II: known amount, uncertain date. III: uncertain amount, known date. IV: both uncertain.

### Q150 — Portfolio With the Greatest Structural Risk [QD-2]

**Context (QD1):**
Liability portfolio: Macaulay duration 5.34, convexity 33.05, BPV $10,505.
| | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Bonds | 4.5y 2.63%; 7.0y 3.50% | 3.0y 2.00%; 6.0y 3.25%; 8.5y 3.88% | 1.5y 1.25%; 11.5y 4.38% |
| Macaulay dur. | 5.35 | 5.34 | 5.36 |
| Cash flow yield | 3.16% | 3.33% | 3.88% |
| Convexity | 31.98 | 34.51 | 50.21 |
| BPV | $10,524 | $10,506 | $10,516 |

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 2, the portfolio with the greatest structural risk is:

A. Portfolio A.
B. Portfolio B.
C. Portfolio C.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A's convexity is 31.98, with bonds closer to the horizon.
- **B is wrong.** B is laddered, with convexity 34.51.
- **C is correct.** **Portfolio C.** It is a barbell (1.5y and 11.5y) with the highest convexity (50.21); structural risk rises with dispersion and convexity, exposing it to twists and non-parallel shifts.

**Key Principle:**
> Structural risk ↑ with dispersion (barbell, higher convexity).

### Q151 — Portfolio That Fails the Multiple-Liability Immunization Requirements [QD-3]

**Context (QD1):**
Same Exhibit 2; liability convexity 33.05, BPV $10,505.

**Question:**
Which portfolio in Exhibit 2 fails to meet the requirements to achieve immunization for multiple liabilities?

A. Portfolio A
B. Portfolio B
C. Portfolio C

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Portfolio A.** Requirements: asset money duration (BPV) matches the liabilities, and asset convexity exceeds liability convexity. All three have similar BPVs, but A's convexity (31.98) is below the liabilities' 33.05.
- **B is wrong.** B's convexity (34.51) exceeds 33.05.
- **C is wrong.** C's convexity (50.21) exceeds 33.05.

**Key Principle:**
> Multiple-liability immunization: match money duration/BPV; asset convexity ≥ liability convexity.

### Q152 — Portfolio B (Laddered) vs Portfolio C (Barbell) [QD-4]

**Context (QD1):**
Portfolio B: 3.0y, 6.0y, 8.5y bonds. Portfolio C: 1.5y and 11.5y bonds.

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 2, relative to Portfolio C, Portfolio B:

A. has higher cash flow reinvestment risk.
B. is a more desirable portfolio for liquidity management.
C. provides less protection from yield curve shifts and twists.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Not supported by the key.
- **B is correct.** **More desirable for liquidity management.** B is laddered, so a bond is always near redemption and can provide emergency liquidity; a barbell only has short- and long-end maturities.
- **C is wrong.** B provides more, not less, protection from shifts and twists than the barbell C (lower convexity and dispersion).

**Key Principle:**
> Ladder: liquidity management; barbell: highest dispersion and structural risk.

### Q153 — Risk Indicated by Serena's Assumptions [QD-5]

**Context (QD1):**
Assumptions: parallel yield curve shifts; matching bond types and quality; rebalancing with bonds rather than derivatives.

**Question:**
Serena’s three assumptions regarding the duration-matching strategy indicate the presence of:

A. model risk.
B. spread risk.
C. counterparty credit risk.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Model risk.** Assumptions about future events and approximations may prove wrong, e.g. a non-parallel shift.
- **B is wrong.** Spread risk isn't what these assumptions indicate.
- **C is wrong.** Counterparty credit risk isn't what these assumptions indicate.

**Key Principle:**
> Model risk: assumptions and approximations (e.g., parallel shifts) turn out wrong.

### Q154 — Least Appropriate Benchmark for a Long Horizon [QD-6]

**Context (QD1):**
Global Aggregate (duration 7.73); Global Aggregate GDP Weighted (7.71); Global High Yield (4.18). Young workforce, long-term horizon.

**Question:**
The global bond benchmark in Exhibit 3 that is least appropriate for Kiest to use is the:

A. Global Aggregate Index.
B. Global High Yield Index.
C. Global Aggregate GDP Weighted Index.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Duration 7.73 is appropriate for a long horizon.
- **B is correct.** **Global High Yield.** Its duration (4.18) is much shorter than the long horizon.
- **C is wrong.** Duration 7.71 is appropriate for a long horizon.

**Key Principle:**
> Match benchmark duration to the liability/horizon.

### Q155 — Replication Method Meeting Cost and Enhanced-Return Guidelines [QD-7]

**Context (QD1):**
Replicate a broadly diversified global bond benchmark cost-effectively, with an enhanced return objective.

**Question:**
To meet both of Trey’s guidelines for the pension’s bond fund investment, Serena should recommend:

A. pure indexing.
B. enhanced indexing.
C. active management.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Pure indexing is costly for a broad bond index and gives no enhancement opportunity.
- **B is correct.** **Enhanced indexing.** Buys fewer securities than the index but matches primary risk factors; allows small return enhancements at low tracking error.
- **C is wrong.** Active management goes beyond replicating the benchmark.

**Key Principle:**
> Enhanced indexing: fewer holdings, matched risk factors, modest added return.

### Q156 — Valid Reason for Choosing Bond Mutual Funds [QD-8]

**Context (QD1):**
Reasons: (1) total return swaps have much higher transaction costs and initial cash outlay than bond mutual funds; (2) unlike bond mutual funds, bond ETFs can trade at discounts to underlying indexes, and those can persist; (3) bond mutual funds trade throughout the day at NAV.

**Question:**
Which of Trey’s reasons for choosing bond mutual funds as an investment vehicle is correct?

A. 1
B. 2
C. 3

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Reason 1 is not what the key calls correct.
- **B is correct.** **Reason 2.** Many fixed-income securities are thinly traded or not traded, which allows a persistent divergence between an ETF's price and its NAV.
- **C is wrong.** Mutual funds trade once per day at NAV, not throughout the day.

**Flag:** The key gives no explanation for why Reasons 1 and 3 are wrong; the one-line reasons here are my reading (mutual funds transacting at end-of-day NAV is general knowledge, not from the key).

**Key Principle:**
> Bond ETFs can trade at persistent discounts to NAV; mutual funds transact at end-of-day NAV.

# QD2 — SD&R / DFC (Enhanced Indexing, Immunization, LDI, Custom Benchmarks)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QD2*

### Q157 — Previous Manager's Process [QD-9]

**Context (QD2):**
Previous manager focused on interest rate sensitivities of assets and liabilities in allocation decisions. SD&R analyzes liabilities first, then builds assets on their sensitivity.

**Question:**
The investment process followed by DFC’s previous fixed-income manager is best described as:

A. asset-driven liabilities.
B. liability-driven investing.
C. asset–liability management.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** No such standard classification in the key.
- **B is wrong.** LDI starts with the liabilities (SD&R's approach).
- **C is correct.** **Asset–liability management.** Considers both assets and liabilities.

**Key Principle:**
> ALM: both sides; LDI: liabilities first.

### Q158 — Advantage of Enhanced Indexing Over Full Replication [QD-10]

**Context (QD2):**
Approach 1: fully replicate a small range of government bond benchmarks. Approach 2: enhanced indexing on a subset of the Aggregate, customizable.

**Question:**
Relative to Approach 1, gaining passive exposure, an advantage of Approach 2 is that it:

A. minimizes tracking error.
B. requires less risk analysis.
C. is more appropriate for socially responsible investors.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Full replication, not enhanced indexing, minimizes tracking error.
- **B is wrong.** Enhanced indexing requires more, not less, risk analysis.
- **C is correct.** **More appropriate for socially responsible investors.** Enhanced indexing can include/exclude sectors to reflect client (e.g., ESG) preferences.

**Key Principle:**
> Enhanced indexing: customizable (ESG) but higher tracking error and more risk analysis.

### Q159 — Rate of Return Locked In by Immunization [QD-11]

**Context (QD2):**
Two-bond portfolio: weighted average YTM 9.55%, weighted average coupon 10.25%, cash flow yield 9.85%.

**Question:**
The two-bond hypothetical portfolio’s immunization goal is to lock in a rate of return equal to:

A. 9.55%.
B. 9.85%.
C. 10.25%.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 9.55% is the weighted average YTM.
- **B is correct.** **9.85%.** Immunization locks in the cash flow yield (IRR) of the portfolio.
- **C is wrong.** 10.25% is the weighted average coupon.

**Key Principle:**
> Immunization locks in the cash flow yield.

### Q160 — Statements About LDI [QD-12]

**Context (QD2):**
S1: even with known amount and date, measurement errors in key parameters may hurt. S2: cash flow matching mitigates risk from non-parallel yield curve shifts.

**Question:**
Which of Molly’s statements about liability-driven investing is (are) correct?

A. Statement 1 only
B. Statement 2 only
C. Both Statement 1 and Statement 2

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Statement 2 is also correct.
- **B is wrong.** Statement 1 is also correct.
- **C is correct.** **Both correct.** Measurement error arises even for Type I; cash flow matching removes dependence on parallel shifts.

**Flag:** The key's explanation covers Statement 1 and the structural-risk point about immunization; it doesn't separately explain Statement 2 about cash flow matching. I haven't added an explanation of my own for it.

**Key Principle:**
> Cash flow matching avoids yield-curve and reinvestment risk but is hard to implement.

### Q161 — Best Portfolio to Immunize a Nine-Year Liability [QD-13]

**Context (QD2):**
Liability due in 9 years.
| | P1 | P2 | P3 | P4 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cash flow yield | 7.48% | 7.50% | 7.53% | 7.51% |
| Avg time to maturity | 11.2 | 9.8 | 9.0 | 10.1 |
| Macaulay duration | 9.8 | 8.9 | 8.0 | 9.1 |
| MV-weighted duration | 9.1 | 8.5 | 7.8 | 8.6 |
| Convexity | 154.11 | 131.75 | 130.00 | 109.32 |

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 1, which of the portfolios will best immunize SD&R’s single liability?

A. Portfolio 1
B. Portfolio 2
C. Portfolio 3

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Macaulay duration of 9.8 is further from 9.
- **B is correct.** **Portfolio 2.** For a single liability, match Macaulay duration to the horizon; 8.9 is closest to 9 among the three the key considers (P1 9.8, P2 8.9, P3 8.0).
- **C is wrong.** Macaulay duration of 8.0 is further from 9.

**Flag:** Portfolio 4 (Macaulay 9.1) is closer to 9 than Portfolio 2 (8.9) but isn't offered as a choice in QD-13.

**Key Principle:**
> Single liability: Macaulay duration = horizon.

### Q162 — Portfolio That Minimizes Structural Risk [QD-14]

**Context (QD2):**
Same Exhibit 1.

**Question:**
Which of the portfolios in Exhibit 1best minimizes the structural risk to a single-liability immunization strategy?

A. Portfolio 1
B. Portfolio 3
C. Portfolio 4

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** P1 has the highest convexity (154.11).
- **B is wrong.** P3's Macaulay duration (8.0) is furthest from 9, and its convexity (130.00) is higher than P4's.
- **C is correct.** **Portfolio 4.** Structural risk is reduced by minimizing dispersion, i.e., convexity, for a given duration; P4 has the lowest convexity (109.32) with Macaulay duration (9.1) close to 9.

**Key Principle:**
> Min convexity subject to duration match = min structural risk.

### Q163 — Custom Benchmark Characteristic That Fails [QD-15]

**Context (QD2):**
Char. 1: US investment-grade corporates, minimum issue $250m. Char. 2: valued weekly. Char. 3: historical prices and turnover available.

**Question:**
Which of the custom benchmark’s characteristics violates the requirements for an appropriate benchmark portfolio?

A. Characteristic 1
B. Characteristic 2
C. Characteristic 3

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Clear inclusion rules (investment grade, minimum issue size) are required.
- **B is correct.** **Characteristic 2.** An appropriate benchmark requires daily valuation.
- **C is wrong.** Past returns and turnover availability are required.

**Key Principle:**
> Benchmark requirements: clear rules, investable, daily valuation, history, turnover data.

### Q164 — Benchmark for Short- and Intermediate-Term Bonds [QD-16]

**Context (QD2):**
B1: US Bond Index (80% govt/20% corp), duration 8.7. B2: 50% US Corporate Index (7.5) + 50% Short-Term Treasury Index (0.5). B3: Global Aggregate (60% EUR corp/40% USD corp), 12.3.

**Question:**
Based on DFC’s bond holdings and Exhibit 2, Molly should recommend:

A. Benchmark 1.
B. Benchmark 2.
C. Benchmark 3.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Duration 8.7 is too long for short assets.
- **B is correct.** **Benchmark 2.** A blend of a short-duration and a longer-duration sub-benchmark matches DFC's short- and intermediate-term holdings.
- **C is wrong.** EUR exposure and 12.3 duration don't fit USD short/intermediate bonds.

**Key Principle:**
> Benchmark must match the portfolio's duration and currency; blend sub-benchmarks if needed.

# QD3 — Radford (Zero-Coupon Matching, Duration, Non-Parallel Shifts, Indexing)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QD3*

### Q165 — Disadvantage of Zero-Coupon Matching [QD-17]

**Context (QD3):**
Strategy 1: immunize single liabilities using zero-coupon bonds held to maturity.

**Question:**
A disadvantage of Strategy 1 is that:

A. price risk still exists.
B. interest rate volatility introduces risk to effective matching.
C. there may not be enough bonds available to match all liabilities.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Held-to-maturity zeros remove price risk.
- **B is wrong.** Matching with zeros held to maturity doesn't depend on rate volatility.
- **C is correct.** **Not enough bonds available to match all liabilities.** Zeros may be unavailable at the needed maturities/amounts.

**Key Principle:**
> Zero-coupon matching: no price/reinvestment risk, but availability is limited.

### Q166 — Duration Measure to Match [QD-18]

**Context (QD3):**
Strategy 2: coupon bonds with continuous duration matching.

**Question:**
Which duration measure should be matched when implementing Strategy 2?

A. Key rate
B. Modified
C. Macaulay

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Key rate duration addresses non-parallel shifts.
- **B is wrong.** Modified duration measures price sensitivity.
- **C is correct.** **Macaulay.** When the horizon equals Macaulay duration, price and coupon reinvestment effects offset.

**Key Principle:**
> Immunize: Macaulay duration = horizon.

### Q167 — Effect of an Upward Shift on an Immunized Portfolio [QD-19]

**Context (QD3):**
Strategy 2 with an upward yield curve shift.

**Question:**
An upward shift in the yield curve on Strategy 2 will most likely result in the:

A. price effect canceling the coupon reinvestment effect.
B. price effect being greater than the coupon reinvestment effect.
C. coupon reinvestment effect being greater than the price effect.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Price effect cancels the coupon reinvestment effect.** Prices fall, reinvestment rates rise.
- **B is wrong.** They offset one another.
- **C is wrong.** They offset one another.

**Key Principle:**
> Immunized: price and reinvestment effects offset for rate changes.

### Q168 — Reducing the Effect of Non-Parallel Shifts [QD-20]

**Context (QD3):**
Strategy 2 (immunizing a single liability).

**Question:**
The effects of a non-parallel shift in the yield curve on Strategy 2 can be reduced by:

A. minimizing the convexity of the bond portfolio.
B. maximizing the cash flow yield of the bond portfolio.
C. minimizing the difference between liability duration and bond portfolio duration.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Minimize the portfolio's convexity.** This minimizes dispersion and structural risk.
- **B is wrong.** Maximizing cash flow yield doesn't reduce structural risk.
- **C is wrong.** Duration gap is about parallel shifts, not twists.

**Key Principle:**
> Single liability: initial MV ≥ PV of liability, Macaulay duration = horizon, minimize convexity.

### Q169 — Most Efficient Way to Mimic the Aggregate Index [QD-21]

**Context (QD3):**
School District funds are invested in the Bloomberg Barclays US Aggregate Index.

**Question:**
Hui’s response to Doug’s question about the most efficient portfolio management strategy should be:

A. full replication.
B. active management.
C. an enhanced indexing strategy.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Full replication is costly for a broad bond index.
- **B is wrong.** Active management isn't about mimicking the index.
- **C is correct.** **Enhanced indexing.** Fewer than all constituents but matching primary risk factors is more efficient than full replication.

**Key Principle:**
> Broad bond indexes: enhanced indexing/stratified sampling instead of full replication.

### Q170 — Structure Providing Diversification Over Time and Liquidity [QD-22]

**Context (QD3):**
Options: bullet, barbell, laddered.

**Question:**
Which portfolio structure should Hui recommend that would satisfy the school board member’s preference?

A. Bullet portfolio
B. Barbell portfolio
C. Laddered portfolio

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A bullet concentrates maturities around the horizon.
- **B is wrong.** A barbell has only short and long maturities, with poor liquidity management.
- **C is correct.** **Laddered.** Maturities are spread out, so reinvestment and price risks are balanced and a bond is always near maturity for liquidity.

**Key Principle:**
> Ladder = diversification over time + liquidity.

# QD4 — Chaopraya / Schuylkill (Immunizing a College Fund)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QD4*

### Q171 — Selecting a Portfolio to Immunize a Single $300,000 Outflow [QD-23] (Essay)

**Context (QD4):**
PV of the outflow = $234,535; horizon 10 years.
| | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Market value | $235,727 | $233,428 | $235,306 |
| Cash flow yield | 2.504% | 2.506% | 2.502% |
| Macaulay duration | 9.998 | 10.002 | 9.503 |
| Convexity | 119.055 | 121.498 | 108.091 |

**Question:**
Recommend the best portfolio and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Portfolio A.** Three criteria for a single outflow:
1. **Market value ≥ PV of liability** ($234,535): A ($235,727) and C ($235,306) pass; B ($233,428) fails.
2. **Macaulay duration = horizon (10 yrs):** A (9.998) matches; C (9.503) is furthest.
3. **Minimize convexity:** of the remaining A and B, A has lower convexity (119.055 vs 121.498), reducing structural risk. (C's lower convexity doesn't matter since its duration misses.)

Default risk is ignored because the portfolios hold government bonds.

**Key Principle:**
