> Single liability: MV ≥ PV, Macaulay duration = horizon, lowest convexity.

### Q172 — Selecting a Portfolio to Immunize Four Annual Payments [QD-24] (Essay)

**Context (QD4):**
Outflow portfolio: PV $230,372, money duration $2,609,700, convexity 135.142.
| | P1 | P2 | P3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Market value | $245,178 | $248,230 | $251,337 |
| Cash flow yield | 2.521% | 2.520% | 2.516% |
| Money duration | 2,609,981 | 2,609,442 | 2,609,707 |
| Convexity | 147.640 | 139.851 | 132.865 |

**Question:**
Determine the most appropriate immunization portfolio and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Portfolio 2.**
- **Money duration:** all three closely match the outflow portfolio's $2,609,700.
- **Convexity:** must be ≥ the outflow's 135.142 but as low as possible. P3 (132.865) is below it, so it fails; P1 (147.640) and P2 (139.851) pass, and P2 is lower, minimizing dispersion and structural risk.

**Key Principle:**
> Multiple liabilities: match money duration; asset convexity ≥ liability convexity but as low as possible.

### Q173 — Effectiveness of Immunization via Duration Gaps [QD-25] (Essay)

**Context (QD4):**
Scenario results (immunizing vs outflow portfolio):
| | ΔMV imm. | ΔMV outflow | Diff | ΔBPV diff |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Up parallel | −6,410 | −6,427 | 18 | −1 |
| Down parallel | 6,626 | 6,622 | 4 | 1 |
| Steepening | −1,912 | 347 | −2,259 | −3 |
| Flattening | 1,966 | −343 | 2,309 | 3 |

**Question:**
Discuss the effectiveness of the immunization strategy in terms of duration gaps.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

- **Parallel shifts:** works well. Money durations are closely matched, so market value differences are small and the duration gaps (ΔBPV differences of −1 and +1) are small.
- **Twists (steepening/flattening):** works poorly. Market values diverge substantially (differences of −2,259 and +2,309) and the duration gaps are large relative to parallel shifts (−3 and +3). This is structural risk from non-parallel shifts.

**Flag:** Exhibit 3's 'Difference' for the upward parallel shift ΔMV shows 18, but −6,410 − (−6,427) = 17; the other three differences compute correctly.

**Key Principle:**
> Money-duration matching handles parallel shifts; twists expose structural risk.

# QD5 — Abiquia (Futures Overlay, Indexing, Synthetic Replication)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QD5*

### Q174 — Futures Position to Raise Surplus When Rates Are Expected to Fall [QD-26]

**Context (QD5):**
Assets $217.3m, BPV $243,376; liabilities $206.8m, BPV $299,860. Futures BPV $102.30 per contract. Expects rates to fall; surplus (217.3/206.8 − 1 = 5.1%) exceeds her 4% threshold.

**Question:**
The most appropriate action given Puhuyesva’s views on interest rates and the information in Exhibit 1 would be to buy:

A. 492 contracts.
B. 552 contracts.
C. 614 contracts.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 492 leaves asset BPV below liability BPV; the surplus would fall if rates fall.
- **B is wrong.** 552 fully immunizes the surplus; it would neither rise nor fall.
- **C is correct.** **614 contracts.** Contracts to fully close the gap: N_f = (BPV_L − BPV_A) / BPV_f = (299,860 − 243,376) / 102.30 = +552 (buy). Because she expects rates to fall and the surplus exceeds 4%, she wants assets' duration above liabilities', so she buys **more than 552**; 614 is the only choice above 552.

**Flag:** The key doesn't derive 614 (only that it must exceed 552). I can't derive 614 from the data given, so I only know it's the one choice above 552.

**Key Principle:**
> N_f = (BPV_L − BPV_A)/BPV_f closes the gap; over-hedge past it to bet on a rate view.

### Q175 — Index Choice to Avoid Credit Quality Deterioration [QD-27]

**Context (QD5):**
DS and RFS are value weighted; BSCA is equally weighted. Credit mixes are essentially the same.

**Question:**
If Puhuyesva’s focus is avoiding credit quality deterioration, which of the South American debt indexes should most likely be chosen on the basis of the information in Exhibit 2?

A. DS
B. RFS
C. BSCA

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** DS is value weighted.
- **B is wrong.** RFS is value weighted.
- **C is correct.** **BSCA.** Value-weighted indexes tilt toward issuers with the most debt, and leverage and creditworthiness are negatively correlated, so value-weighted indexes are more exposed to credit deterioration.

**Key Principle:**
> Value-weighted bond index → more weight to the biggest borrowers.

### Q176 — Valuation of Fixed-Income vs Equity Portfolios [QD-28]

**Context (QD5):**
Puhuyesva: fixed-income valuation models are more reliable, so it's easier to value a bond portfolio than an equity portfolio; focus on minimizing tracking risk.

**Question:**
Is Puhuyesva most likely correct in her comments to Honanie regarding South American debt indexes?

A. Yes
B. No, she is incorrect regarding valuation
C. No, she is incorrect regarding tracking risk

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Her valuation statement is wrong.
- **B is correct.** **No, incorrect on valuation.** Equities trade frequently so market values exist; many bonds are illiquid and need estimates such as matrix pricing.
- **C is wrong.** Tracking error is a good measure of how well a portfolio mimics its benchmark.

**Key Principle:**
> Bonds trade infrequently → pricing difficult (matrix pricing).

### Q177 — Synthetic Replication of an Index Without Exchange-Traded Derivatives [QD-29]

**Context (QD5):**
Honanie: full replication too expensive; only options are matching risk characteristics or pooled funds; synthetic is impossible with no exchange-traded derivatives. Portfolio size 6% × $217.3m ≈ $13.0m.

**Question:**
Are Honanie’s comments to Puhuyesva regarding replicating a South American debt index most likely correct?

A. Yes
B. No, he is incorrect regarding full replication
C. No, he is incorrect regarding synthetic strategies

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** His synthetic claim is wrong.
- **B is wrong.** He is right that full replication is prohibitively costly for a ~$13.0m portfolio.
- **C is correct.** **No, incorrect on synthetic strategies.** An active futures market isn't needed; a total return swap can be arranged over the counter.

**Key Principle:**
> TRS gives synthetic index exposure OTC.

# QD6 — Cávado (Futures Overlay, Immunization, Retiring Debt)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QD6*

### Q178 — Rate View Implied by an Under-Hedged Short Futures Position [QD-30]

**Context (QD6):**
Liabilities BPV €59,598; assets BPV €91,632; futures BPV €97.40; the fund is short 254 contracts.

**Question:**
Given the futures position entered into by the pension fund, Ruelas most likely believes interest rates will:

A. fall.
B. remain the same.
C. rise.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Fall.** Full hedge: N_f = (59,598 − 91,632)/97.40 = −328.9, i.e. short about 329. Being short only 254 leaves assets longer in duration than liabilities, which benefits if rates fall.
- **B is wrong.** If he expected no change, he would fully hedge.
- **C is wrong.** If he expected rates to rise, he would over-hedge (short more).

**Key Principle:**
> Under-hedged (asset duration > liability) = bet that rates fall; over-hedged = bet that rates rise.

### Q179 — Risk Virtually Eliminated by the Choice of Derivatives [QD-31]

**Context (QD6):**
Overlay uses futures on euro German government bonds (or swaps).

**Question:**
In her response to Ruelas regarding risks, Maestre is most likely referring to:

A. model risk.
B. spread risk.
C. counterparty credit risk.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Model risk is implicit in managing a DB plan (Type IV liabilities).
- **B is wrong.** Spread risk is hard to eliminate because the assets are investment- and speculative-grade corporates, while derivatives trade on government securities.
- **C is correct.** **Counterparty credit risk.** Essentially absent in exchange-traded derivatives and can be essentially eliminated in OTC swaps through a Credit Support Annex.

**Key Principle:**
> Counterparty credit risk: exchange-traded or CSA-collateralized.

### Q180 — Least Likely Bond Indexing Strategy for a Small Portfolio [QD-32]

**Context (QD6):**
Small portfolio; bonds trade in blocks over USD1 million.

**Question:**
What bond indexing strategy would Maestre least likely recommend?

A. An index mutual fund
B. A stratified sampling approach
C. A synthetic strategy using a total return swap

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** An index mutual fund is easy to implement and likely cheaper.
- **B is correct.** **Stratified sampling.** Building a bond index fund directly is difficult for a small portfolio because bonds trade in large blocks.
- **C is wrong.** A TRS plus cash is easier and cheaper than stratified sampling.

**Key Principle:**
> Small portfolios: pooled funds or TRS beat direct bond sampling.

### Q181 — Selecting an Immunizing Portfolio for a 6.5-Year Liability [QD-33]

**Context (QD6):**
| | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cash flow yield | 2.18% | 2.14% | 2.16% |
| Macaulay duration | 6.50 | 6.52 | 6.47 |
| Convexity | 102.64 | 86.16 | 129.43 |

**Question:**
Which of the portfolios described in Exhibit 2 would most likely be recommended by Maestre?

A. Portfolio A
B. Portfolio B
C. Portfolio C

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A's convexity is substantially larger than B's.
- **B is correct.** **Portfolio B.** Yields and durations are essentially equal to the 6.5-year horizon, so the decision is convexity: the lowest convexity minimizes dispersion, making the portfolio most like the zero-coupon bond.
- **C is wrong.** C's convexity is substantially larger than B's.

**Key Principle:**
> Equal durations: choose the lowest convexity for a single liability.

### Q182 — Retiring the Cávado Bond [QD-34]

**Context (QD6):**
€22m bond issue held by two funds willing to sell at a small premium over market price.

**Question:**
Which of the following strategies would Maestre most likely recommend for retiring the Cávado bond?

A. Bond tender offer
B. Duration matching with corporate bonds
C. Cash flow matching with government bonds

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Bond tender offer.** The bonds can likely be bought at a small premium, so no need for a complicated defeasance.
- **B is wrong.** Duration matching is more complicated than necessary.
- **C is wrong.** Cash flow matching is more complicated than necessary.

**Key Principle:**
> If holders will sell at a small premium, a tender is simplest.

# QD7 — Endicott (Defeasance, Immunization, Futures)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QD7*

### Q183 — Most Cost-Efficient Proposal to Retire the Debt [QD-35] (Essay)

**Context (QD7):**
P1: Treasuries matching coupons and principal (accounting defeasance). P2: high-quality corporate bonds matched on duration + interest rate derivatives to keep duration on target. P3: tender offer at a spread consistent with A-rated corporates.

**Question:**
Determine the proposal that best meets Lennar's objective (most cost-efficient) and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Proposal 2.** It is a duration-matching strategy using corporate bonds, which likely cost less than Proposal 1's lower-yielding Treasuries that must match each coupon and principal payment. Proposal 3 (tender) would likely be costly: bonds would be repurchased at a spread consistent with A-rated bonds, likely tighter than the spread of Endicott's BBB debt.

**Key Principle:**
> Duration matching with corporates is usually cheaper than cash-flow matching with Treasuries.

### Q184 — Portfolio to Immunize Endicott's Debt [QD-36] (Essay)

**Context (QD7):**
Liabilities: MV $455m, modified duration 3.81, convexity 39.5, BPV $173,355.
| | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Modified duration | 3.80 | 3.68 | 3.68 |
| Convexity | 39.65 | 37.95 | 40.25 |
| BPV | $188,100 | $182,655 | $182,160 |

**Question:**
Determine the portfolio that best immunizes the liabilities and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Portfolio C.** Its BPV ($182,160) is closest to the liabilities' $173,355, and its convexity (40.25) exceeds the liabilities' 39.5.
- A: BPV ($188,100) is furthest from the liabilities'.
- B: convexity (37.95) is below the liabilities'; for multiple liabilities, asset convexity must exceed the liabilities'.

**Key Principle:**
> Multiple liabilities: BPV (money duration) close, asset convexity > liability convexity.

### Q185 — Futures Needed to Close the Money Duration Gap [QD-37] (Essay)

**Context (QD7):**
Liability BPV $173,355; Portfolio A BPV $188,100. CTD bond BPV $43.25 per $100,000 par; conversion factor 0.74.

**Question:**
Calculate the number of five-year Treasury note futures to sell to close the gap with Portfolio A.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Futures BPV = BPV_CTD / CF = 43.25 / 0.74 = $58.45.
N_f = (BPV_L − BPV_A) / BPV_f = (173,355 − 188,100) / 58.45 = **−252** (sell 252 contracts).

**Key Principle:**
> Futures BPV = CTD BPV ÷ conversion factor; N_f = (BPV_L − BPV_A)/BPV_f.

# QD8 — AL Capital / Brown (LDI, Immunization, Indexing)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QD8*

### Q186 — Statements About Liability-Driven Investing [QD-38]

**Context (QD8):**
S1: immunization minimizes the variance of the realized return over a known horizon. S2: single-liability immunization is achieved by matching modified duration to the horizon.

**Question:**
Are both of Brown’s statements regarding liability-driven investing most likely correct?

A. Yes
B. No, only Statement 1 is correct
C. No, only Statement 2 is correct

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Statement 2 is wrong (Macaulay, not modified).
- **B is correct.** **Only Statement 1 is correct.** Single-liability immunization matches the portfolio's **Macaulay** duration, not modified duration, to the horizon.
- **C is wrong.** Statement 1 is correct and Statement 2 is wrong.

**Key Principle:**
> Immunization of a single liability: Macaulay duration = horizon.

### Q187 — Portfolio to Immunize a 7-Year Liability [QD-39]

**Context (QD8):**
| | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cash flow yield | 5.10% | 5.50% | 5.70% |
| Avg time to maturity | 9.0 | 10.5 | 7.0 |
| Macaulay duration | 7.0 | 7.3 | 6.0 |
| Convexity | 34.0 | 14.0 | 58.0 |

**Question:**
Which portfolio would best immunize TM’s single liability?

A. Portfolio A
B. Portfolio B
C. Portfolio C

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Portfolio A.** Macaulay duration of 7.0 equals the horizon.
- **B is wrong.** Macaulay duration is 7.3.
- **C is wrong.** Macaulay duration is 6.0 (average time to maturity of 7.0 is not the criterion).

**Key Principle:**
> Match Macaulay duration, not average maturity.

### Q188 — Lowest Structural Risk [QD-40]

**Context (QD8):**
Same exhibit; assume all meet other immunization conditions.

**Question:**
Assuming all three portfolios met the other conditions for immunizing a single liability, which portfolio would most likely exhibit the lowest structural risk?

A. Portfolio A
B. Portfolio B
C. Portfolio C

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A's convexity (34.0) is higher than B's.
- **B is correct.** **Portfolio B.** Structural risk falls as portfolio convexity (cash flow dispersion) falls; B has the lowest convexity (14.0).
- **C is wrong.** C has the highest convexity (58.0).

**Key Principle:**
> Lowest convexity for a given duration = lowest structural risk.

### Q189 — Strategy Meeting Cost-Effective, Low-Tracking-Error Guidelines [QD-41]

**Context (QD8):**
Broadly diversified US bond index benchmark; most cost-effective, low tracking error.

**Question:**
Which of the following strategies would best meet both of Green’s guidelines for the TM pension plan’s fixed-income portfolio?

A. Pure indexing
B. Enhanced indexing
C. Active management

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Pure indexing (full replication) is neither feasible nor cost-effective for broad bond indexes.
- **B is correct.** **Enhanced indexing.** Fewer securities than the index but matches primary risk factors; more efficient than full replication.
- **C is wrong.** Active management takes risk-factor positions that deviate from the index.

**Key Principle:**
> Enhanced indexing = low tracking error at lower cost.

# QD9 — Fair Haven (DB Plan Hedging, Overlay, Immunization)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QD9*

### Q190 — Immunizing a DB Plan Against a Bear Steepener [QD-42]

**Context (QD9):**
Assets: MV $517.342m; modified duration 12.66; convexity 21.40; dispersion 6.48; PV01 654,281. Liabilities: PBO $500m; duration 13.10; convexity 22.51; dispersion 6.70; PV01 654,276. Forecast: bear steepener (long rates rise faster than short).

**Question:**
Based on the data in Exhibit 1, will the client discussed most likely be able to immunize its DB plan given the interest rate scenario described by Silver?

A. Yes
B. No, because of the differences in money duration
C. No, because of the differences in convexity and dispersion

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The convexity/dispersion differences are unfavorable.
- **B is wrong.** Money durations are equal.
- **C is correct.** **No, because of convexity and dispersion.** Money durations are essentially equal (PV01 654,281 vs 654,276), so parallel shifts are matched. But assets have lower convexity and dispersion than the liabilities, so in a bear steepener they will underperform.

**Flag:** The key states 517,342,000 × 12.66 = 500,000,000 × 13.10 = 6,548,381,000. Actual: 6,549,549,720 and 6,550,000,000 (close but not identical). The conclusion (essentially equal) is unaffected.

**Key Principle:**
> Equal money duration handles parallel shifts; assets need convexity/dispersion ≥ liabilities for twists.

### Q191 — Hedging Risk From Discount-Rate Quality Mismatch [QD-43]

**Context (QD9):**
Liability discount rates use high-quality bond yields; solutions use lower-quality corporates; Treasury futures/swaps create the opposite mismatch.

**Question:**
The risk that Silver describes to Shrewsbury in hedging the liabilities is most likely:

A. model risk.
B. spread risk.
C. liquidity risk.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Model risk is about incorrect assumptions or approximations.
- **B is correct.** **Spread risk.** The spread between the liability discount rate and the hedge instruments' yields can change.
- **C is wrong.** Liquidity risk comes from margin calls and benefit payments.

**Key Principle:**
> Spread risk: asset/liability/hedge yields don't move equally.

### Q192 — Contingent Strategy for a Plan Expecting Rates to Rise [QD-44]

**Context (QD9):**
Underfunded DB clients with hedge ratios well below 100% expect rates to rise; they want a costless derivative position that protects if rates fall further and raises the hedge ratio if rates rise.

**Question:**
What contingent strategies would Shrewsbury’s DB clients most likely enter into under the scenario he outlines?

A. Short a receiver swap
B. Long a payer swaption, short a receiver swaption
C. Long a receiver swaption, short a payer swaption

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A receiver swap isn't contingent.
- **B is wrong.** That is the reverse of the correct combination.
- **C is correct.** **Long a receiver swaption, short a payer swaption.** The receiver swaption protects if rates fall; the sold payer swaption finances it, and if rates rise the plan is put a pay-fixed swap, increasing duration toward the hedge target.

**Key Principle:**
> Costless collar-like: long receiver swaption / short payer swaption.

### Q193 — Instrument for Passive Interest-Rate and Credit Exposure [QD-45]

**Context (QD9):**
Wants to hedge interest rate and credit components; corporate bond index reference; synthetic secured financing structure.

**Question:**
Shrewsbury would most likely choose which instrument to achieve his alternate investment objective?

A. Creation units
B. Total return swap
C. Interest rate swap

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Creation units are large blocks of ETF shares traded against baskets; they don't establish a passive bond position.
- **B is correct.** **Total return swap.** An OTC swap exchanging cash flows on a reference corporate bond index.
- **C is wrong.** An interest rate swap gives interest rate exposure only; credit exposure is also needed.

**Key Principle:**
> TRS on a corporate bond index = passive rate + credit exposure.

# Standalone — LDI, Immunization, Bond Indexing

### Q194 — Derivatives Overlay for Duration Drift [QD-46]

**Context:**
Liabilities: modified duration 8.15, BPV $250,000. Assets: 8.35, BPV $265,000. Drift arose from widening corporate–government spreads.

**Question:**
A bond portfolio manager uses government bonds to immunize a portfolio of corporate debt liabilities. Currently, the corporate debt liabilities have a modified duration of 8.15, and basis point value (BPV) of $250,000. The asset portfolio has a modified duration of 8.35, and BPV of $265,000. The duration drift has arisen because of a widening spread between corporate and government bond yields due to a decline in interest rates. The institutional investor wants the portfolio manager ​​​​​​to use a derivatives overlay strategy to manage the duration drift. The portfolio manager should:

A. do nothing because the duration drift represents credit spread risk.
B. go short on bond futures because the money duration of assets is greater than the money duration of liabilities.
C. go long on bond futures because the modified duration of liabilities is lower than the modified duration of assets.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The investor wants to manage the drift, and a futures overlay can reduce the gap.
- **B is correct.** **Short bond futures.** Asset BPV ($265,000) exceeds liability BPV ($250,000); money duration is the better gap measure since market values differ.
- **C is wrong.** Liabilities have lower duration and BPV than assets; reducing the gap requires shorting.

**Key Principle:**
> Asset money duration > liability → short futures; < → long.

### Q195 — Characteristics of a Single-Liability Immunizing Portfolio [QD-47]

**Question:**
A bond portfolio structured to immunize a single liability must:

A. maximize the portfolio convexity statistic.
B. have a Macaulay duration that matches the liability’s due date.
C. have an initial market value that is less than the present value of the liability.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** It minimizes, not maximizes, convexity.
- **B is correct.** **Macaulay duration matches the liability's due date.**
- **C is wrong.** Initial market value must equal or exceed the PV of the liability.

**Key Principle:**
> Single liability: MV ≥ PV, Macaulay duration = horizon, minimum convexity.

### Q196 — Portfolio for a 5-Year Liability With PV $550,250 [QD-48]

**Context:**
| | P1 | P2 | P3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Market value | $549,500 | $551,800 | $552,970 |
| Macaulay duration | 4.980 | 5.040 | 5.310 |
| Convexity | 84.170 | 78.550 | 79.850 |

**Question:**
A fixed-income investment advisor must recommend a portfolio to a client that will immunize today a liability due in 5-years with a present value of $550,250. The advisor considers three portfolios consisting of non-callable, fixed-rate government bonds which are considered default risk free. The relevant data on the three portfolios is as follows:

|  | Portfolio 1 | Portfolio 2 | Portfolio 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Market value | $549,500 | $551,800 | $552,970 |
| Macaulay duration | 4.980 | 5.040 | 5.310 |
| Convexity | 84.170 | 78.550 | 79.850 |

The investment advisor is most likely to recommend:

A. Portfolio 1.
B. Portfolio 2.
C. Portfolio 3.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** MV ($549,500) is below the PV of the liability, and convexity is the highest.
- **B is correct.** **Portfolio 2.** MV above the PV, Macaulay duration close to 5, and the lowest convexity (78.550).
- **C is wrong.** Macaulay duration (5.310) is not near 5, and its convexity is higher than P2's.

**Key Principle:**
> Single liability: all three criteria.

### Q197 — Portfolio for Multiple Future Payments [QD-49]

**Context:**
Liabilities: money duration $1,540,500; convexity 93.655.
| | P1 | P2 | P3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cash flow yield | 2.540% | 2.560% | 2.554% |
| Money duration | $1,541,350 | $1,542,250 | $1,541,900 |
| Convexity | 92.530 | 98.732 | 95.125 |

**Question:**
A client has asked a fixed-income investment advisor to recommend a portfolio that will immunize a series of future cash flow payments with a money duration of $1,540,500 and convexity of 93.655. The advisor considers the following three portfolios to immunize these future payments:

|  | Portfolio 1 | Portfolio 2 | Portfolio 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cash flow yield | 2.540% | 2.560% | 2.554% |
| Money duration | $1,541,350 | $1,542,250 | $1,541,900 |
| Convexity | 92.530 | 98.732 | 95.125 |

Which portfolio best achieves this immunization objective?

A. Portfolio 1
B. Portfolio 2
C. Portfolio 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** P1's convexity (92.530) is below the liabilities'.
- **B is wrong.** P2's convexity (98.732) is higher than P3's.
- **C is correct.** **Portfolio 3.** Money durations are all close; convexity must be ≥ 93.655 and as low as possible: P3 (95.125) beats P2 (98.732).

**Key Principle:**
> Multiple liabilities: lowest convexity above the liabilities'.

### Q198 — Duration Measure for Multiple Liabilities [QD-50]

**Context:**
Market values and cash flow yields of portfolio and liabilities differ.

**Question:**
An active portfolio manager is seeking to immunize multiple liabilities using a portfolio of coupon-paying government bonds. If the market value and cash flow yields of the portfolio and the liabilities are not the same, the most appropriate duration measure to use when immunizing the liabilities is:

A. money duration.
B. modified duration.
C. Macaulay duration.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Money duration** (modified duration × market value).
- **B is wrong.** Modified duration ignores market value differences.
- **C is wrong.** Macaulay duration isn't the relevant measure here.

**Key Principle:**
> Multiple liabilities → match money duration (BPV).

### Q199 — Immunizing Multiple Liabilities When All Portfolios Exceed Liability Convexity [QD-51]

**Question:**
An active portfolio manager wants to immunize multiple liabilities using coupon-paying government bonds. The cash flow yields and the market value of the assets and liabilities are not the same. If the convexity of all the immunizing portfolios is greater than that of the liabilities, the portfolio manager will most likely prefer to immunize the liabilities by:

A. maximizing the immunizing portfolio's convexity.
B. matching the immunizing portfolio's money duration with the money duration of the liabilities.
C. matching the Macaulay duration of the immunizing portfolio with the Macaulay duration of the liabilities.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Maximizing convexity isn't the goal; choose the lowest convexity above the liabilities'.
- **B is correct.** **Match money duration with the liabilities'.** (Also choose the lowest convexity above the liabilities'.)
- **C is wrong.** Money durations, not Macaulay durations, should be matched.

**Key Principle:**
> Multiple liabilities: money duration match, minimum qualifying convexity.

### Q200 — Name for Non-Parallel Shift / Twist Risk [QD-52]

**Question:**
In fixed-income immunization strategies, the risk associated with non-parallel shifts and twists to the yield curve is best referred to as:

A. model risk.
B. spread risk.
C. structural risk.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Model risk: assumptions and approximations being wrong.
- **B is wrong.** Spread risk: asset, liability and hedge yields don't change equally.
- **C is correct.** **Structural risk.** Arises from portfolio design; twists/non-parallel shifts change the cash flow yield.

**Key Principle:**
> Structural risk = non-parallel shifts and twists.

### Q201 — Risk of Rebalancing With Bonds for Parallel Shifts [QD-53]

**Context:**
Multiple corporate liabilities; on-the-run government bonds bought on an exchange; rebalancing with bonds, not derivatives.

**Question:**
An active fixed-income portfolio manager implements a strategy to immunize multiple corporate liabilities using a portfolio of on-the-run government bonds bought through ​​​​​​an established bond exchange. The portfolio will be rebalanced for parallel shifts in the yield curve by buying or selling bonds rather than by using derivatives. The strategy is most likely exposed to:

A. model risk.
B. asset liquidity risk.
C. counterparty credit risk.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Model risk.** The strategy relies on assumptions and approximations (parallel shifts).
- **B is wrong.** On-the-run government bonds are liquid.
- **C is wrong.** No derivative overlay is used.

**Key Principle:**
> Assuming parallel shifts → model risk.

### Q202 — When to Match Macaulay Duration to Minimize Transaction Costs [QD-54]

**Context:**
Single liability; minimize transaction costs.

**Question:**
A client requests her portfolio manager to immunize a single liability and minimize transaction costs relating to the immunization process. The manager should match the Macaulay duration of the portfolio with that of the liability if the:

A. portfolio consists of only coupon-paying government bonds.
B. portfolio consists of zero-coupon bonds with the same maturity.
C. money duration of the portfolio matches the money duration of the liability.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A coupon bond portfolio must be regularly rebalanced as duration changes.
- **B is correct.** **Portfolio of zero-coupon bonds with the same maturity.** A zero's Macaulay duration equals its maturity and needs no rebalancing.
- **C is wrong.** Money duration applies to multiple liabilities.

**Key Principle:**
> Zeros held to maturity need no rebalancing.

### Q203 — Single-Liability Immunizing Portfolio Characteristics [QD-55]

**Question:**
A bond portfolio structured to immunize a single liability is one that most likely:

A. maximizes the portfolio convexity statistic.
B. has a portfolio Macaulay duration that matches the liability’s due date.
C. has an initial market value that is less than the present value of the liability.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** It minimizes convexity.
- **B is correct.** **Macaulay duration matches the due date.**
- **C is wrong.** Initial market value ≥ PV of the liability.

**Key Principle:**
> MV ≥ PV; Macaulay = horizon; minimize convexity.

### Q204 — Strategy Name: Representative Constituents at Lower Cost [QD-56]

**Question:**
A bond portfolio manager seeks to match returns of a bond market index. The manager aims to replicate the index performance efficiently by acquiring the representative index constituents and minimizing transaction costs. This strategy is best referred to as:

A. pure indexing.
B. full replication.
C. enhanced indexing.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Pure indexing = full replication.
- **B is wrong.** Full replication is the same as pure indexing.
- **C is correct.** **Enhanced indexing.** Fewer securities but matches primary risk factors.

**Key Principle:**
> Pure indexing = full replication; enhanced indexing = representative sample matching risk factors.

### Q205 — Why Choose ETFs Over Total Return Swaps [QD-57]

**Question:**
An investor is exploring alternatives to direct investing in fixed income securities. After extensive analysis, the investor decides to invest in ETFs instead of total returns swaps because ETFs:

A. offer greater liquidity.
B. require less initial cash outlay.
C. offer exposure to assets that are difficult to access directly.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Greater liquidity.**
- **B is wrong.** TRS, not ETFs, need less initial cash.
- **C is wrong.** TRS, not ETFs, offer exposure to hard-to-access assets (e.g., some high yield, commercial loans).

**Key Principle:**
> ETF: liquidity. TRS: low cash outlay, hard-to-access exposure.

### Q206 — Benchmark for a Highly Risk-Averse, Long-Horizon Fund [QD-58]

**Context:**
B1: investment-grade corporate, duration 5.1. B2: high-yield, 9.2. B3: sovereign, 8.5.

**Question:**
An analyst gathers the following information about fixed-income benchmarks:

| Benchmark | Composition | Duration |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Investment grade corporate bond index | 5.1 |
| 2 | High yield corporate bond index | 9.2 |
| 3 | Sovereign bond index | 8.5 |

For a highly risk-averse pension fund that has a long-term investment horizon, which benchmark is the most appropriate?

A. Benchmark 1
B. Benchmark 2
C. Benchmark 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Investment grade but shorter duration.
- **B is wrong.** Longest duration but low credit quality.
- **C is correct.** **Benchmark 3.** High credit quality and a long duration.

**Key Principle:**
> Benchmark must fit risk tolerance (credit) and horizon (duration).

# QE1 — Sydney Fund (Duration, Convexity, Curve Trades, Butterfly)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QE1*

### Q207 — Highest Modified Duration Among Bullet, Barbell and Equal-Weight Portfolios [QE-1]

**Context (QE1):**
| Tenor | Coupon | Yield | Price | Mod. dur. | Convexity |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | 5.75% | 0.28% | 110.90 | 1.922 | 4.9 |
| 4.5y | 3.25% | 0.55% | 111.98 | 4.241 | 22.1 |
| 9y | 2.50% | 1.10% | 111.97 | 8.175 | 85.2 |
Bullet = 4.5y only. Barbell = equal 2y and 9y. Equal weights = equal 2y, 4.5y, 9y.

**Question:**
The portfolio alternative with the highest modified duration is the:

A. bullet portfolio.
B. barbell portfolio.
C. equally weighted portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Bullet duration is 4.241, the lowest.
- **B is correct.** **Barbell.** Portfolio duration ≈ weighted average: barbell (1.922 + 8.175)/2 = 5.049; bullet 4.241; equal-weight (1.922 + 4.241 + 8.175)/3 = 4.779.
- **C is wrong.** Equal-weight duration is 4.779.

**Key Principle:**
> Portfolio duration (and convexity) = weighted average of the bonds' values.

### Q208 — Smallest Loss for a +50 bp Yield Shift [QE-2]

**Context (QE1):**
| Tenor | Coupon | Yield | Price | Mod. dur. | Convexity |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | 5.75% | 0.28% | 110.90 | 1.922 | 4.9 |
| 4.5y | 3.25% | 0.55% | 111.98 | 4.241 | 22.1 |
| 9y | 2.50% | 1.10% | 111.97 | 8.175 | 85.2 |
Bullet = 4.5y only. Barbell = equal 2y and 9y. Equal weights = equal 2y, 4.5y, 9y.
Yields rise 50 bps.

**Question:**
The manager estimates that accelerated economic growth in Australia will increase the level of government yields-to-maturity by 50 bps. Under this scenario, which of the three portfolios experiences the smallest decline in market value?

A. Bullet portfolio
B. Barbell portfolio
C. Equally weighted portfolio

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Bullet.** %ΔPV ≈ −ModDur × ΔY + ½ × Convexity × ΔY². Bullet: −4.241(0.005) + 0.5(22.1)(0.005²) = −2.093%. Equal-weight (4.779, 37.4): −2.343%. Barbell (5.049, 45.05): −2.468%.
- **B is wrong.** Barbell has the largest loss (−2.468%): its extra convexity doesn't offset its higher duration at +50 bps.
- **C is wrong.** Equal-weight falls 2.343%.

**Key Principle:**
> %ΔPV ≈ −ModDur × ΔY + ½ × Convexity × ΔY².

### Q209 — Positioning for a Bear Flattening With Swaps [QE-3]

**Context (QE1):**
Bear flattening: short-term rates rise more than long-term rates (long-short spread narrows).

**Question:**
Assume the manager is able to extend her mandate by adding derivatives strategies to the three portfolio alternatives. The best way to position her portfolio to benefit from a bear flattening scenario is to combine a:

A. 2-year receive-fixed Australian dollar (AUD) swap with the same modified duration as the bullet portfolio.
B. 2-year pay-fixed AUD swap with twice the modified duration as the 2-year government bond in the barbell portfolio.
C. 9-year receive-fixed AUD swap with twice the modified duration as the 9-year government bond position in the equally weighted portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A receive-fixed 2-year swap is a synthetic long position that loses when short rates rise.
- **B is correct.** **2-year pay-fixed swap with twice the barbell's 2-year modified duration.** It more than offsets the existing 2-year long, leaving net short 2-year and long 9-year, which gains if short yields rise.
- **C is wrong.** A receive-fixed 9-year swap adds long-maturity duration.

**Key Principle:**
> Bear flattening: short the short end (pay-fixed), be long the long end.

### Q210 — Futures Addition for a Bull Flattening [QE-4]

**Context (QE1):**
3-year and 10-year ASX Treasury bond futures; barbell portfolio. Bull flattening: long-term yields fall more than short-term yields.

**Question:**
In her market research, the manager learns that ASX 3-year and 10-year Treasury bond futures are the most liquid products for investors trading and hedging medium- to long-term Australian dollar (AUD) interest rates. Although neither contract matches the exact characteristics of the cash bonds of her choice, which of the following additions to a barbell portfolio best positions her to gain under a bull flattening scenario?

A. Purchase a 3-year Treasury bond future matching the money duration of the short-term (2-year) position.
B. Sell a 3-year Treasury bond future matching the money duration of the short-term bond position.
C. Purchase a 10-year Treasury bond future matching the money duration of the long-term bond position.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A and B change exposure to short-term rates, not long-term rates.
- **B is wrong.** A and B change exposure to short-term rates, not long-term rates.
- **C is correct.** **Buy a 10-year futures contract matching the long-term position's money duration.** It increases exposure to long-term rates, which fall.

**Key Principle:**
> Bull flattening: add duration at the long end.

### Q211 — Largest Gain for a 25 bp Yield Decline [QE-5]

**Context (QE1):**
| Tenor | Coupon | Yield | Price | Mod. dur. | Convexity |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | 5.75% | 0.28% | 110.90 | 1.922 | 4.9 |
| 4.5y | 3.25% | 0.55% | 111.98 | 4.241 | 22.1 |
| 9y | 2.50% | 1.10% | 111.97 | 8.175 | 85.2 |
Bullet = 4.5y only. Barbell = equal 2y and 9y. Equal weights = equal 2y, 4.5y, 9y.
Yields fall 25 bps.

**Question:**
An economic slowdown is expected to result in a 25 bp decline in Australian yield levels. Which portfolio alternative will experience the largest gain under this scenario?

A. Bullet portfolio
B. Barbell portfolio
C. Equally weighted portfolio

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Bullet gains 1.067%.
- **B is correct.** **Barbell.** Barbell: −5.049(−0.0025) + 0.5(45.05)(0.0025²) = +1.276%. Equal-weight: +1.207%. Bullet: +1.067%.
- **C is wrong.** Equal-weight gains 1.207%.

**Key Principle:**
> Higher duration and convexity → larger gains when yields fall.

### Q212 — Portfolio With Least Convexity [QE-6]

**Context (QE1):**
| Tenor | Coupon | Yield | Price | Mod. dur. | Convexity |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | 5.75% | 0.28% | 110.90 | 1.922 | 4.9 |
| 4.5y | 3.25% | 0.55% | 111.98 | 4.241 | 22.1 |
| 9y | 2.50% | 1.10% | 111.97 | 8.175 | 85.2 |
Bullet = 4.5y only. Barbell = equal 2y and 9y. Equal weights = equal 2y, 4.5y, 9y.

**Question:**
The portfolio alternative with the least exposure to convexity is the:

A. bullet portfolio.
B. barbell portfolio.
C. equally weighted portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Bullet.** Convexity 22.1, vs barbell (4.9 + 85.2)/2 = 45.05 and equal-weight (4.9 + 22.1 + 85.2)/3 = 37.4.
- **B is wrong.** Barbell convexity is 45.05.
- **C is wrong.** Equal-weight convexity is 37.4.

**Key Principle:**
> Barbells have more convexity than bullets of equal duration.

### Q213 — Current Butterfly Spread [QE-7]

**Context (QE1):**
2y yield 0.28%, 4.5y yield 0.55%, 9y yield 1.10%.

**Question:**
The current butterfly spread for the Australian government yield curve based upon the manager’s portfolio choices is:

A. 83 bps.
B. 28 bps.
C. −28 bps.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 83 bps isn't what the formula gives.
- **B is wrong.** Sign is wrong.
- **C is correct.** **−28 bps.** Butterfly = −short yield + 2 × medium yield − long yield = −0.28% + 1.10% − 1.10% = −0.28%.

**Key Principle:**
> Butterfly spread = −short + 2 × medium − long.

### Q214 — Positive Butterfly View [QE-8]

**Context (QE1):**
Positive butterfly: expected fall in the butterfly spread (short and long yields rise, medium yield falls).

**Question:**
If the manager has a positive butterfly view on Australian government yields-to-maturity, the best portfolio position strategy to pursue is to:

A. purchase the bullet portfolio and sell the barbell portfolio.
B. sell the bullet portfolio and buy the barbell portfolio.
C. purchase the equally weighted portfolio and sell the barbell portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Buy the bullet, sell the barbell.** Long the medium-term, short the short- and long-term.
- **B is wrong.** That is the negative-butterfly position.
- **C is wrong.** Long equal-weight and short barbell leaves bullet exposure but is not the key's answer.

**Flag:** The key says option C leaves the investor with 'bullet portfolio exposure' without saying why A is still better; I haven't added my own reasoning.

**Key Principle:**
> Positive butterfly view: long bullet, short barbell.

# Standalone — Static Curve Strategies and Embedded Options

### Q215 — Least Attractive Static-Curve Strategy [QE-9]

**Context:**
Benchmark: US Treasury Index, mod. duration 7.25, avg maturity 8.5y; upward-sloping curve expected unchanged.

**Question:**
An analyst manages an active fixed-income fund that is benchmarked to the Bloomberg Barclays US Treasury Index. This index of US government bonds currently has a modified portfolio duration of 7.25 and an average maturity of 8.5 years. The yield curve is upward-sloping and expected to remain unchanged. Which of the following is the least attractive portfolio positioning strategy in a static curve environment?

A. Purchasing a 10-year zero-coupon bond with a yield of 2% and a price of 82.035
B. Entering a pay-fixed, 30-year USD interest rate swap
C. Purchasing a 20-year Treasury and financing it in the repo market

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A 10-year zero at 2% (price 82.035) extends duration: 10/1.02 = 9.80 vs 7.25.
- **B is correct.** **Pay-fixed 30-year swap.** A short-duration position that earns negative carry in an upward-sloping curve (pays fixed above the floating rate received).
- **C is wrong.** Buying a 20-year bond financed in repo is leverage, earning carry as the curve is upward sloping.

**Key Principle:**
> Static curve: extend duration or lever to earn carry/rolldown.

### Q216 — Bonds With Embedded Options When Yields Rise [QE-10]

**Context:**
Upward parallel shift expected: callable bond (unlikely to be called), putable bond (likely to be put), option-free bond.

**Question:**
An investment manager is considering decreasing portfolio duration versus a benchmark index given her expectations of an upward parallel shift in the yield curve. If she has a choice between a callable bond which is unlikely to be called, a putable bond which is likely to be put, or an option-free bond with otherwise comparable characteristics, the most profitable position would be to:

A. own the callable bond.
B. own the putable bond.
C. own the option-free bond.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The investor is short the call (owned by the issuer).
- **B is correct.** **Own the putable bond.** The investor owns the put, which gains value when yields rise.
- **C is wrong.** The option-free bond underperforms the putable bond.

**Key Principle:**
> Putable bond = long put; callable = short call.

### Q217 — Duration Statistic for an MBS Portfolio [QE-11]

**Context:**
Tracks the Bloomberg Barclays US MBS Index.

**Question:**
An active fixed-income manager holds a portfolio of commercial and residential mortgage-backed securities that tracks the Bloomberg Barclays US Mortgage-Backed Securities Index. Which of the following choices is the most relevant portfolio statistic for evaluating the first-order change in his portfolio’s value for a given change in benchmark yield?

A. Effective duration
B. Macaulay duration
C. Modified duration

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Effective duration.** Measures sensitivity to a benchmark curve shift for bonds with uncertain cash flows.
- **B is wrong.** Macaulay duration is only relevant for option-free bonds.
- **C is wrong.** Modified duration is only relevant for option-free bonds.

**Key Principle:**
> Bonds with embedded options (MBS) → effective duration.

### Q218 — Option Positions for a Curve Steepening [QE-12]

**Context:**
Upward-sloping curve expected to steepen.

**Question:**
An active fund trader seeks to capitalize on an expected steepening of the current upward-sloping yield curve using option-based fixed-income instruments. Which of the following portfolio positioning strategies best positions her to gain if her interest rate view is realized?

A. Sell a 30-year receiver swaption and a 2-year bond put option.
B. Purchase a 30-year receiver swaption and a 2-year bond put option.
C. Purchase a 30-year payer swaption and a 2-year bond call option.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Selling two options caps profit at the premiums.
- **B is wrong.** B positions for a flattening.
- **C is correct.** **Buy a 30-year payer swaption and a 2-year bond call option.** Gains if long yields rise (payer swaption) and short yields fall (call).

**Key Principle:**
> Steepener: short long-end duration, long short-end duration.

# QE2 — KRD Analyst (Key Rate Durations)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QE2*

### Q219 — Active vs Index Performance for a +50 bp Shift [QE-13]

**Context (QE2):**
| Tenor | KRD active | KRD index | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | −0.532 | 0.738 | −1.270 |
| 5y | 0.324 | 1.688 | −1.364 |
| 10y | 5.181 | 2.747 | 2.434 |
| 30y | 1.142 | 2.162 | −1.020 |
| Portfolio | 6.115 | 7.335 | −1.220 |

**Question:**
Which of the following statements is true if yield levels increase by 50 bps?

A. The active portfolio will outperform the index portfolio by approximately 61 bps.
B. The index portfolio will outperform the active portfolio by approximately 61 bps.
C. The index portfolio will outperform the active portfolio by approximately 21 bps.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Active outperforms by about 61 bps.** The sum of key rate durations is effective duration; ΔPV ≈ −EffDur × ΔY: −1.22 × 0.005 = 0.61%. The active portfolio has lower duration, so it falls less when yields rise.
- **B is wrong.** The index has more duration, so it loses more.
- **C is wrong.** 21 bps is not −1.22 × 0.005.

**Key Principle:**
> Sum of KRDs = effective duration; first-order ΔPV = −EffDur × ΔY.

### Q220 — Curve Positioning of the Active Portfolio [QE-14]

**Context (QE2):**
| Tenor | KRD active | KRD index | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | −0.532 | 0.738 | −1.270 |
| 5y | 0.324 | 1.688 | −1.364 |
| 10y | 5.181 | 2.747 | 2.434 |
| 30y | 1.142 | 2.162 | −1.020 |
| Portfolio | 6.115 | 7.335 | −1.220 |

**Question:**
Which of the following statements best characterizes how the active portfolio is positioned for yield curve changes relative to the index portfolio?

A. The active portfolio is positioned to benefit from a bear steepening of the yield curve versus the benchmark portfolio.
B. The active portfolio is positioned to benefit from a positive butterfly movement in the shape of the yield curve versus the index.
C. The active portfolio is positioned to benefit from yield curve flattening versus the index.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Not the pattern in the table.
- **B is correct.** **Positive butterfly.** Active is short duration vs index at 2y, 5y and 30y and long at 10y; it gains if the butterfly spread falls.
- **C is wrong.** Not the pattern in the table.

**Key Principle:**
> Short wings/long belly = positive butterfly (profits when butterfly spread falls).

### Q221 — Derivatives to Offset the Curve Exposure Difference [QE-15]

**Context (QE2):**
| Tenor | KRD active | KRD index | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | −0.532 | 0.738 | −1.270 |
| 5y | 0.324 | 1.688 | −1.364 |
| 10y | 5.181 | 2.747 | 2.434 |
| 30y | 1.142 | 2.162 | −1.020 |
| Portfolio | 6.115 | 7.335 | −1.220 |

**Question:**
Which of the following derivatives strategies would best offset the yield curve exposure difference between the active and index portfolios?

A. Add a pay-fixed 10-year swap and long 2-year, 5-year, and 30-year bond futures positions to the active portfolio.
B. Add a receive-fixed 30-year swap, a pay-fixed 10-year swap, and short positions in 2-year and 5-year bond futures to the active portfolio.
C. Add a pay-fixed 10-year swap, a short 30-year bond futures, and long 2-year and 5-year bond futures positions to the active portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Add a pay-fixed 10-year swap and long 2-, 5- and 30-year futures.** Active is short vs index at 2/5/30 (add duration) and long at 10 (reduce it).
- **B is wrong.** It shorts 2y and 5y futures, adding to the short exposure there.
- **C is wrong.** It shorts 30y futures, adding to the short exposure there.

**Key Principle:**
> Offset negative KRD differences with long positions; positive with short/pay-fixed.

# Standalone — Forward Rate Bias

### Q222 — Forward Rate Bias [QE-16]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements best describes the forward rate bias?

A. Investors tend to favor fixed-income investments in currencies that trade at a premium on a forward basis.
B. Investors tend to hedge fixed-income investments in higher-yielding currencies given the potential for lower returns due to currency depreciation.
C. Investors tend to favor unhedged fixed-income investments in higher-yielding currencies that are sometimes enhanced by borrowing in lower-yielding currencies.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Lower-yielding currencies trade at a forward premium.
- **B is wrong.** Under covered interest rate parity, hedged foreign investments earn about the domestic risk-free rate.
- **C is correct.** **Favor unhedged investments in higher-yielding currencies, sometimes enhanced by borrowing in lower-yielding currencies.** Forward rate bias is an observed divergence from interest rate parity.

**Key Principle:**
> Forward rate bias: carry from high-yield currencies persists vs parity.

# QE3 — THB Strategies (Rolldown and Currency)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QE3*

### Q223 — Rolldown Returns for the THB Strategies [QE-17]

**Context (QE3):**
Buy and hold: 1-year THB zero at 1.00%, price 99.0090 → 100.00. Rolldown: 2-year zero at 2.00%, price 96.1169 → 99.0090. Expected THB appreciation 1.5%.

**Question:**
The rolldown returns over the 1-year investment horizon for the Buy-and-Hold and Yield Curve Rolldown portfolios are closest to:

A. 1.00% for the Buy-and-Hold portfolio and 3.01% for the Yield Curve Rolldown portfolio, respectively.
B. 0.991% for the Buy-and-Hold portfolio and 3.01% for the Yield Curve Rolldown portfolio, respectively.
C. 0.991% for the Buy-and-Hold portfolio and 2.09% for the Yield Curve Rolldown portfolio, respectively.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **1.00% and 3.01%.** Buy and hold: (100 − 99.009)/99.009 = 1.00%. Rolldown: (99.009 − 96.1169)/96.1169 = 3.01%.
- **B is wrong.** 0.991% is not the buy-and-hold rolldown.
- **C is wrong.** 0.991% and 2.09% don't match the price changes.

**Key Principle:**
> Rolldown return = (expected price in 1 yr − price today) / price today, static curve.

### Q224 — Total Expected Return in USD [QE-18]

**Context (QE3):**
Buy and hold: 1-year THB zero at 1.00%, price 99.0090 → 100.00. Rolldown: 2-year zero at 2.00%, price 96.1169 → 99.0090. Expected THB appreciation 1.5%.

**Question:**
The total expected return over the 1-year investment horizon for the Buy-and-Hold and Yield Curve Rolldown portfolios are closest to:

A. 2.515% for the Buy-and-Hold portfolio and 4.555% for the Yield Curve rolldown portfolio, respectively.
B. 2.42% for the Buy-and-Hold portfolio and 4.51% for the Yield Curve Rolldown portfolio, respectively.
C. 2.491% for the Buy-and-Hold portfolio and 3.59% for the Yield Curve Rolldown portfolio, respectively.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **2.515% and 4.555%.** R_DC = (1 + R_FC)(1 + R_FX) − 1: (1.01)(1.015) − 1 = 2.515%; (1.0301)(1.015) − 1 = 4.555%.
- **B is wrong.** Doesn't match the formula.
- **C is wrong.** Doesn't match the formula.

**Key Principle:**
> R_DC = (1 + R_FC)(1 + R_FX) − 1.

### Q225 — Effect of a Rise in THB Yields on Expected Returns [QE-19]

**Context (QE3):**
Buy and hold: 1-year THB zero at 1.00%, price 99.0090 → 100.00. Rolldown: 2-year zero at 2.00%, price 96.1169 → 99.0090. Expected THB appreciation 1.5%.

**Question:**
Which of the following statements best describes how the expected total return results would change if THB yields were to rise significantly over the investment horizon?

A. Both the Buy-and-Hold and Yield Curve Rolldown expected portfolio returns would increase due to higher THB yields.
B. Both the Buy-and-Hold and Yield Curve Rolldown expected portfolio returns would decrease due to higher THB yields.
C. The Buy-and-Hold expected portfolio returns would be unchanged and the Yield Curve Rolldown expected portfolio returns would decrease due to the rise in yields.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Higher yields lower, not raise, the sale price of the rolldown bond.
- **B is wrong.** The buy-and-hold bond matures, so it is unaffected.
- **C is correct.** **Buy-and-hold unchanged; rolldown falls.** The 1-year zero matures at the horizon; the rolldown bond is sold in a year at a lower price when yields are higher.

**Key Principle:**
> Bonds held to maturity are unaffected by interim yield changes; rolldown positions are exposed.

# Standalone — Rolldown and Curve Flattener

### Q226 — Rolldown Return of a 5-Year Bond [QE-20]

**Context:**
Dutch investor; 5-year EUR government bond; yields expected to fall 25 bps in six months.

**Question:**
A Dutch investor considering a 5-year EUR government bond purchase expects yields-to-maturity to decline by 25 bps in the next six months. Which of the following statements about the rolldown return is correct?

A. The rolldown return equals the difference between the price of the 5-year bond and that of a 4.5-year bond at the lower yield-to-maturity.
B. The rolldown return consists of the 5-year bond’s basis point value multiplied by the expected 25 bp yield-to-maturity change over the next six months.
C. The rolldown return will be negative if the 5-year bond has a zero coupon and is trading at a premium.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** That uses the lower yield, which includes the yield-change effect.
- **B is wrong.** That describes the price effect of the investor's yield view (BPV × Δyield).
- **C is correct.** **Negative if the 5-year bond is a zero-coupon trading at a premium.** Rolldown return is the price difference between the 5-year and 4.5-year bond at the same yield; a zero at a premium has a negative yield and its premium amortizes as it pulls to par.

**Key Principle:**
> Rolldown holds yield constant; yield-view effect is separate.

### Q227 — Loss on a Duration-Neutral Flattener [QE-21]

**Context:**
Short 2-year / long 10-year duration-neutral position.

**Question:**
An active investor enters a duration-neutral yield curve flattening trade that combines 2-year and 10-year Treasury positions. Under which of the following yield curve scenarios would you expect the investor to realize the greatest portfolio loss?

A. Bear steepening
B. Bull flattening
C. Yields unchanged

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Bear steepening.** The 10-year yield rises more than the 2-year, so the long 10-year loses; the spread widens.
- **B is wrong.** Bull flattening: the long 10-year gains.
- **C is wrong.** Unchanged yields: no change.

**Key Principle:**
> Flattener loses when the curve steepens.

# QE4 — Boyd / Cook (Curve Positioning, Total Return, Swaptions)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QE4*

### Q228 — Portfolio Positioned for a Bear Steepener [QE-22] (Essay)

**Context (QE4):**
Boyd expects a bear steepening of the UK curve.
| | Index | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2y KRD | 2.50 | 2.50 | 4.00 | 1.00 |
| 5y KRD | 3.60 | 3.60 | 6.50 | 4.00 |
| 10y KRD | 7.25 | 7.25 | 2.85 | 8.35 |

**Question:**
Determine the portfolio most likely to outperform the index; justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Portfolio B.** In a bear steepening long-term yields rise more than short-term yields. B is overweight the short end (2y, 5y) and underweight the 10-year relative to the index, so it loses least from rising long yields.
- A mirrors the index, so no outperformance.
- C is overweight the 10-year and would underperform.

**Key Principle:**
> Bear steepener: underweight long-end duration.

### Q229 — Expected Total Return With Rolldown, Yield and Currency Effects [QE-23] (Essay)

**Context (QE4):**
| | Bullet | Barbell |
|---|---|---|
| Price today | 97.00 | 97.75 |
| Price in 1y (unchanged curve) | 98.75 | 99.75 |
| Duration in 1y | 4.50 | 4.45 |
| Convexity in 1y | 17.0 | 40.0 |
Yields fall 50 bps in parallel; USD expected to depreciate 0.75% vs EUR.

**Question:**
Calculate the expected total return (in EUR) for each portfolio.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

E(R) ≈ rolldown + E(%ΔPrice from yield view) + E(%ΔPrice from currency).

**Bullet:** rolldown = (98.75 − 97.00)/97.00 = 1.80%; yield view = 4.50(0.005) + 0.5(17.0)(0.005²) = 2.27%; currency −0.75% → **3.32%**.
**Barbell:** rolldown = (99.75 − 97.75)/97.75 = 2.05%; yield view = 4.45(0.005) + 0.5(40.0)(0.005²) = 2.28%; currency −0.75% → **3.58%**.

**Flag:** Key sums rounded components. Unrounded: bullet 1.8041 + 2.2713 − 0.75 = 3.325%; barbell 2.0460 + 2.2750 − 0.75 = 3.571%. Rounds to 3.33% and 3.57%, vs the key's 3.32% and 3.58%.

**Key Principle:**
> E(R) ≈ rolldown + yield view effect + currency effect.

### Q230 — Receiver Swaption Position for Falling Rates [QE-24] (Essay)

**Context (QE4):**
Cook expects a 50 bp downward parallel shift in US rates.

**Question:**
Determine whether long or short receiver swaption gives a higher return; justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Long.** A long receiver swaption gives the right to receive fixed and pay floating, which gains as rates fall.

**Key Principle:**
> Long receiver swaption = bet on lower rates.

# QE5 — Eagle Investments / Yeti (Static Curve Strategies)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QE5*

### Q231 — Strategy Least Likely to Add Excess Return in a Static Environment [QE-25]

**Context (QE5):**
Yeti expects the yield curve and rates to remain stable.

**Question:**
Given Yeti’s yield curve and interest rate view, which of the following strategies is least likely to generate excess returns?

A. Adding leverage to a portfolio
B. Reducing portfolio duration below that of the benchmark
C. Increasing portfolio duration above that of the benchmark

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Leverage is one of the two basic static-curve strategies.
- **B is correct.** **Reducing duration below the benchmark.** Under a static curve, excess return comes from adding duration and adding leverage.
- **C is wrong.** Adding duration is one of the two basic static-curve strategies.

**Key Principle:**
> Static curve: extend duration, add leverage.

# QE6 — Lee / Cook (Riding the Yield Curve, Convexity)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QE6*

### Q232 — Riding the Yield Curve [QE-26]

**Context (QE6):**
US YTM: 1y 2.00%, 2y 2.50%, 3y 2.78%, 5y 2.90%, 10y 3.20%. Germany: 1y −0.20%, 2y 0.15%, 3y 0.32%, 5y 0.61%, 10y 0.95%.

**Question:**
It is most likely correct that a “riding the yield curve” strategy:

A. cannot work in Germany.
B. is more suited for implementation in the US.
C. assumes that the level of the yield curve will change.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Germany's curve is also upward sloping.
- **B is correct.** **More suited to the US.** The strategy needs a static, upward-sloping curve; the US curve is steeper.
- **C is wrong.** It assumes a static yield curve.

**Key Principle:**
> Riding the curve: buy long, sell before maturity; needs a stable upward-sloping curve.

### Q233 — Buying Convexity [QE-27]

**Question:**
Buying convexity will most likely:

A. require selling puts.
B. require buying calls.
C. increase the portfolio yield.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Selling puts doesn't add convexity.
- **B is correct.** **Requires buying calls.** Convexity can be added by buying calls, puts or putable bonds, or reducing MBS/callable exposure; the cost is lower yield.
- **C is wrong.** Convexity costs yield.

**Key Principle:**
> Buying convexity costs yield; buy options or putable bonds.

# Standalone — Duration, Convexity, Curve and Currency

### Q234 — Lowest Loss for a +60 bp Shift [QE-28]

**Context:**
| Tenor | Price | Mod. dur. | Convexity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | 104.85 | 1.906 | 4.7 |
| 6y | 111.26 | 5.378 | 33.4 |
| 12y | 116.42 | 10.251 | 120.5 |
Yields rise 60 bps.

**Question:**
A long-only bond portfolio manager expects a higher inflation outlook to result in a parallel upward shift in the yield curve by 60 bps. The manager gathers the following information on three government bonds:

| Tenor (Years) | Price | Modified Duration | Convexity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 104.85 | 1.906 | 4.7 |
| 6 | 111.26 | 5.378 | 33.4 |
| 12 | 116.42 | 10.251 | 120.5 |

Which of the following portfolios will have the lowest decline in value in response to this expected view in the yield curve?

A. A portfolio long on the 6-year government bonds
B. An equally weighted portfolio long on the 2-year and 12-year government bonds
C. An equally weighted portfolio long on the 2-year, 6-year and 12-year government bonds

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Long the 6-year.** −5.378(0.006) + 0.5(33.4)(0.006²) = −3.167%.
- **B is wrong.** Equal 2y/12y: duration 6.0785, convexity 62.6 → −3.534%.
- **C is wrong.** Equal 2y/6y/12y: duration 5.845, convexity 52.9 → −3.412%.

**Key Principle:**
> Lower duration → smaller loss when yields rise.

### Q235 — Highest Convexity Portfolio [QE-29]

**Context:**
| Tenor | Price | Mod. dur. | Convexity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | 104.85 | 1.906 | 4.7 |
| 6y | 111.26 | 5.378 | 33.4 |
| 12y | 116.42 | 10.251 | 120.5 |

**Question:**
A portfolio manager is analyzing three fixed-income portfolios that can use the following government bonds:

| Tenor (Years) | Coupon | Yield | Price | Modified Duration | Convexity |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 5.0% | 2.5% | 104.85 | 1.906 | 4.7 |
| 6 | 4.0% | 2.0% | 111.26 | 5.378 | 33.4 |
| 12 | 3.0% | 1.5% | 116.42 | 10.251 | 120.5 |

Which of the following portfolios has the highest convexity assuming a parallel shift in the yield curve?

A. A portfolio that is long the 6-year government bond
B. An equally weighted portfolio that is long the 2-year and the 12-year government bonds
C. An equally weighted portfolio that is long the 2-year, the 6-year and the 12-year government bonds

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 6-year convexity is 33.4, the lowest.
- **B is correct.** **Equal 2y and 12y.** Convexity (4.7 + 120.5)/2 = 62.6.
- **C is wrong.** Convexity (4.7 + 33.4 + 120.5)/3 = 52.9.

**Key Principle:**
> Barbell has higher convexity than the bullet or equal-weight mix.

### Q236 — Change in the Butterfly Spread [QE-30]

**Context:**
2y yield 4.0%, 5y 3.1%, 10y 2.6%. Short and long yields fall 20 bps; medium yield rises 15 bps.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager is analyzing the effects of interest rate changes on several fixed-income portfolio strategies using the following government bonds:

| Tenor (years) | Coupon | Yield | Price |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 5.5% | 4.0% | 102.86 |
| 5 | 4.2% | 3.1% | 105.06 |
| 10 | 3.5% | 2.6% | 107.88 |

If the short- and long-term yields are expected to fall by 20 bps and the medium-term yield to rise by 15 bps, the butterfly spread will:

A. narrow by 70 bps.
B. remain unchanged.
C. widen by 70 bps.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The spread rises, not narrows.
- **B is wrong.** The spread changes.
- **C is correct.** **Widens by 70 bps.** Current = −4.0% + 2(3.1%) − 2.6% = −0.4%. New = −3.8% + 2(3.25%) − 2.4% = +0.3%. Change = +0.7%.

**Key Principle:**
> Butterfly = −short + 2 × medium − long.

### Q237 — Active vs Index Value Change for a 30 bp Decline [QE-31]

**Context:**
$100m portfolios. Active (25/25/50 in 2y/6y/12y): duration 6.946, convexity 69.8. Index (equal weights): 5.845, 52.9.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager is examining the effects of a change in the yields-to-maturity on a $100 million active bond portfolio with weights of 25% each in 2-year and 6-year Treasuries and the remaining 50% in 12-year Treasuries relative to an equally weighted $100 million Treasury bond index portfolio holding 2-year, 6-year and 12-year Treasuries. The summary data related to the two portfolios is as follows:

Portfolio

| Modified Duration | Convexity |  |
|---|---|---|
| Active (25/25/50) | 6.946 | 69.8 |
| Index (Equally weighted) | 5.845 | 52.9 |

A parallel shift of 30 bps decline in yields would most likely result in the:

A. active portfolio rising by $330,000 more than the index portfolio.
B. active portfolio rising by $338,000 more than the index portfolio.
C. index portfolio rising by $338,000 more than the active portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** $330,000 ignores the convexity term.
- **B is correct.** **Active rises $338,000 more.** Active: 6.946(0.003) + 0.5(69.8)(0.003²) = 2.115%. Index: 5.845(0.003) + 0.5(52.9)(0.003²) = 1.777%. Difference 0.338% × $100m = $338,000.
- **C is wrong.** Sign reversed.

**Key Principle:**
> Include the convexity term when comparing large moves.

### Q238 — Unhedged Return on a US Zero for a UK Investor [QE-32]

**Context:**
1-year US zero at 3.2%. GBP/USD spot GBP 0.74 per USD, moving to 0.75 at year end.

**Question:**
A UK-based portfolio manager purchases a US Treasury zero-coupon bond maturing exactly one year from today at a yield-to-maturity of 3.2%. The current GBP/USD spot rate is GBP 0.74 per USD and the spot rate moves to GBP 0.75 per USD at the end of the year. If the yield curve is flat and unchanged, the unhedged return earned by the portfolio manager over the year is closest to:

A. 1.82%.
B. 3.20%.
C. 4.59%.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 1.82% divides the change by 0.75 and gets the sign wrong.
- **B is wrong.** 3.20% is the hedged return.
- **C is correct.** **4.59%.** USD appreciates vs GBP by (0.75 − 0.74)/0.74 = 1.351%. R_DC = (1.032)(1.01351) − 1 = 4.59%.

**Key Principle:**
> R_DC = (1 + R_FC)(1 + R_FX) − 1, with R_FX the foreign currency's change in domestic terms.

### Q239 — Cross-Currency Carry Trade Realized Return [QE-33]

**Context:**
Borrow AUD at 2% for 1 year; invest in 90-day country X bills rolled over. 90-day rates: 6.5%, 6.6%, 6.7%, 7.0% (annualized). X/AUD spot: 5.80 today, 5.95 at 360 days.

**Question:**
An Australia-based active portfolio manager is analyzing a cross-currency carry trade between the Australian dollar (AUD) and Country X’s money market rates. The manager wants to borrow AUD for one year, invest in the 90-day Treasury bills of Country X, and roll them over at maturity for the next 12 months. The 1-year AUD interest rate is 2%​​​​​.​The 90-day Country X interest rates and the X/AUD spot exchange rates are expected to change as follows over the coming year:

| Time | 90-Day Country X rate | X/AUD spot rate |
|---|---|---|
| Today | 6.5% | 5.80 |
| 90 Days | 6.6% | 5.82 |
| 180 Days | 6.7% | 5.85 |
| 270 Days | 7.0% | 5.90 |
| 360 Days | --- | 5.95 |

If the manager rolls over this trade for the entire year, her realized return would be closest to:

A. 2.18%.
B. 4.87%.
C. 7.63%.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **2.18%.** (1 + 0.065/4)(1 + 0.066/4)(1 + 0.067/4)(1 + 0.070/4) × (5.80/5.95) − 1.02 = 2.18%.
- **B is wrong.** 4.87% ignores the currency move.
- **C is wrong.** 7.63% inverts the exchange-rate ratio (5.95/5.80).

**Key Principle:**
> Carry trade: rolled local-currency return × FX conversion − funding cost.

### Q240 — Bullet vs Barbell Expected Return With Currency [QE-34]

**Context:**
| | Bullet | Barbell |
|---|---|---|
| Price today | 94.50 | 96.00 |
| Price in 1y (stable curve) | 98.00 | 99.00 |
| Duration in 1y | 5.00 | 5.00 |
| Convexity in 1y | 11.70 | 38.00 |
Yields fall 50 bps; EUR depreciates 0.40% vs USD; US-based investor.

**Question:**
A US-based portfolio manager invests in euro-denominated zero-coupon bonds. She expects that over the coming year the euro zero-coupon yield curve will experience a downward parallel shift of 50 bps and that the euro will depreciate by 0.40% against the US dollar. The manager gathers the following information about bullet and barbell strategies:

| Statistic | Bullet Strategy | Barbell Strategy |
|---|---|---|
| Investment horizon (years) | 1.0 | 1.0 |
| Average portfolio bond price in Year 0 (today) | 94.50 | 96.00 |
| Average portfolio bond price in Year 1 (stable yield curve) | 98.00 | 99.00 |
| Expected portfolio effective duration in Year 1 | 5.00 | 5.00 |
| Expected portfolio convexity in Year 1 | 11.70 | 38.00 |
| Expected change in euro zero-coupon yields | −0.50% | −0.50% |
| Expected change in the EUR against the USD | −0.40% | −0.40% |

Based on the expected return, the portfolio manager should:

A. choose the barbell strategy over the bullet strategy.
B. choose the bullet strategy over the barbell strategy.
C. be indifferent between the barbell and bullet strategies.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The barbell's extra convexity (+0.03%) doesn't offset its lower rolldown.
- **B is correct.** **Bullet.** Rolldown: bullet 3.704%, barbell 3.125%. Yield view: bullet 5.00(0.005) + 0.5(11.7)(0.005²) = 2.515%; barbell 2.548%. With currency: bullet (1 + 0.03704 + 0.02515)(0.996) − 1 = 5.79%; barbell 5.25%.
- **C is wrong.** The expected returns differ.

**Key Principle:**
> E(R) ≈ rolldown + yield view + currency effect.

### Q241 — Portfolio When Rate Volatility Is Expected to Rise [QE-35]

**Question:**
An investor expects an increase in interest rate volatility over the next year. All else being equal, the investor is most likely to construct a fixed-income portfolio with bonds having:

A. lower convexity.
B. shorter duration.
C. higher cash flow dispersion.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Higher convexity is desired.
- **B is wrong.** Longer duration (more convexity) is desired per the key.
- **C is correct.** **Higher cash flow dispersion.** Higher dispersion raises convexity, which is valuable when volatility rises.

**Key Principle:**
> Higher convexity benefits from higher rate volatility; dispersion raises convexity.

### Q242 — Accurate Statement About Convexity [QE-36]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements relating to bond convexity is most accurate? All else being equal, a bond that has higher:

A. convexity has a higher yield-to-maturity.
B. cash flow dispersion has a lower convexity.
C. convexity leads to a higher price increase when interest rates decrease.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Higher convexity might come with a lower yield for a similar-duration bond.
- **B is wrong.** Higher cash flow dispersion means higher convexity.
- **C is correct.** **Higher convexity leads to a higher price increase when rates fall** (and a smaller decrease when they rise) vs duration alone.

**Key Principle:**
> Convexity is valuable; it costs yield.

### Q243 — Action When Yields Are Expected to Rise [QE-37]

**Question:**
A portfolio manager who expects yields to increase is most likely to:

A. sell a contract for forward bond delivery.
B. extend the portfolio duration with longer-dated bonds.
C. enter into an interest rate swap as a fixed-rate receiver.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Sell a contract for forward bond delivery** (reduces duration).
- **B is wrong.** Extending duration is for expected lower yields.
- **C is wrong.** Receiving fixed on a swap adds duration (for expected lower yields).

**Key Principle:**
> Expect higher yields → shorten duration.

### Q244 — Positioning for a Flattening Upward-Sloping Curve [QE-38]

**Question:**
Which of the following is the best portfolio positioning strategy for an expected flattening of upward-sloping yield curve?

A. Buy a 30-year payer swaption and buy a 2-year bond put option
B. Buy a 30-year receiver swaption and buy a 2-year bond put option
C. Buy a 30-year receiver swaption and sell a 2-year bond put option

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A payer swaption loses value when long yields fall.
- **B is correct.** **Buy a 30-year receiver swaption and buy a 2-year bond put option.** The receiver gains as long yields fall; the put gains as short yields rise.
- **C is wrong.** Selling the 2-year put gives up the gain from rising short yields.

**Key Principle:**
> Flattener: receiver swaption (long end) + put (short end).

### Q245 — Position for a Parallel Upward Shift [QE-39]

**Question:**
For a parallel upward shift in the yield curve, the best strategy for an investor is to go long a:

A. bond call option.
B. receiver swaption.
C. put option on bond futures.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A bond call loses value when yields rise.
- **B is wrong.** A receiver swaption loses value when rates rise.
- **C is correct.** **Put option on bond futures.** It gains as rates rise, decreasing duration.

**Key Principle:**
> Rates up → long puts / payer swaptions.

### Q246 — Key Rate Durations From Positions [QE-40]

**Context:**
Key rate duration = mod. dur. × position ÷ portfolio value.
| Tenor | Mod. dur. | Active ($m) | Index ($m) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2y | 1.68 | 30 | 30 |
| 5y | 4.52 | 40 | 45 |
| 10y | 8.32 | 30 | 55 |

**Question:**
An analyst gathers the following information about an active portfolio and the corresponding index portfolio:

| Tenor (years) | Modified Duration | Active Portfolio Position (in $ million) | Index Portfolio Position (in $ million) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 1.68 | 30 | 30 |
| 5 |  |  |  |

4.52

| 40 | 45 |  |  |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10 | 8.32 | 30 | 55 |

Which of the following statements is most accurate?

A. The active portfolio has higher exposure to the 5-year maturity than index portfolio
B. The total key rate duration of the active portfolio is higher than that of the index portfolio
C. The difference in key rate duration of the 2-year maturity between the active portfolio and the index portfolio is zero

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Active has higher 5-year exposure.** 5y KRD: active 4.52 × 40/100 = 1.808; index 4.52 × 45/130 = 1.565.
- **B is wrong.** Total KRD: active 0.504 + 1.808 + 2.496 = 4.808; index 0.388 + 1.565 + 3.520 = 5.473, so the active total is lower.
- **C is wrong.** 2y KRD: active 0.504 vs index 0.388, a 0.116 difference, not zero.

**Key Principle:**
> KRD_i = ModDur_i × (position_i ÷ total portfolio value).

# Standalone — Empirical Duration, Spreads, Spread-Based Portfolios

### Q247 — Empirical Duration [QF-1]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements best describes empirical duration?

A. A common way to calculate a bond’s empirical duration is to run a regression of its price returns on changes in a benchmark interest rate.
B. A bond’s empirical duration tends to be larger than its effective duration.
C. The price sensitivity of high-yield bonds to interest rate changes is typically higher than that of investment-grade bonds.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Estimated by regressing a bond's price returns on changes in a benchmark interest rate.**
- **B is wrong.** Not the keyed answer; the key doesn't explain this statement.
- **C is wrong.** Not the keyed answer; the key doesn't explain this statement.

**Key Principle:**
> Empirical duration = regression of price returns on benchmark yield changes.

### Q248 — Combined Effect of Rate and Spread Increases [QF-2]

**Context:**
EKN 10-year high-yield bond: price 91.82, modified duration 8.47, spread duration 8.47. Rates +20 bps and credit spread +20 bps.

**Question:**
A junior analyst considers a 10-year high-yield bond issued by EKN Corporation (EKN) position in a high-yield portfolio. The bond has a price of 91.82, a modified duration of 8.47, and a spread duration of 8.47. The analyst speculates on the effects of an interest rate increase of 20 bps and, because of a change in its credit risk, an increase in the EKN bond’s credit spread of 20 bps. The analyst comments that because the modified duration and the credit spread duration of the EKN bond are equal, the bond’s price will not change (all else being equal) in response to the interest rate and credit spread changes.

Is the analyst’s prediction correct that the EKN bond price will not change in response to the interest rate and credit spread changes, all else being equal?

A. Yes.
B. No, the bond price should decrease.
C. No, the bond price should increase.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Both effects reduce price; they don't offset.
- **B is correct.** **Price decreases about 3.4%.** Rate effect −8.47 × 0.0020 = −1.694%; spread effect −8.47 × 0.0020 = −1.694%; total −3.388%.
- **C is wrong.** Both increases lower the price.

**Key Principle:**
> %ΔP ≈ −ModDur × ΔYield − SpreadDur × ΔSpread.

### Q249 — Effect of a Higher Recovery Rate [QF-3]

**Context:**
Spread ≈ LGD × POD; LGD = 1 − RR.

**Question:**
Which of the following outcomes is most likely if the junior analyst revises the bond’s original recovery rate higher?

A. An increase in the bond’s POD
B. A decrease in the bond’s POD
C. A decrease in the bond’s credit spread

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Recovery rate affects LGD, not POD.
- **B is wrong.** Recovery rate affects LGD, not POD.
- **C is correct.** **A decrease in credit spread.** A higher recovery rate lowers LGD, so spread falls.

**Key Principle:**
> Spread ≈ POD × LGD; LGD = 1 − RR.

### Q250 — Risks of Spread-Based Portfolios [QF-4]

**Question:**
Which of the following observations on the risks of spread-based fixed-income portfolios is the most accurate?
