A. Because credit spreads equal the product of the LGD and the POD, distinguishing between the credit risk and liquidity risk components of yield spread across all market scenarios is straightforward.
B. Given that frequent issuers with many bonds outstanding across maturities have their own issuer-specific credit curve, distinguishing between the credit spread and liquidity spread of all bonds for these issuers is straightforward.
C. The yield spread of a particular bond comprises both credit and liquidity risk and depends on market conditions and the specific supply-and-demand dynamics of each fixed-income security.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Separating credit and liquidity components is not straightforward.
- **B is wrong.** Separating credit and liquidity spreads is not straightforward even for frequent issuers.
- **C is correct.** **Yield spread comprises both credit and liquidity risk** and depends on market conditions and supply/demand for each security.

**Key Principle:**
> Yield spread = credit + liquidity components; hard to separate.

# QF1 — Spread Measures (G-Spread, I-Spread, Asset Swap Spread)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF1*

### Q251 — G-Spread of the Corporate Bond [QF-5]

**Context (QF1):**
| Issuer | Term | Coupon | Yield | ModDur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Corporate | 12y | 3.00% | 2.80% | 9.99 |
| Government | 10y | 1.75% | 1.85% | 9.09 |
| Government | 20y | 2.25% | 2.30% | 15.94 |
10-year and 20-year swap spreads: 0.20% and 0.25%. Interpolation weights for 12y: 80% (10y), 20% (20y).

**Question:**
Calculate the G-spread of the corporate bond.

A. 0.860%
B. 0.725%
C. 0.950%

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **0.860%.** 12-year government yield = 0.8(1.85%) + 0.2(2.30%) = 1.94%. G-spread = 2.80% − 1.94% = 0.86%.
- **B is wrong.** 0.725% doesn't match.
- **C is wrong.** 0.950% doesn't match.

**Key Principle:**
> G-spread = bond YTM − interpolated government YTM.

### Q252 — I-Spread of the Corporate Bond [QF-6]

**Context (QF1):**
| Issuer | Term | Coupon | Yield | ModDur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Corporate | 12y | 3.00% | 2.80% | 9.99 |
| Government | 10y | 1.75% | 1.85% | 9.09 |
| Government | 20y | 2.25% | 2.30% | 15.94 |
10-year and 20-year swap spreads: 0.20% and 0.25%. Interpolation weights for 12y: 80% (10y), 20% (20y).

**Question:**
Calculate the I-spread of the corporate bond.

A. 0.85%
B. 0.65%
C. 0.95%

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 0.85% is the ASW.
- **B is correct.** **0.65%.** Swap rates: 10y 1.85% + 0.20% = 2.05%; 20y 2.30% + 0.25% = 2.55%. 12y swap = 0.8(2.05%) + 0.2(2.55%) = 2.15%. I-spread = 2.80% − 2.15% = 0.65%.
- **C is wrong.** 0.95% doesn't match.

**Key Principle:**
> I-spread = bond YTM − interpolated swap rate.

### Q253 — Asset Swap Spread of the Corporate Bond [QF-7]

**Context (QF1):**
| Issuer | Term | Coupon | Yield | ModDur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Corporate | 12y | 3.00% | 2.80% | 9.99 |
| Government | 10y | 1.75% | 1.85% | 9.09 |
| Government | 20y | 2.25% | 2.30% | 15.94 |
10-year and 20-year swap spreads: 0.20% and 0.25%. Interpolation weights for 12y: 80% (10y), 20% (20y).

**Question:**
Calculate the ASW of the corporate bond.

A. 0.65%
B. 0.95%
C. 0.85%

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 0.65% is the I-spread.
- **B is wrong.** 0.95% doesn't match.
- **C is correct.** **0.85%.** ASW = bond coupon − interpolated swap rate = 3.00% − 2.15%.

**Key Principle:**
> ASW = coupon − swap rate.

### Q254 — Price Change After a Steepening [QF-8]

**Context (QF1):**
| Issuer | Term | Coupon | Yield | ModDur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Corporate | 12y | 3.00% | 2.80% | 9.99 |
| Government | 10y | 1.75% | 1.85% | 9.09 |
| Government | 20y | 2.25% | 2.30% | 15.94 |
10-year and 20-year swap spreads: 0.20% and 0.25%. Interpolation weights for 12y: 80% (10y), 20% (20y).
20-year government YTM +0.20%; 10-year unchanged; G-spread unchanged.

**Question:**
Estimate the corporate bond’s percentage price change if the government yield curve steepens, assuming a 0.20% increase in the 20-year YTM and no change to the 10-year government YTM or corporate G-spread.

A. –0.40%
B. 0.40%
C. –0.04%

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **−0.40%.** 12y government yield rises 0.2 × 0.20% = 0.04% (to 1.98%). %ΔP ≈ −9.99 × 0.04% = −0.40%.
- **B is wrong.** Price falls when yields rise.
- **C is wrong.** The yield change applies to the 12-year (0.04%), giving −0.40%.

**Key Principle:**
> Interpolated benchmark moves by weight × change; %ΔP ≈ −ModDur × ΔY.

# Standalone — Credit Spread Measures

### Q255 — Credit Spread Measures [QF-9]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements about credit spread measures is most accurate?

A. The DM is the yield spread over the MRR established upon issuance to compensate investors for assuming an issuer’s credit risk.
B. The Z-DM will be above the DM if the MRR is expected to remain constant over time.
C. The yield spread for a corporate bond will be equal to the G-spread if the government benchmark yield curve is flat.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Not the keyed answer.
- **B is wrong.** Not the keyed answer.
- **C is correct.** **Yield spread equals G-spread if the government curve is flat.** The yield spread uses a single on-the-run government bond; the G-spread interpolates.

**Key Principle:**
> Flat curve → yield spread = G-spread.

# QF2 — BBB and BB Bonds (Excess Return and Excess Spread)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF2*

### Q256 — Six-Month Excess Return With Spread Narrowing [QF-10]

**Context (QF2):**
BBB: spread 2.75%, effective spread duration 6. BB: spread 3.50%, effective spread duration 5.
BBB held six months; spread narrows 40 bps; no default losses.

**Question:**
What is the approximate excess return if the BBB rated bond is held for six months and the credit spread narrows by 40 bps, ignoring spread duration changes and assuming no default losses?

A. 3.775%
B. 2.35%
C. 2.40%

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **3.775%.** ExcessSpread ≈ Spread₀/periods − SpreadDur × ΔSpread = 2.75% × 0.5 − 6 × (−0.40%) = 1.375% + 2.40%.
- **B is wrong.** 2.35% doesn't match.
- **C is wrong.** 2.40% omits the carry (1.375%).

**Key Principle:**
> ExcessSpread ≈ Spread₀ × t − SpreadDur × ΔSpread − POD × LGD.

### Q257 — Instantaneous Excess Return for a Spread Widening [QF-11]

**Context (QF2):**
BBB: spread 2.75%, effective spread duration 6. BB: spread 3.50%, effective spread duration 5.
BB spread widens 50 bps.

**Question:**
What is the instantaneous (holding period of zero) excess return for the BB rated bond if the spread widens by 50 bps?

A. 3.00%
B. –2.50%
C. 2.50%

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Widening produces a loss.
- **B is correct.** **−2.50%.** −SpreadDur × ΔSpread = −5 × 0.5%.
- **C is wrong.** Widening produces a loss.

**Key Principle:**
> Instantaneous excess return = −SpreadDur × ΔSpread.

### Q258 — One-Year Expected Excess Spread [QF-12]

**Context (QF2):**
BBB: spread 2.75%, effective spread duration 6. BB: spread 3.50%, effective spread duration 5.
BBB, one-year holding; LGD 40%; POD 0.75%; 50 bp decline (key uses a spread decline).

**Question:**
What is the expected excess spread of the BBB rated bond for a 50 bp decline in yield over a one-year holding period if the bond’s LGD is 40% and the POD is 0.75%?

A. 1.95%
B. 2.45%
C. 5.45%

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 1.95% doesn't match.
- **B is wrong.** 2.45% doesn't match.
- **C is correct.** **5.45%.** Spread₀ − SpreadDur × ΔSpread − POD × LGD = 2.75% − 6 × (−0.50%) − 0.75% × 40% = 2.75% + 3.00% − 0.30%.

**Flag:** The stem says a 50 bp decline in 'yield', but the key treats it as a 50 bp decline in spread.

**Key Principle:**
> E[ExcessSpread] ≈ Spread₀ − SpreadDur × ΔSpread − POD × LGD.

# Standalone — Credit Ratios and Statistical Models

### Q259 — Ratio Change That Lowers Probability of Default [QF-13]

**Question:**
An active manager is considering the senior bonds of one of several corporate issuers. Holding other factors constant, which of the following key financial ratio changes would lead the manager to expect a decrease in the POD for that issuer?

A. An increase in the issuer’s coverage ratio
B. An increase in the issuer’s stock price volatility
C. An increase in the issuer’s leverage ratio

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Higher coverage ratio.** More cash flow to service debt means lower distress risk.
- **B is wrong.** Higher stock price volatility raises default risk.
- **C is wrong.** Higher leverage raises default risk.

**Flag:** Key explains only A; the reasons given for B and C are my reading, not from the key.

**Key Principle:**
> ↑ coverage → ↓ POD; ↑ leverage or volatility → ↑ POD.

### Q260 — Statistical Credit Models [QF-14]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements about statistical credit analysis models is most accurate?

A. Structural credit models solve for the POD using observable company-specific variables such as financial ratios and macroeconomic variables.
B. Reduced-form credit models use market-based variables to estimate an issuer’s asset value and the volatility of asset value.
C. Structural credit models define the likelihood of default as the probability of the asset value falling below that of liabilities.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Describes reduced-form models (observable company variables and macro factors).
- **B is wrong.** Describes structural models (market variables to estimate asset value and volatility).
- **C is correct.** **Structural models define default as the probability of asset value falling below liabilities.**

**Key Principle:**
> Structural: asset value vs liabilities. Reduced-form: observable variables.

# QF3 — Rating Categories (Expected Excess Return)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF3*

### Q261 — Highest Expected Excess Return With Stable Spreads [QF-15]

**Context (QF3):**
| Rating | OAS | Expected loss (POD×LGD) | EffSpreadDur |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | 1.00% | 0.10% | 7 |
| BBB | 1.75% | 0.75% | 6 |
| BB | 2.75% | 2.50% | 5 |

**Question:**
Which bond rating category offers the highest expected excess return if credit spreads remain stable under current market conditions?

A. A rated bond category
B. BBB rated bond category
C. BB rated bond category

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A: 0.90%.
- **B is correct.** **BBB.** With ΔSpread = 0, E[excess] = OAS − expected loss: A 0.90%, BBB 1.00%, BB 0.25%.
- **C is wrong.** BB: 0.25%.

**Flag:** The key's explanation calls the lowest category 'B rated'; the table says BB.

**Key Principle:**
> Stable spreads: excess ≈ OAS − POD × LGD.

### Q262 — Active Portfolio That Maximizes Expected Excess Return [QF-16]

**Context (QF3):**
| Rating | OAS | Expected loss (POD×LGD) | EffSpreadDur |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | 1.00% | 0.10% | 7 |
| BBB | 1.75% | 0.75% | 6 |
| BB | 2.75% | 2.50% | 5 |
Benchmark: equal-weighted across the three categories.

**Question:**
Which active bond portfolio maximizes expected excess return under a stable credit market assumption versus an equally weighted benchmark portfolio across the three rating categories?

A. 50% A rated bonds, 50% BBB rated bonds
B. 50% BBB rated bonds, 50% BB rated bonds
C. 50% A rated bonds, 50% BB rated bonds

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **50% A, 50% BBB.** Expected excess = (0.90% + 1.00%)/2 = 0.95% vs the benchmark's (0.90 + 1.00 + 0.25)/3 = 0.7167%, i.e. +0.233%.
- **B is wrong.** 50% BBB/50% BB has (1.00 + 0.25)/2 = 0.625%, below the benchmark.
- **C is wrong.** 50% A/50% BB has (0.90 + 0.25)/2 = 0.575%, below the benchmark.

**Key Principle:**
> Compare weighted E[excess spread] with the benchmark's.

### Q263 — Highest Expected Excess Return if Spreads Rise 10% [QF-17]

**Context (QF3):**
| Rating | OAS | Expected loss (POD×LGD) | EffSpreadDur |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | 1.00% | 0.10% | 7 |
| BBB | 1.75% | 0.75% | 6 |
| BB | 2.75% | 2.50% | 5 |
Spreads rise 10% in relative terms (A +0.10%, BBB +0.175%, BB +0.275%).

**Question:**
Which bond rating category offers the highest expected excess return if spreads rise 10% across all ratings categories?

A. A rated bond category
B. BBB rated bond category
C. BB rated bond category

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **A.** E[excess] = OAS − SpreadDur × ΔSpread − POD × LGD: A 0.200%; BBB −0.050%; BB −1.125%.
- **B is wrong.** BBB: −0.050%.
- **C is wrong.** BB: −1.125%.

**Key Principle:**
> Widening hurts long-spread-duration, low-quality bonds most.

# Standalone — Liquidity and Tail Risk

### Q264 — Addressing Liquidity Risk of an Illiquid Bond [QF-18]

**Question:**
Which of the following strategies best addresses the liquidity risk of a less frequently traded bond position in an active manager’s portfolio?

A. Enter into a receive fixed, pay floating asset swap, unwinding the swap position once the illiquid bond position is sold.
B. Sell single-name CDS protection on the illiquid bond issuer, unwinding the CDS contract when the bond is sold.
C. Allocate the illiquid bond to the buy-and-hold portion of the investment portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A and B are long-risk positions that would increase, not offset, the rate/credit exposure.
- **B is wrong.** A and B are long-risk positions that would increase, not offset, the exposure.
- **C is correct.** **Allocate it to the buy-and-hold portion.** Doesn't require selling it.

**Key Principle:**
> Illiquid positions → buy-and-hold sleeve.

### Q265 — Methods for Assessing Tail Risk [QF-19]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements best describes methods for assessing portfolio tail risk?

A. Parametric methods use expected value and standard deviation of risk factors under a normal distribution and are well suited for option-based portfolios.
B. Historical simulation methods use historical parameters and ranking results and are not well suited for option-based portfolios.
C. Monte Carlo methods generate random outcomes using portfolio measures and sensitivities and are well suited for option-based portfolios.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Parametric methods aren't well suited for options or non-normal returns.
- **B is wrong.** Historical simulation assumes no distribution and accommodates options.
- **C is correct.** **Monte Carlo** generates random outcomes from portfolio measures and sensitivities, suited to option-based portfolios.

**Key Principle:**
> Options → Monte Carlo or historical; parametric assumes normality.

# QF4 — 12-Year Corporate Bond (VaR)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF4*

### Q266 — VaR Measure for a New Position [QF-20]

**Question:**
Which of the following VaR measures is most appropriate for the portfolio manager to use to evaluate how this position would affect portfolio tail risk?

A. CVaR
B. Relative VaR
C. Incremental VaR

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** CVaR is the average loss beyond the VaR cutoff.
- **B is wrong.** Relative VaR is the VaR of active (relative) returns.
- **C is correct.** **Incremental VaR** measures how the added position changes the portfolio's VaR.

**Flag:** The key doesn't explain B or A; the one-line definitions here are standard, not from the key.

**Key Principle:**
> Incremental VaR = change in portfolio VaR from adding a position.

### Q267 — One-Month 99% VaR for a Bond Position [QF-21]

**Context (QF4):**
$75m face; price 104.0175; modified duration 9.887; daily yield volatility 1.50 bps; 2.33 SDs; 21 days.

**Question:**
What is the approximate VaR for the bond position at a 99% confidence interval (equal to 2.33 standard deviations) for one month (with 21 trading days) if daily yield volatility is 1.50 bps and returns are normally distributed?

A. $1,234,105
B. $2,468,210
C. $5,413,133

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **$1,234,105.** Yield move = 1.5 bps × 2.33 × √21 ≈ 16 bps. VaR = $75m × 1.040175 × 9.887 × 0.0016.
- **B is wrong.** Twice the correct VaR.
- **C is wrong.** Doesn't match.

**Key Principle:**
> VaR ≈ MV × ModDur × (σ_yield × z × √t).

# Standalone — CDS Pricing

### Q268 — CDS Pricing at Inception [QF-22]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements best describes how a single-name CDS contract is priced at inception?

A. If the reference entity’s credit spread trades below the standard coupon rate, the CDS contract will be priced at a premium above par because the protection buyer pays a “below market” periodic coupon.
B. If the reference entity’s credit spread trades above the standard coupon rate, the CDS contract will be priced at a discount to par because the protection seller effectively receives a “below market” periodic premium.
C. Similar to fixed-rate bonds, CDS contracts are initially priced at par with a fixed coupon and a price that changes over time as the reference entity’s credit spreads change.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** When the spread is below the coupon, the buyer pays an above-market premium (price at a premium to par).
- **B is correct.** **Spread above standard coupon → priced at a discount.** The protection seller receives a below-market premium. E.g., spread 1.50% vs coupon 1.00%: discount = 0.50% × CDS spread duration × notional.
- **C is wrong.** Not the key's answer: CDS prices change over time but are not initially priced at par if spread ≠ standard coupon.

**Flag:** The key's explanation for the keyed answer gives an example where spread is above coupon; for A it says the buyer pays an 'above market' premium, which is a different framing from the option as worded.

**Key Principle:**
> CDS price ≈ 100 − (spread − coupon) × EffSpreadDur.

# QF5 — Single-Name CDS (Upfront Payment and Mark-to-Market)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF5*

### Q269 — Upfront Payment for a New CDS [QF-23]

**Context (QF5):**
Market credit spread 1.75% (10-year); standard IG coupon 1.00%; effective spread duration 8.75; basis ≈ 0.

**Question:**
What should the protection buyer expect to pay or receive to enter a new 10-year CDS contract?

A. The buyer should receive approximately 6.5625% of the notional.
B. The buyer should pay approximately 15.3125% of the notional.
C. The buyer should pay approximately 6.5625% of the notional.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The buyer pays, not receives, because the spread exceeds the coupon.
- **B is wrong.** 15.3125% = 8.75 × 1.75%, ignoring the 1.00% coupon.
- **C is correct.** **Buyer pays about 6.5625% of notional.** 8.75 × (1.75% − 1.00%) = 6.5625%; price = 93.4375.

**Key Principle:**
> Upfront = EffSpreadDur × (spread − standard coupon).

### Q270 — Mark-to-Market on Purchased CDS Protection [QF-24]

**Context (QF5):**
Spread falls from 1.75% to 1.60% right after purchase. Notional €10m; EffSpreadDur 8.75; price at inception 93.4375.

**Question:**
Once the manager purchases CDS protection, the issuer’s CDS spread immediately falls to 1.60%. What is the investor’s approximate mark-to-market gain or loss for a contract notional of €10,000,000?

A. The manager realizes an approximate loss of €131,250.
B. The manager realizes an approximate gain of €131,250.
C. The manager realizes an approximate gain of €525,000.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Loss of about €131,250.** New price = 100 − 8.75 × 0.60% = 94.75. Buyer is short credit risk; loss = (94.75 − 93.4375)/100 × €10m.
- **B is wrong.** Falling spreads hurt the protection buyer.
- **C is wrong.** €525,000 doesn't match.

**Key Principle:**
> Protection buyer gains when spreads widen, loses when they tighten.

# Standalone — Credit Positioning and Credit Curves

### Q271 — Underweighting Financials Using CDX [QF-25]

**Question:**
Which of the following credit portfolio positioning strategies is the most appropriate to underweight the financial sector versus an index?

A. Purchase protection on the CDX and sell protection on the CDX Financials subindex.
B. Sell protection on the CDX and purchase protection on the CDX Financials subindex.
C. Purchase a payer option on the CDX and sell protection on the CDX Financials subindex.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Increases financial exposure (the reverse).
- **B is correct.** **Sell protection on CDX and buy protection on CDX Financials.** Long index risk, offset by short risk in financial names.
- **C is wrong.** Increases financial exposure.

**Key Principle:**
> Underweight a sector: sell index protection, buy sector-subindex protection.

### Q272 — Credit Cycle Phase With Fewer Defaults [QF-26]

**Question:**
Which of the following phases of the credit cycle typically involves a decline in the number of issuer defaults?

A. Late expansion phase
B. Early expansion phase
C. Peak phase

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Late expansion.** Accelerating growth, peak profits, stable leverage, and declining defaults.
- **B is wrong.** Not the keyed answer.
- **C is wrong.** Not the keyed answer.

**Key Principle:**
> Late expansion: defaults declining, spreads tight.

### Q273 — Credit Spread Curve for High-Yield Issuers [QF-27]

**Question:**
Which of the following regarding the shape of the credit spread curve for high-yield issuers is most accurate?

A. High-yield credit spread curves change shape more over the cycle than investment-grade ones do and usually invert during the peak phase.
B. Investors should exercise caution in interpreting credit spread curve shape for distressed debt issuers because their bonds tend to trade on a price rather than credit spread basis as the likelihood of default increases.
C. High-yield credit spread curves often invert because of the empirical observation that DTS is the best way to measure high-yield bond price changes.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The high-yield curve tends to invert during a contraction, not the peak.
- **B is correct.** **Caution in reading curve shape for distressed issuers.** Their bonds trade on price (close to recovery rate) rather than spread as default likelihood rises.
- **C is wrong.** Inversion relates to near-term vs long-term default risk, not DTS.

**Key Principle:**
> Distressed bonds trade on price, so spread curve shape is unreliable.

### Q274 — Credit Curve Roll-Down Strategy [QF-28]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements best describes a credit curve roll-down strategy?

A. Returns from a credit curve roll-down strategy can be estimated by combining the incremental coupon from a longer maturity corporate bond with price appreciation due to the passage of time.
B. A synthetic credit curve roll-down strategy involves purchasing protection using a single-name CDS contract for a longer maturity.
C. A credit curve roll-down strategy is expected to generate a positive return if the credit spread curve is upward sloping.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Benchmark yield changes must be separated from credit spread changes.
- **B is wrong.** A synthetic roll-down involves selling (not buying) protection on a longer-maturity CDS.
- **C is correct.** **Positive return only if the spread curve is upward sloping.**

**Key Principle:**
> Credit roll-down: upward-sloping spread curve; synthetic = sell protection on longer maturity.

# QF6 — CDX Contracts (Curve and Contraction Positioning)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF6*

### Q275 — Positioning for an IG Credit Curve Steepening [QF-29]

**Context (QF6):**
| Contract | Tenor | CDS spread | EffSpreadDur |
|---|---|---|---|
| CDX IG | 5y | 85 bps | 4.9 |
| CDX IG | 10y | 135 bps | 8.9 |
| CDX HY | 5y | 175 bps | 4.7 |
| CDX HY | 10y | 275 bps | 8.7 |

**Question:**
Select the most appropriate credit portfolio positioning strategy to capitalize on an expected steepening of the investment-grade credit spread curve.

A. Sell protection on the 10-year CDX IG index and purchase protection on the 5-year CDX IG index using contracts of equal notional value.
B. Sell protection on the 10-year CDX IG index and purchase protection on the 5-year CDX IG index using a contract with a notional amount equal to 1.82 times that of the 10-year contract.
C. Buy protection on the 10-year CDX IG index and sell protection on the 5-year CDX IG index using a contract with a notional amount equal to 1.82 times that of the 10-year contract.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Reverse positions (benefit from flattening) with unequal durations.
- **B is wrong.** Right ratio but reversed positions.
- **C is correct.** **Buy 10y protection, sell 5y protection with 1.82× notional (8.9/4.9).** Short risk on the 10-year, long on the 5-year, duration matched.

**Key Principle:**
> Steepener: buy long-tenor protection, sell short-tenor protection; duration-match notionals.

### Q276 — Positioning for an Economic Contraction [QF-30]

**Context (QF6):**
| Contract | Tenor | CDS spread | EffSpreadDur |
|---|---|---|---|
| CDX IG | 5y | 85 bps | 4.9 |
| CDX IG | 10y | 135 bps | 8.9 |
| CDX HY | 5y | 175 bps | 4.7 |
| CDX HY | 10y | 275 bps | 8.7 |

**Question:**
Which of the following is the most appropriate credit portfolio positioning strategy to capitalize on an expected economic contraction?

A. Buy protection on the 5-year CDX HY index and sell protection on the 5-year CDX IG index in approximately equal notional amounts.
B. Buy protection on the 10-year CDX IG index and sell protection on the 5-year CDX IG index using a contract with a notional amount equal to 1.82 times that of the 10-year contract.
C. Buy protection on the 10-year CDX HY index and sell protection on the 5-year CDX HY index using a contract with a notional amount equal to 1.85 times that of the 10-year contract.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Buy 5y CDX HY protection, sell 5y CDX IG protection (equal notionals).** Contractions bring sharp rises in short-term HY spreads, IG flattening and HY inversion.
- **B is wrong.** Benefits from a steeper IG curve.
- **C is wrong.** Benefits from a steeper HY curve.

**Key Principle:**
> Contraction: short HY near-term risk, long IG.

# Standalone — International Credit Markets

### Q277 — International Credit Markets [QF-31]

**Question:**
Which of the following is the most accurate statement related to international credit markets?

A. Fixed exchange-rate regimes among emerging markets usually reduce the likelihood of financial distress because the domestic currency is tied to a major foreign currency.
B. Although many emerging economies have domestic bond markets that include sovereign, financial, and corporate issuers, investments across these bonds offer less diversification than similar investments in developed markets.
C. Higher domestic currency YTMs among emerging versus developed markets are due to expected currency appreciation resulting from higher economic growth.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Fixed exchange rate regimes usually result in greater instability and higher distress risk.
- **B is correct.** **Emerging market bonds across issuer types offer less diversification** than similar developed-market investments.
- **C is wrong.** Higher domestic-currency YTMs signal expected depreciation, not appreciation.

**Key Principle:**
> EM: fixed FX regimes risky; high yields reflect expected depreciation.

# QF7 — US and European Credit Portfolios (Excess Return and Positioning)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF7*

### Q278 — Unhedged Excess Return for a Four-Portfolio International Index [QF-32]

**Context (QF7):**
| | OAS | EffSpreadDur | Expected loss |
|---|---|---|---|
| USD IG | 1.25% | 4.50 | 0.40% |
| USD HY | 3.00% | 5.50 | 2.25% |
| EUR IG | 1.15% | 4.75 | 0.50% |
| EUR HY | 3.25% | 6.00 | 2.50% |
Equal weights; EUR expected to depreciate 2% vs USD; spreads and losses unchanged.

**Question:**
What is the expected unhedged excess return to the United States–based credit manager for an international credit portfolio index equally weighted across the four portfolio choices, assuming no change to spread duration and no changes to the expected loss occur?

A. –0.257%
B. –0.850%
C. 0.750%

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **−0.257%.** Excess spread = OAS − expected loss: USD IG 0.85%, USD HY 0.75%, EUR IG 0.65%, EUR HY 0.75%. USD half averages 0.80%. EUR half converted: (1 + (0.65% + 0.75%)/2) × 0.98 − 1 = −1.314%. Total = (0.80% − 1.314%)/2.
- **B is wrong.** Doesn't match the calculation.
- **C is wrong.** 0.750% ignores the currency loss.

**Key Principle:**
> R_DC = (1 + R_FC)(1 + R_FX) − 1 for the unhedged foreign sleeve.

### Q279 — Portfolio Positioned for an Earlier European Credit Recovery [QF-33]

**Question:**
Which of the following active portfolios is expected to have the highest excess return versus the index if European economies are expected to experience an earlier and much stronger credit cycle recovery than the United States?

A. EUR HY 50.0%, EUR IG 25.0%, USD IG 12.5%, USD HY 12.5%
B. EUR IG 50.0%, EUR HY 25.0%, USD IG 12.5%, USD HY 12.5%
C. EUR HY 33.3%, US HY 33.3%, EUR IG 16.7%, USD IG 16.7%

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **EUR HY 50%, EUR IG 25%, USD IG 12.5%, USD HY 12.5%.** High-yield spreads fall most in a recovery, so overweight EUR HY.
- **B is wrong.** Overweights EUR IG rather than EUR HY.
- **C is wrong.** Has less EUR HY (33.3%).

**Key Principle:**
> Recovery: overweight the region and quality (HY) with the most spread tightening.

# Standalone — Structured Products and High Yield vs Investment Grade

### Q280 — Structured Products in an Active Credit Portfolio [QF-34]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements about the role of structured products in an active credit portfolio is most accurate?

A. Covered bonds perform relatively well in a downturn versus other fixed-income bonds with real estate exposure because a covered bond investor also has recourse to the issuer.
B. Higher-rated ABS tranches are attractive for active investors seeking to overweight default risk when the credit cycle is in recovery.
C. CLO tranches are more advantageous than CDO tranches with similar ratings under an economic slowdown scenario.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Covered bonds perform relatively well in a downturn** vs other real-estate-exposed bonds because investors also have recourse to the issuer.
- **B is wrong.** Not the keyed answer.
- **C is wrong.** Not the keyed answer.

**Flag:** The key explains only the correct choice A; I've given no reason for B and C beyond 'not the keyed answer'.

**Key Principle:**
> Covered bonds = dual recourse (pool plus issuer).

### Q281 — Assumption Change That Hurts High Yield vs Investment Grade [QF-35]

**Question:**
An active fixed-income manager is evaluating the relative performance of an investment-grade corporate versus a high-yield corporate debt allocation in a fixed-income portfolio. Which of the following analytical model assumption changes is most likely to reduce the future value of the high-yield portfolio relative to the investment-grade holdings?

A. Steepening of the benchmark yield volatility curve
B. Decreased likelihood of an economic slowdown
C. Increased likelihood of a flight to quality associated with bullish benchmark yield curve flattening (long-term rates fall by more than short-term rates do)

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Not the keyed answer.
- **B is wrong.** A lower likelihood of slowdown would help, not hurt, high yield.
- **C is correct.** **Greater likelihood of a flight to quality (bullish flattening).** Lower government yields come with wider HY spreads from a higher likelihood and severity of distress.

**Key Principle:**
> Flight to quality: government yields ↓, HY spreads ↑.

# QF8 — CCHC / Larent (IG Risk, Z-Spread vs OAS, International and Structured Products)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF8*

### Q282 — Risk Considerations for Investment-Grade Bonds [QF-36]

**Context (QF8):**
Larent: (1) the primary component of credit risk is loss severity; (2) rating migration can cause spread risk to become realized; (3) interest rate risk reflects a positive correlation between risk-free rates and credit spreads.

**Question:**
With respect to investment-grade bonds, Larent is most likely correct with respect to which risk consideration?

A. Credit risk
B. Spread risk
C. Interest rate risk

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Credit loss is a lesser consideration than migration for IG (primary for HY).
- **B is correct.** **Spread risk.** For IG bonds, migration risk exceeds the risk of outright loss, so spread volatility matters most.
- **C is wrong.** For IG the correlation between spreads and risk-free rates is negative.

**Key Principle:**
> IG: spread risk (migration). HY: default loss. IG spreads and rates are negatively correlated.

### Q283 — Callable Bond From Z-Spread vs OAS [QF-37]

**Context (QF8):**
| | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Z-spread | 112 | 163 | 213 |
| OAS | 112 | 152 | 213 |

**Question:**
Which bond from Exhibit 1 is most likely callable?

A. Bond A
B. Bond B
C. Bond C

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Z-spread equals OAS, so likely non-callable.
- **B is correct.** **Bond B.** OAS (152) is below Z-spread (163); the difference reflects the call option's value.
- **C is wrong.** Z-spread equals OAS, so likely non-callable.

**Key Principle:**
> Z-spread − OAS = option cost; zero → no embedded option.

### Q284 — Best Relative Value When Spreads Are Expected to Widen [QF-38]

**Context (QF8):**
| | D | E | F |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rating | A | Baa | Ba |
| POD | 0.25% | 0.50% | 0.75% |
| Spread duration | 3.0 | 3.5 | 4.0 |
| Z-spread | 0.75% | 1.00% | 1.25% |
| Loss severity | 40% | 50% | 60% |
Expected Z-spread change +0.25% each; 1-year holding.

**Question:**
The bond in Exhibit 2 with the best relative value is most likely:

A. Bond D.
B. Bond E.
C. Bond F.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Bond D.** EXR = s·t − Δs·SD − t·p·L: D −0.100%, E −0.125%, F −0.200%. D has the smallest loss.
- **B is wrong.** E: −0.125%.
- **C is wrong.** F: −0.200%.

**Key Principle:**
> EXR = spread × t − ΔSpread × SD − t × POD × LGD.

### Q285 — Top-Down Statements by Larent [QF-39]

**Context (QF8):**
Larent: lower-quality bonds in cyclical sectors outperform when growth improves; effective duration can assess a yield curve steepening; underweight longer-maturity bonds within ratings given expected flattening of a wide spread curve.

**Question:**
In Larent’s discussion about the top-down approach to portfolio construction, she is most likely correct about:

A. assessing the impact of yield curve reshaping.
B. maturity weighting related to a change in spread curve.
C. the allocation of lower-quality bonds in a credit portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Effective duration measures parallel shifts, not steepening.
- **B is wrong.** When a wide spread curve flattens, the best approach is to overweight longer maturities and underweight shorter ones.
- **C is correct.** **Allocation of lower-quality bonds.** Overweighting lower quality when the economy improves is correct.

**Key Principle:**
> Effective duration = parallel shifts; use key rate durations for curve reshaping.

### Q286 — Factors in International Bond Investing [QF-40]

**Question:**
Which factors about investing in the bonds of international companies is Larent most likely correct about?

A. Credit rating
B. Credit cycles
C. Bankruptcy laws

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Ratings are generally capped by the sovereign rating (sovereign ceiling), so they aren't independent.
- **B is correct.** **Credit cycles.** Timing and location of credit cycle changes is an important top-down consideration; regions differ.
- **C is wrong.** Bankruptcy laws differ and are a source of risk for non-domestic investors.

**Key Principle:**
> Sovereign ceiling; legal differences; regional credit cycles.

### Q287 — Larent's Statements on Structured Instruments [QF-41]

**Context (QF8):**
Larent: AA CDOs offer value since spread reflects BB collateral default expectations; CDOs plus underlying bonds diversify; senior tranches should rise more than mezzanine as correlations rise.

**Question:**
Which of Larent’s statements about structured financial instruments is most likely correct? The statement about:

A. relative value.
B. diversification.
C. the value of the senior tranches.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Relative value.** A CDO valued differently from its collateral gives an arbitrage: short (or buy CDS on) the underlying bonds and buy the undervalued CDO.
- **B is wrong.** The CDO's collateral is the corporate bonds, so no diversification benefit.
- **C is wrong.** Per the key, the mezzanine tranche rises by more than the senior when correlations increase.

**Key Principle:**
> CDO vs collateral mispricing → arbitrage.

# QF9 — Goodwin / XBK (Credit Strategy, Excess Spread, Alternatives to Individual Bonds)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF9*

### Q288 — Goodwin's Credit Strategy Approach [QF-42] (Essay)

**Context (QF9):**
Goodwin is positive on the global economy, believes cyclical companies will benefit, and thinks the market overreacted to geopolitical events.

**Question:**
Determine whether she uses a bottom-up or top-down approach and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Top-down.** She focuses on global economic trends and geopolitics, i.e., broad macro factors (growth, real rates and inflation, volatility and risk appetite, spread changes, industry trends, geopolitical risk, currencies), rather than issuer-specific analysis.

**Key Principle:**
> Top-down: macro factors. Bottom-up: issuer-specific.

### Q289 — Alternative to Buying Individual Bonds [QF-43] (Essay)

**Context (QF9):**
Goodwin wants credit exposure while limiting liquidity risk in a rapidly changing market.

**Question:**
Identify one alternative to purchasing individual bonds.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**CDS** (e.g., CDX index) or **bond ETFs.** Liquid alternatives that allow quick adjustment.

**Key Principle:**
> Liquid alternatives to cash bonds: CDS/CDX, bond ETFs.

### Q290 — Highest Expected Excess Spread if Spreads Tighten 30% [QF-44] (Essay)

**Context (QF9):**
| | Bond 1 | Bond 2 | Bond 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rating | A2 | Baa2 | B2 |
| Eff. spread dur. | 5.25 | 8.00 | 3.75 |
| OAS | 100 bps | 225 bps | 430 bps |
| POD | 0.25% | 1.00% | 3.50% |
| Loss severity | 20% | 25% | 60% |
Spreads tighten 30% for all three.

**Question:**
Determine which bond has the highest expected excess spread and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

E[Excess spread] ≈ Spread₀ − EffSpreadDur × ΔSpread − POD × LGD.
- Bond 1: 1.00% − 5.25(−0.300%) − 0.25%(20%) = 2.53%
- Bond 2: 2.25% − 8.00(−0.675%) − 1.00%(25%) = **7.40%**
- Bond 3: 4.30% − 3.75(−1.290%) − 3.50%(60%) = 7.04%

**Bond 2**: its long spread duration, combined with a 30% proportional tightening of a 225 bp spread, outweighs Bond 3's higher spread.

**Key Principle:**
> E[ExcessSpread] ≈ Spread₀ − SpreadDur × ΔSpread − POD × LGD.

### Q291 — Highest Expected Excess Spread if Spreads Are Unchanged [QF-45] (Essay)

**Context (QF9):**
| | Bond 1 | Bond 2 | Bond 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rating | A2 | Baa2 | B2 |
| Eff. spread dur. | 5.25 | 8.00 | 3.75 |
| OAS | 100 bps | 225 bps | 430 bps |
| POD | 0.25% | 1.00% | 3.50% |
| Loss severity | 20% | 25% | 60% |

**Question:**
Determine which bond has the highest expected excess spread with constant spreads and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

With ΔSpread = 0: OAS − POD × LGD.
- Bond 1: 1.00% − 0.25%(20%) = 0.95%
- Bond 2: 2.25% − 1.00%(25%) = 2.00%
- Bond 3: 4.30% − 3.50%(60%) = **2.20%**

**Bond 3.**

**Key Principle:**
> Static spreads: excess spread = OAS − expected loss.

# QF10 — Eagle Investments / Nucor / Yeti (Spread Duration, Liquidity, Structured Instruments)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF10*

### Q292 — Argument Against Selling 30-Year BB for 5-Year BB Bonds [QF-46]

**Context (QF10):**
Nucor wants to cut spread duration toward the index without cutting portfolio yield; the trade sells 30-year BB bonds and buys 5-year BB bonds. Smith says the trade could lead to greater losses.

**Question:**
Which of the following best supports Smith’s argument against Nucor’s idea to sell 30-year BB-rated bonds and buy 5-year BB-rated bonds?

A. The trade is unlikely to reduce overall spread duration
B. The credit curve may invert for lower-quality bonds in a sell-off
C. The trade is unlikely to reduce overall DTS (duration times spread)

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The trade does reduce spread duration (selling longer, buying shorter, same rating).
- **B is correct.** **The credit curve may invert for lower-quality bonds in a sell-off.** Near-term default/downgrade risk is higher in a downturn, so high-yield spreads at the short end can widen more than at the long end.
- **C is wrong.** The trade also reduces DTS.

**Key Principle:**
> HY curves invert in a downturn: short-end spreads rise most.

### Q293 — Least Correct Liquidity Statement [QF-47]

**Context (QF10):**
S1: in high volatility, govts and higher-quality US corporates are much more liquid than lower-rated; S2: bonds with embedded options trade with lower liquidity; S3: liquidity is reduced when large new supply is issued.

**Question:**
Which of Smith’s statements is least likely correct?

A. Statement 1
B. Statement 2
C. Statement 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Correct: bid–ask spreads on governments and higher-rated corporates are lower.
- **B is wrong.** Correct: conventional bonds typically have lower bid–ask spreads than bonds with embedded options.
- **C is correct.** **Statement 3.** Bond liquidity is typically highest immediately after issuance.

**Key Principle:**
> Liquidity is highest right after issue and fades with seasoning.

### Q294 — Structured Instrument for Expected Wider Credit Spreads [QF-48]

**Context (QF10):**
Yeti expects wider credit spreads.

**Question:**
Given Yeti’s credit spread view, which structured financial instrument is most likely to be a prudent addition to the portfolio?

A. Covered bonds
B. Lower-rated tranches of ABS
C. Collateralized loan obligations (CLOs)

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Covered bonds.** Dual recourse (issuer and asset pool) and substitution of non-performing assets give lower credit risk.
- **B is wrong.** Lower-rated ABS tranches would likely underperform if spreads widen.
- **C is wrong.** CLOs are not dual recourse.

**Key Principle:**
> Spreads widening: covered bonds (dual recourse) > lower-rated ABS tranches.

# QF11 — Lee / Cook (UK G-Spread and I-Spread)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF11*

### Q295 — G-Spread of the UK Corporate Bond [QF-49]

**Context (QF11):**
| Issuer | Term | Coupon | YTM | ModDur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Corporate | 7y | 4.50% | 4.30% | 5.8 |
| Government | 5y | 2.50% | 2.65% | 4.6 |
| Government | 10y | 3.50% | 3.70% | 8.5 |
UK swap spreads: 5y 0.23%, 10y 0.47%.

**Question:**
The G-spread of the UK corporate bond is closest to:

A. 1.00%.
B. 1.23%.
C. 1.60%.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 1.00% doesn't match.
- **B is correct.** **1.23%.** Interpolated 7y = 2.65% + (2/5)(3.70% − 2.65%) = 3.07%. G-spread = 4.30% − 3.07%.
- **C is wrong.** 1.60% doesn't match.

**Key Principle:**
> G-spread = YTM − interpolated government yield.

### Q296 — I-Spread of the UK Corporate Bond [QF-50]

**Context (QF11):**
| Issuer | Term | Coupon | YTM | ModDur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Corporate | 7y | 4.50% | 4.30% | 5.8 |
| Government | 5y | 2.50% | 2.65% | 4.6 |
| Government | 10y | 3.50% | 3.70% | 8.5 |
UK swap spreads: 5y 0.23%, 10y 0.47%.

**Question:**
The I-spread of the UK corporate bond is closest to:

A. 0.90%.
B. 1.13%.
C. 1.37%.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **0.90%.** 5y swap = 2.65% + 0.23% = 2.88%; 10y swap = 3.70% + 0.47% = 4.17%. Interpolated 7y = 2.88% + (2/5)(4.17% − 2.88%) = 3.40%. I-spread = 4.30% − 3.40%.
- **B is wrong.** 1.13% doesn't match.
- **C is wrong.** 1.37% doesn't match.

**Key Principle:**
> I-spread = YTM − interpolated swap rate (govt yield + swap spread).

# QF12 — Hale / Choate (Strategy, Risk, Spread Measures, Tail Risk, Emerging Markets)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF12*

### Q297 — Ipswich's Strategy Approach [QF-51]

**Context (QF12):**
Choate evaluates relative value of securities within countries and sectors by expected excess return, and uses overall profitability, default rates and industry trends to allocate to sectors/asset classes.

**Question:**
Based on Choate’s comments regarding the Credit Opportunities Fund, which strategy approach(es) does Ipswich most likely follow?

A. Bottom-up
B. Top-down and bottom-up
C. Top-down with ESG considerations

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** He also applies top-down in asset allocation.
- **B is correct.** **Top-down and bottom-up.** Macro views (growth, default rates) guide allocation; individual relative value guides selection.
- **C is wrong.** No ESG considerations are mentioned.

**Key Principle:**
> Top-down: macro view then bonds; bottom-up: relative value within similar bonds.

### Q298 — IG vs HY Risk Considerations [QF-52]

**Context (QF12):**
Choate: IG bonds are more sensitive to rates and credit migration (spread volatility); HY less rate-sensitive, and monitoring outright market value is more critical given default losses.

**Question:**
Are Choate’s comments regarding the risk considerations for investment-grade bonds compared to high-yield bonds most likely correct?

A. Yes
B. No, he is incorrect with respect to high-yield bonds
C. No, he is incorrect with respect to investment-grade bonds

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Yes, correct.** IG has lower default risk and is more sensitive to rates and migration; HY emphasizes credit risk and market value.
- **B is wrong.** He is correct about HY.
- **C is wrong.** He is correct about IG.

**Key Principle:**
> IG → spread (migration) risk; HY → default loss/market value.

### Q299 — Spread Measures at Security and Portfolio Levels [QF-53]

**Context (QF12):**
Security level: spread over a linear interpolation of two on-the-run government bonds. Portfolio level: applicable to a diversified group of securities, with or without embedded options.

**Question:**
The spread measures Choate refers to in evaluating credit at the security and portfolio levels, respectively, are most likely the:

A. I-spread and OAS.
B. G-spread and OAS.
C. I-spread and Z-spread.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** I-spread uses swap rates, not government bonds.
- **B is correct.** **G-spread and OAS.** G-spread interpolates two on-the-run governments; OAS handles optionality across a diversified portfolio.
- **C is wrong.** I-spread uses swap rates; Z-spread can't compare bonds with and without options.

**Key Principle:**
> Security: G-spread. Portfolio: OAS.

### Q300 — Least Correct Tail-Risk Consideration [QF-54]

**Context (QF12):**
C1: assess liquidity via bid–ask, volume, fund flows; manage via position sizes, Treasuries, ETFs, derivatives. C2: scenario analysis (e.g., spread widening or replaying a past crisis). C3: tail risk is hard/costly to hedge by diversification; prefer CDS, which have a very low cost.

**Question:**
Which of Choate’s considerations regarding the precautions he can take in the portfolio is least likely correct?

A. Consideration 1
B. Consideration 2
C. Consideration 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Correct.
- **B is wrong.** Correct.
- **C is correct.** **Consideration 3 is incorrect.** Hedging tail risk through diversification is not difficult and adds only modest cost; CDS hedging is effective but costly.

**Key Principle:**
> Diversification: cheap tail-risk mitigation; CDS: effective but costly.

### Q301 — Implication of Including Emerging Market Credits [QF-55]

**Context (QF12):**
Portfolio holds 5% emerging market credit and 3% HY vs a benchmark with none (an IG corporate index).

**Question:**
An implication of Choate’s investment strategy is that relative to the benchmark, the portfolio most likely would have:

A. lower credit quality but higher recovery rates.
B. lower credit quality and a higher concentration in commodities.
C. low diversification benefit because of high credit cycle correlation.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** EM debt has lower recovery rates.
- **B is correct.** **Lower credit quality and higher concentration in commodities.** EM credit has commodity/bank/government-owned concentrations; creditor rights are less certain.
- **C is wrong.** Regional differences in credit cycles provide diversification.

**Key Principle:**
> EM credit: lower quality, commodity concentration, weaker creditor rights.

### Q302 — Expectations Implied by the Fund's Positioning [QF-56]

**Context (QF12):**
Exhibit: IG 82% (bm 100); EM 5%; HY 3%; CDO senior (Class A) −10%; CDO subordinated (Class B) +10%; MBS 10%.

**Question:**
Based on Choate’s final comments and the COF portfolio positions in Exhibit 1, Choate is most likely expecting:

A. improved real estate markets and higher interest rate volatility.
B. lower interest rate volatility and increasing default correlations.
C. lower interest rate volatility and decreasing default correlations.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** An MBS buyer expects lower, not higher, volatility.
- **B is correct.** **Lower interest rate volatility and increasing default correlations.** MBS holders benefit from lower volatility; short Class A / long Class B profits if default correlation rises (mezzanine value rises relative to senior).
- **C is wrong.** Short Class A / long Class B implies higher, not lower, default correlation.

**Key Principle:**
> MBS: short volatility. Higher default correlation → mezzanine up relative to senior.

# QF13 — West / Stone / Palma (Risk, Spread Quotes, Top-Down Reports, Liquidity, Out-of-Benchmark)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QF13*

### Q303 — Least Correct Risk Comment by West [QF-57]

**Context (QF13):**
C1: IG bonds of the same duration are more sensitive to rates than HY; C2: the four Cs assess both PD and LGD; C3: spread duration is more useful for HY than IG.

**Question:**
Which of West’s comments regarding risk considerations in corporate bonds is least likely correct?

A. Comment 1
B. Comment 2
C. Comment 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Correct: IG moves in lockstep with rates; HY is quoted in prices/yields and moves less with rates.
- **B is wrong.** Correct: the four Cs assess creditworthiness, thus PD and LGD.
- **C is correct.** **Comment 3 is incorrect.** Spread duration measures sensitivity to credit spread changes, which is more relevant for IG, where spread volatility rather than outright default loss matters.

**Key Principle:**
> IG: spread duration; HY: default loss and price level.

### Q304 — Spread Quoted to a Treasury of Non-Matching Maturity [QF-58]

**Context (QF13):**
Dealers quote a spread to Treasuries whose maturity doesn't match the bond's.

**Question:**
In Island’s conversation with West, the spread measure that dealers often quote Island is most likely the:

A. I-Spread.
B. G-spread.
C. benchmark spread.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** I-spread uses same-currency swap rates.
- **B is wrong.** G-spread uses an actual or interpolated government bond.
- **C is correct.** **Benchmark spread.** Yield minus the yield of a recent on-the-run benchmark bond; maturity mismatch is the problem.

**Key Principle:**
> Benchmark spread = yield − nearest on-the-run govt yield.

### Q305 — Most Useful Data Item With Unchanged Spreads [QF-59]

**Context (QF13):**
Candidate items: expected PD, rating agency credit outlook, historical sector default rates.

**Question:**
Assuming no change in credit spreads, which data item that the analysts provide Stone would portfolio managers most likely find useful in their analysis?

A. Rating agency credit outlook
B. Expected probability of default
C. Historical sector default rates

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** The outlook doesn't enter the excess return formula.
- **B is correct.** **Expected probability of default.** EXR ≈ s·t − Δs·SD − t·p·L; with Δs = 0, p and L are the key unknowns.
- **C is wrong.** Historical sector default rates are used in top-down analysis.

**Key Principle:**
> Unchanged spreads: relative value = spread vs expected loss (PD × LGD).

### Q306 — West's Top-Down Report Structure [QF-60]

**Context (QF13):**
Section 1 macro factors: corporate profitability, economic growth, currency movements, expected volatility changes, key rate durations, default rates. Section 2 risk measures: average rating, average spread duration, DTS, average OAS, duration, effective convexity.

**Question:**
Is West’s two-section report regarding the top-down approach most likely correctly structured?

A. Yes
B. No. She is incorrect with respect to the macro factors
C. No, she is incorrect with respect to the risk measurement factors

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Section 1 includes key rate durations.
- **B is correct.** **No, incorrect on macro factors.** Key rate durations measure exposure to non-parallel curve shifts; they aren't a macro factor.
- **C is wrong.** The risk measurements are correct.

**Key Principle:**
> Macro factors ≠ portfolio risk measures (KRD is a risk measure).

### Q307 — Stone's Liquidity Comments [QF-61]

**Context (QF13):**
Stone's three points: (1) trading volume has declined, so liquidity management is less relevant to alpha; (2) spread changes are more pronounced in HY outflows, so macro forecasting helps position for liquidity risk; (3) bid–ask spreads vary over time and are a good indicator of liquidity; wider bid–ask spreads in a downturn create opportunities for PMs to add value.

**Question:**
Stone’s comments to West regarding liquidity risk in credit markets is most likely correct with regard to:

A. spread changes.
B. bid–ask spreads.
C. liquidity management.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Spread changes** (his second point) is correct. Point 1 is wrong: liquidity management has become more relevant to generating alpha. Point 3 is right that bid–ask varies and indicates liquidity but wrong that wider spreads benefit PMs; trading costs are higher.
- **B is wrong.** His third point is wrong: more volatile markets widen bid–ask spreads, so trading costs detract from performance.
- **C is wrong.** His first point is wrong: liquidity management has become more relevant to adding alpha.

**Key Principle:**
> Outflows hit HY spreads harder; wider bid–ask raises trading costs.

### Q308 — Palma's Out-of-Benchmark Investment Suggestions [QF-62]

**Context (QF13):**
Stone's three suggestions concern: (A) the credit cycle, (B) spread advantage, (C) local currency investment.

**Question:**
Which of the suggestions Stone outlines for Palma in selecting the proposed investments is most likely correct? The one regarding:

A. the credit cycle is most likely correct.
B. spread advantage is most likely correct.
C. local currency investment is most likely correct.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Credit cycles vary across regions, which can be used to diversify.
- **B is correct.** **The spread advantage point is correct.** Hedging FX can offset much of the yield pickup; pegged currencies may not need hedging.
- **C is wrong.** For pegged currencies, FX hedging may not be needed (exchange rate more stable).

**Key Principle:**
> Foreign yield pickup must be net of hedging cost.

# Standalone — Credit Strategies, Spreads, CDS, Structured Products

### Q309 — Credit Spread Curve of High-Grade Issuers at Economic Peaks [QF-63]

**Question:**
During economic peaks, the credit spread curve of high-grade issuers will most likely:

A. steepen.
B. remain stable.
C. become inverted.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Steepen.** Peaks have stable profitability and defaults, and the spread curve steepens (long spread minus short spread widens).
- **B is wrong.** Stable curves are typical for high-grade issuers in early expansion/recovery.
- **C is wrong.** Inverted curves are for low-grade issuers in contraction or early expansion.

**Key Principle:**
> Peak: IG curve steepens. Contraction: HY curve inverts.

### Q310 — Lower Recovery Rate and the Credit Spread [QF-64]

**Context:**
Spread ≈ LGD × POD; LGD = EE × (1 − RR).

**Question:**
If the recovery rate of an investment-grade bond falls, the bond's credit spread will most likely:

A. contract.
B. remain unchanged.
C. widen.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Spread widens, not contracts.
- **B is wrong.** LGD changes, so spread changes.
- **C is correct.** **Widen.** A lower recovery rate raises LGD, so spread widens.

**Key Principle:**
> ↓ RR → ↑ LGD → ↑ spread.

### Q311 — Yield Spread vs G-Spread [QF-65]

**Question:**
The yield spread for a corporate bond will be the same as the G-spread for that bond if the government benchmark yield curve is:

A. downward sloping.
B. flat.
C. upward sloping.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** They differ if the curve is downward sloping.
- **B is correct.** **Flat.** Yield spread uses an on-the-run government bond of similar maturity; G-spread interpolates. Same if the curve is flat.
- **C is wrong.** They differ if the curve is upward sloping.

**Key Principle:**
> Flat government curve → yield spread = G-spread.

### Q312 — Models Using Observable Company-Specific Financial Ratios [QF-66]

**Question:**
Which of the following statistical credit models solve for the probability of default using observable company-specific financial ratios?

A. Only structural credit models
B. Only reduced form credit models
C. Both structural credit models and reduced form credit models

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Structural models use market-based variables for asset value and volatility.
- **B is correct.** **Only reduced-form models.** They solve for default intensity from financial ratios, recovery assumptions and macro variables.
- **C is wrong.** Only reduced-form models use company-specific ratios.

**Key Principle:**
> Reduced-form: observable ratios + macro. Structural: asset value vs liabilities.

### Q313 — Key Financial Ratios [QF-67]

**Context:**
T1: Debt/Capital is more relevant for HY than IG. T2: EBITDA/Interest isn't suitable for firms with high cash flow variability. T3: EBITDA/Total Assets takes account of working capital changes.

**Question:**
At a training workshop, the following comments are made in relation to key financial ratios:

| Trainee 1 | The Debt/Capital (or leverage) ratio is more relevant for analyzing high-yield issuers than investment-grade issuers. |
|---|---|
| Trainee 2 | The EBITDA/Interest Expense (or coverage) ratio is not suitable for analyzing firms with high cash flow variability. |
| Trainee 3 | The EBITDA/Total Assets (or leverage) ratio takes account of working capital changes. |

Which trainee’s statement is most accurate?

A. Trainee 1
B. Trainee 2
C. Trainee 3

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Debt/Capital is more relevant for IG than HY.
- **B is correct.** **Trainee 2.** The coverage ratio is volatile for firms with highly variable cash flow.
- **C is wrong.** EBITDA ignores capital expenditures and working capital changes.

**Key Principle:**
> Leverage ratios: IG. Coverage ratios: HY (but volatile when cash flows are variable).

### Q314 — Rating Category to Underweight [QF-68]

**Context:**
| | Current OAS | Expected ΔOAS | Expected loss | EffSpreadDur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A | 0.80% | −0.20% | 0.05% | 6.0 |
| Baa | 1.25% | −0.50% | 0.50% | 5.5 |
| Ba | 2.40% | −0.38% | 0.80% | 4.5 |

**Question:**
An investor has formed expectations for the following bond rating categories and intends to underweight the rating category with the lowest expected excess return over the coming year:

| Rating Category | Current OAS | Expected ∆OAS | Expected Loss (POD × LGD) | Effective Spread Duration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A | 0.80% | −0.20% | 0.05% | 6.0 |
| Baa | 1.25% | −0.50% | 0.50% | 5.5 |
| Ba | 2.40% | −0.38% | 0.80% | 4.5 |

Assuming no change in spread duration over the coming year, which rating category ​​​​​​should the investor underweight?

A. A
B. Baa
C. Ba

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **A.** E[excess] = OAS − SD × ΔOAS − EL: A 0.80 + 1.20 − 0.05 = 1.95%, the lowest.
- **B is wrong.** Baa: 1.25 + 2.75 − 0.50 = 3.50%.
- **C is wrong.** Ba: 2.40 + 1.71 − 0.80 = 3.31%.

**Key Principle:**
> Underweight the lowest expected excess spread.

### Q315 — Active Portfolio With Stable Spreads [QF-69]

**Context:**
| | OAS | Expected loss | EffSpreadDur |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | 1.5% | 0.2% | 4.0 |
| BBB | 2.0% | 0.8% | 5.0 |
| BB | 3.5% | 2.8% | 6.0 |
Excess spread: A 1.3%, BBB 1.2%, BB 0.7%. Benchmark (equal weights): 1.067%.

**Question:**
An investor is considering an active portfolio decision across the following bond rating categories:

| Rating Category | Current OAS | Expected Loss (POD × LGD) | Effective Spread Duration |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | 1.5% | 0.2% | 4.0 |
| BBB | 2.0% | 0.8% | 5.0 |
| BB | 3.5% | 2.8% | 6.0 |

The credit spreads are expected to remain stable under the current market conditions. Which of the following active bond portfolios is the investor most likely to choose if the objective is to maximize the expected excess return across these rating categories?

A. An equally weighted portfolio of A rated bonds and BB rated bonds
B. An equally weighted portfolio of A rated bonds and BBB rated bonds
C. An equally weighted portfolio of BBB rated bonds and BB rated bonds

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A/BB: (1.3% + 0.7%)/2 = 1.00%, −0.067% vs the benchmark.
- **B is correct.** **50% A, 50% BBB.** (1.3% + 1.2%)/2 = 1.25%, +0.183% vs the benchmark.
- **C is wrong.** BBB/BB: (1.2% + 0.7%)/2 = 0.95%, −0.117% vs the benchmark.

**Key Principle:**
> Stable spreads: excess spread = OAS − POD × LGD.

### Q316 — Disadvantage of the I-Spread [QF-70]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements about the disadvantages of I-spread is most accurate? I-spread:

A. has maturity mismatch biases.
B. fails to use the term structure of interest rates to estimate yield spreads.
C. uses government yields as benchmark and lacks consistency over time.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Maturity mismatch bias is a yield spread drawback; the I-spread addresses it.
- **B is correct.** **It fails to use the term structure of interest rates;** it uses a point on the curve, and is limited to option-free bonds.
- **C is wrong.** Government-benchmark inconsistency over time is a yield spread drawback.

**Key Principle:**
> I-spread: point on the swap curve, option-free only. Z-spread uses the full curve.

### Q317 — Why OAS Is the Most Appropriate Spread Measure [QF-71]

**Question:**
Option-adjusted spread (OAS) is the most appropriate yield spread measure for active fixed-income portfolios because:

A. the measure is not dependent on volatility.
B. bonds with embedded options will realize the spread implied by the bond’s option-adjusted spread.
C. the measure provides a consistent basis for comparing yield spreads for option-free and structured fixed income instruments.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** OAS depends heavily on volatility and model assumptions.
- **B is wrong.** OAS is theoretical; bonds with options are unlikely to realize it.
- **C is correct.** **It gives a consistent basis for comparing option-free, callable, putable and structured bonds.**

**Key Principle:**
> OAS: comparability across bonds with and without options; model-dependent.

### Q318 — Strategy When Spreads Are Fair and Curves Stable [QF-72]

**Context:**
Credit spreads are correctly priced; credit curves stable for six months.

**Question:**
An active credit manager believes that current credit spreads are correctly priced and that the credit curves will remain stable during the next six months. The most appropriate strategy is to:

A. buy credit default swaps for longer maturities.
B. rebalance the portfolio to improve the portfolio's average credit rating.
C. increase the investment in longer-dated bonds with a similar rating to the current portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Should sell, not buy, longer-maturity CDS protection (adds spread duration).
- **B is wrong.** Should lower, not improve, the average rating.
- **C is correct.** **Increase investment in longer-dated bonds of similar rating** (adds spread duration to earn carry).

**Key Principle:**
> Static spreads: add spread duration or lower credit quality.

### Q319 — CDX Positioning for HY Spreads Widening More Than IG [QF-73]

**Question:**
An active credit portfolio manager believes that high-yield credit spreads will rise more than investment-grade credit spreads in the next year due to an economic slowdown. To generate excess return, the manager's best course of action is to:

A. sell protection on the high-yield credit default swap index.
B. buy protection on the investment-grade credit default swap index.
C. sell protection on the investment-grade credit default swap index and buy protection on the high-yield credit default swap index.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Should buy, not sell, HY protection.
- **B is wrong.** Should sell, not buy, IG protection.
- **C is correct.** **Sell IG CDX protection and buy HY CDX protection.**

**Key Principle:**
> Slowdown: short HY (buy protection), long IG (sell protection).

### Q320 — Fixed Income in Emerging Markets [QF-74]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements about fixed-income investments in emerging markets is most accurate?

A. External debt to GDP is a key measure of financial leverage
B. Geopolitical risk refers to the ability and willingness of issuers to repay debt
C. The largest company's credit rating in emerging markets is generally higher than the sovereign credit rating of its domicile

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **External debt to GDP is a key leverage measure** (with reserves/GDP as a liquidity measure).
- **B is wrong.** Describes sovereign credit risk, not geopolitical risk.
- **C is wrong.** A sovereign ceiling normally caps corporate ratings at the sovereign's rating.

**Key Principle:**
> EM: external debt/GDP, reserves/GDP; sovereign ceiling.

### Q321 — Structured Products for an Economic Recovery With Fewer Defaults [QF-75]

**Question:**
An active fixed-income portfolio manager anticipates an economic recovery accompanied by lower than expected defaults. Which of the following portfolio positioning strategies involving structured products is the most appropriate?

A. Underweight credit exposure using a higher-rated tranche of a collateralized loan obligation
B. Increase exposure to covered bonds and decrease exposure to mortgage-backed securities
C. Decrease exposure to A-rated asset-backed securities tranche and increase exposure to BBB-rated asset-backed securities tranche

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Underweighting credit with higher-rated tranches is a downturn position.
- **B is wrong.** Covered bonds over MBS reduces credit risk, wrong for a recovery.
- **C is correct.** **Reduce A-rated ABS tranche, add BBB-rated ABS tranche.** Overweights default risk, benefiting from greater spread tightening in lower-rated tranches.

**Key Principle:**
> Recovery: add credit risk via lower-rated tranches.

### Q322 — Structured Products Positioning Statements [QF-76]

**Question:**
Which of the following statements about portfolio positioning strategies involving structured products is most accurate?

A. Covered bonds involve higher credit risk and higher yield than asset-backed securities
B. Collateralized loan obligation tranches benefit more from short-term rate rises than collateralized debt obligation tranches
C. Portfolio manager should overweight credit exposure using lower-rated asset-backed securities tranche in an economic downturn

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Covered bonds have lower credit risk and lower yield than ABS.
- **B is correct.** **CLO tranches benefit more from short-term rate rises than CDO tranches.** CLOs hold leveraged loans paying MRR plus a spread.
- **C is wrong.** In a downturn the manager would underweight, not overweight, credit via lower-rated tranches.

**Key Principle:**
> CLO: floating-rate loans → benefit from rate rises.

### Q323 — Conditional VaR [QF-77]

**Question:**
A one-day Conditional Value at Risk (1%) of a portfolio can be best described as the:

A. the minimum one-day loss expected to occur 99% of the time.
B. the average one-day loss if the Value at Risk cutoff is exceeded.
C. change in portfolio Value at Risk when a new position is added to the portfolio.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** That describes VaR.
- **B is correct.** **Average one-day loss if the VaR cutoff is exceeded** (expected tail loss/expected shortfall).
- **C is wrong.** That describes incremental VaR.

**Key Principle:**
> CVaR = average loss beyond the VaR cutoff.

### Q324 — Instruments for Rapidly Changing Markets [QF-78]

**Question:**
An active bond manager expects market conditions to change rapidly. For short-term tactical positioning, the manager is most likely to prefer:

A. bond ETFs.
B. off-the-run government bonds.
C. corporate bonds with long records of successful coupon payments.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Bond ETFs** (and CDS) are liquid alternatives for quick tactical moves.
- **B is wrong.** Active managers favor on-the-run governments for tactical positioning.
- **C is wrong.** Seasoned illiquid positions are for buy-and-hold.

**Key Principle:**
> Tactical: liquid instruments (ETFs, CDS, on-the-run). Strategic: illiquid.

### Q325 — Effect of Index Derivatives Used for Downside Protection [QF-79]

**Question:**
The use of index derivatives to protect against downside risk in a fixed-income portfolio reduces:

A. portfolio concentration.
B. excess portfolio spread over time.
C. hedging costs in a distressed market.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Concentration is reduced by position limits and risk budgeting, not by derivatives.
- **B is correct.** **Reduces excess portfolio spread over time** (swaption/CDS-swaption premiums are a drag).
- **C is wrong.** Hedging in distressed markets costs more because of higher option volatility.

**Key Principle:**
> Option-based protection costs premium → lowers excess spread.

### Q326 — Positioning for a Steepening Credit Curve [QF-80]

**Question:**
An active credit manager expects the credit curve slope to steepen. To benefit from the view, the manager will most likely:

A. only sell long-term protection on a CDS subindex.
B. buy short-term protection on a CDS subindex and sell long-term protection on the same subindex.
C. sell short-term protection using a single-name CDS contract and buy long-term protection on that same reference entity.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** To benefit from steepening you must buy long-term protection, not only sell it.
- **B is wrong.** That is a flattening trade.
- **C is correct.** **Sell short-term protection and buy long-term protection on the same single-name reference entity.**

**Key Principle:**
> Steepener: sell short-tenor, buy long-tenor protection. Flattener: the reverse.

### Q327 — Positioning Near the End of a Growth Cycle [QF-81]

**Question:**
A credit portfolio manager believes the economy is nearing the end of a growth cycle. The manager will most likely:

A. buy short-term CDS protection on a subindex and sell long-term protection on the same subindex.
B. sell short-term protection on a single-name CDS contract and buy long-term protection on that same reference entity.
C. buy five-year protection on a high-yield CDS index and sell protection on an investment-grade CDS index of the same tenor.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Buy short-term protection and sell long-term protection on the same subindex.** Near-term spreads rise, flattening the CDS curve.
- **B is wrong.** That capitalizes on a steepening view (falling near-term default probability).
- **C is wrong.** That is a weaker-economy view: HY spreads widening vs IG.

**Key Principle:**
> Late cycle: CDS curve flattening.

### Q328 — CDX Pair When Slowdown Hurts Lower-Rated Issuers Most [QF-82]

**Question:**
An active fixed-income manager anticipates an economic slowdown in the coming year with a greater adverse impact on lower-rated issuers. There are two credit default swap index (CDX) contracts available, namely the investment-grade CDX and high-yield CDX contracts. If the manager chooses a tactical strategy combining positions in investment-grade CDX and high-yield CDX contracts to capitalize on this view, the manager will most likely:

A. buy protection on both high-yield CDX contracts and investment-grade CDX contracts.
B. buy protection on high-yield CDX contracts and sell protection on investment-grade CDX contracts.
C. buy protection on investment-grade CDX contracts and sell protection on high-yield CDX contracts.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is the keyed answer.** The key's feedback text for this choice says it is **incorrect**: buying protection on both contracts protects against the slowdown but doesn't capitalize on a greater impact on lower-rated issuers.
- **B is marked incorrect in the key.** The key's feedback text for this choice says it is **correct**: buy HY protection and sell IG protection captures the greater hit to lower-rated issuers. This matches QF-73 and QF-30.
- **C is wrong.** Buys IG and sells HY protection, the opposite of the view.

**Flag:** Key contradiction: the 'Correct answer' line lists A, but the feedback text calls A incorrect and B correct, and QF-73/QF-30 support B. I'd treat B as intended.

**Key Principle:**
> Slowdown hitting low-rated issuers harder: buy HY protection, sell IG protection.

# QG1 — Harding / Yellow (Trading Strategy, Benchmarks, Algorithms, Costs, Policy)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG1*

### Q329 — Factors Affecting Trading Strategy Selection [QG-1]

**Context (QG1):**
S1: greater urgency → lower execution risk. S2: larger orders with higher urgency reduce market impact. S3: high alpha decay requires less aggressive trading.

**Question:**
Which of Yellow’s statements regarding the factors affecting the selection of a trading strategy is correct?

A. Statement 1
B. Statement 2
C. Statement 3

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Statement 1.** Faster execution means less time exposed to price volatility and changing conditions.
- **B is wrong.** Larger, more urgent orders raise (not reduce) market impact.
- **C is wrong.** High alpha decay calls for more aggressive (faster) trading.

**Key Principle:**
> ↑ urgency → ↓ execution risk, ↑ market impact.

### Q330 — Benchmark That Excludes Trade Outliers [QG-2]

**Context (QG1):**
Benchmark price computed from market prices during the trading period, excluding outliers.

**Question:**
Given the parameters for the benchmark given by Harding, Yellow should recommend a benchmark that is based on the:

A. arrival price.
B. time-weighted average price.
C. volume-weighted average price.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Arrival price is a pre-trade benchmark.
- **B is correct.** **TWAP.** Intraday benchmark; chosen to exclude potential trade outliers.
- **C is wrong.** VWAP is also intraday but isn't the one used to exclude outliers.

**Key Principle:**
> Intraday: VWAP, TWAP (TWAP when outliers should be excluded).

### Q331 — Strategy for Filling the Rest of the ABC Order [QG-3]

**Context (QG1):**
Buy 30,000 ABC (~1% of expected daily volume, very liquid); part at the open auction, the rest executed at prices close to the market price when the order was received.

**Question:**
To fill the remaining portion of the ABC order, Yellow is using:

A. an arrival price trading strategy.
B. a TWAP participation strategy.
C. a VWAP participation strategy.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Arrival price strategy.** High urgency, liquid stock, small order vs volume.
- **B is wrong.** A TWAP participation strategy spreads trades over time.
- **C is wrong.** A VWAP participation strategy follows the volume schedule.

**Key Principle:**
> Arrival price strategy: trade near the price when the order arrives.

### Q332 — Algorithm for a Liquid Stock With Market-Impact Priority [QG-4]

**Context (QG1):**
XYZ shares are relatively liquid; priority is to minimize market impact/information leakage; no expected adverse price movements.

**Question:**
What type of algorithm should be used to purchase the XYZ shares given Harding’s priority in building the XYZ position and his belief about potential price movements?

A. Scheduled algorithm
B. Arrival price algorithm
C. Opportunistic algorithm

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Scheduled algorithm.** Appropriate when no adverse price move is expected, with more tolerance for longer execution and priority on minimizing market impact; works for small orders in liquid securities.
- **B is wrong.** Arrival price algorithms are for expected adverse price moves, trading aggressively.
- **C is wrong.** Opportunistic algorithms aren't the keyed choice here.

**Flag:** The key explains only the correct answer; the B reasoning is from QG-16's description of arrival price algorithms, and nothing is given for C.

**Key Principle:**
> Scheduled (VWAP/TWAP/POV): no adverse move expected, minimize impact.

### Q333 — Non-Equity Trade Implementation [QG-5]

**Context (QG1):**
S4: small currency and small exchange-traded derivatives trades typically use DMA. S5: high-touch agency approach is typically used for large, non-urgent trades in fixed income and exchange-traded derivatives.

**Question:**
Which of Yellow’s statements regarding the trade implementation of non-equity investments is correct?

A. Only Statement 4
B. Only Statement 5
C. Both Statement 4 and Statement 5

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Only Statement 4.** Small currency trades are usually via DMA, and buy-side traders generally use DMA for exchange-traded derivatives, particularly smaller trades.
- **B is wrong.** Statement 4 is also correct.
- **C is wrong.** Statement 5 is not correct per the key.

**Flag:** The key doesn't explain why Statement 5 is wrong.

**Key Principle:**
> Small FX/ETD trades: DMA.

### Q334 — Execution Cost of the BYYP Purchase [QG-6]

**Context (QG1):**
