Order: buy 120,000 BYYP; decision price $40.00; limit $42.00; released at $40.50; commission $0.02/share; 90,000 filled at an average of $41.42; close $42.50.
| Trade | Price | Shares |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | $40.75 | 10,000 |
| 2 | $41.25 | 30,000 |
| 3 | $41.50 | 20,000 |
| 4 | $41.75 | 30,000 |

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 1, the execution cost for purchasing the 90,000 shares of BYYP is:

A. $60,000.
B. $82,500.
C. $127,500.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Doesn't match.
- **B is wrong.** Doesn't match.
- **C is correct.** **$127,500.** Execution cost = Σ(shares × price) − shares × decision price = $3,727,500 − 90,000 × $40.00 = $127,500.

**Key Principle:**
> Execution cost = Σ s_j p_j − Σ s_j p_d (buy).

### Q335 — Opportunity Cost of the BYYP Purchase [QG-7]

**Context (QG1):**
Order: buy 120,000 BYYP; decision price $40.00; limit $42.00; released at $40.50; commission $0.02/share; 90,000 filled at an average of $41.42; close $42.50.
| Trade | Price | Shares |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | $40.75 | 10,000 |
| 2 | $41.25 | 30,000 |
| 3 | $41.50 | 20,000 |
| 4 | $41.75 | 30,000 |

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 1, the opportunity cost for purchasing the 90,000 shares of BYYP is:

A. $22,500.
B. $60,000.
C. $75,000.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Doesn't match.
- **B is wrong.** Doesn't match.
- **C is correct.** **$75,000.** Unexecuted shares × (closing price − decision price) = 30,000 × ($42.50 − $40.00).

**Key Principle:**
> Opportunity cost = (S − Σs_j)(p_n − p_d).

### Q336 — Arrival Cost of the BYYP Purchase [QG-8]

**Context (QG1):**
Order: buy 120,000 BYYP; decision price $40.00; limit $42.00; released at $40.50; commission $0.02/share; 90,000 filled at an average of $41.42; close $42.50.
| Trade | Price | Shares |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | $40.75 | 10,000 |
| 2 | $41.25 | 30,000 |
| 3 | $41.50 | 20,000 |
| 4 | $41.75 | 30,000 |

**Question:**
The arrival cost for purchasing the 90,000 shares of BYYP is:

A. 164.4 bp.
B. 227.2 bp.
C. 355.0 bp.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 164.4 bp doesn't match.
- **B is correct.** **227.2 bp.** Arrival cost = side × (average price − arrival price)/arrival price × 10,000 = (41.42 − 40.50)/40.50 × 10,000.
- **C is wrong.** 355.0 bp doesn't match.

**Flag:** The key's formula image didn't paste. I recomputed 227.2 bp using the stated average of $41.42 and arrival price $40.50. Using the exact average from Exhibit 1 ($41.4167) gives 226.3 bp.

**Key Principle:**
> Arrival cost (bp) = side × (avg exec − arrival price)/arrival price × 10,000.

### Q337 — Trade Policy Change to Implement [QG-9]

**Context (QG1):**
Yellow's proposals: (1) policy for treatment of trade errors; (2) policy that OTC derivatives are traded on venues with minimum price transparency rules; (3) eligible brokers only if they give the lowest possible trading cost.

**Question:**
As it relates to the trade policy document, ValleyRise should implement Yellow’s recommendation related to:

A. the list of eligible brokers.
B. a policy for the treatment of trade errors.
C. a policy for over-the-counter derivatives trades.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Not the keyed answer; the key gives no reason.
- **B is correct.** **A trade error policy.** Errors and any gains/losses must be disclosed to compliance and logged, resolved to avoid adverse client impact.
- **C is wrong.** Not the keyed answer; the key gives no reason.

**Flag:** The key explains only the trade error policy and doesn't say why the other two recommendations should not be implemented.

**Key Principle:**
> Trade policy should cover errors: report to compliance, log, resolve without harm to the client.

# QG2 — Morris / Ogive (Strategy Aggressiveness, Cash Management)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG2*

### Q338 — Aggressiveness of the Ogive Fund's Strategy [QG-10] (Essay)

**Context (QG2):**
Ogive: long-term outperformance vs the S&P 500 by buying undervalued companies; fundamentals-based persistent mispricings; three-year average holding period.

**Question:**
Determine his likely inclination to implement aggressively and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Not aggressive (measured).** Trade urgency is low because positions are held for years, little trading is needed, and necessary trades can be patient. Alpha decay is low because the long-term, value-oriented views are unlikely to be rapidly acted on by others.

**Key Principle:**
> Long holding period + persistent mispricing → low urgency, low alpha decay.

### Q339 — Cash Management After a Large Inflow [QG-11] (Essay)

**Context (QG2):**
Pengwyn Fund (small-cap value) received a very large inflow; Barker expects equity markets to drift higher near-term.

**Question:**
Describe an appropriate cash management strategy.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Equitization.** Temporarily invest the cash using equity futures or ETFs to get equity exposure and avoid cash drag, then gradually buy the underlying securities and unwind the futures/ETF positions. Useful when the underlying securities are too illiquid to buy immediately.

**Key Principle:**
> Equitization: futures/ETFs as a temporary bridge to avoid cash drag.

# QG3 — Sailors (Valley Ranch vs North Circle, Execution Risk)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG3*

### Q340 — Trade Strategy Selection Criteria: Valley Ranch vs North Circle [QG-12] (Essay)

**Context (QG3):**
| Feature | Valley Ranch | North Circle |
|---|---|---|
| Philosophy | Short-term long/short alpha, equity and non-equity | Long equity value |
| Trade size | Small | Large |
| Risk appetite | Low | Moderate to high |
| Venue | Listed only | Listed and non-listed |
| Bid–ask in downturn | Moderate-to-wide | Very wide |

**Question:**
Identify one difference between the two firms for each criterion: Order Characteristics, Security Characteristics, Market Conditions, Individual Risk Aversion.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

- **Order characteristics:** order size (larger at North Circle) and order side (North Circle skews to buys).
- **Security characteristics:** security type (North Circle trades only equities); short-term alpha focus at Valley Ranch; liquidity (North Circle buys non-listed securities).
- **Market conditions:** North Circle's non-listed investments have greater exposure to adverse market liquidity conditions.
- **Individual risk aversion:** Valley Ranch's managers are more risk averse than North Circle's.

**Key Principle:**
> Four criteria: order, security, market conditions, individual risk aversion.

### Q341 — Execution Risk and Market Impact by Order [QG-13] (Essay)

**Context (QG3):**
| Stock | Order size | Last | ADV | Volatility | Bid–ask |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ABC | 45,000 | $310.10 | 195,000 | Low | $309.75–$310.35 |
| DEF | 55,000 | $40.45 | 4,125,260 | Low | $40.39–$40.56 |
| XYZ | 8,000 | $101.94 | 750,850 | High | $100.82–$102.00 |

**Question:**
Which trade has the greatest execution risk and which the greatest market impact? Justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

- **Execution risk: XYZ.** Highest price volatility; execution risk (adverse price movement over the trading horizon) is often proxied by volatility.
- **Market impact: ABC.** Largest order as a share of ADV (45,000 / 195,000 = 23.1%); the larger the trade relative to ADV, the greater the expected market impact.

**Key Principle:**
> Execution risk ← volatility; market impact ← size relative to ADV.

# QG4 — Wong / SWM (Benchmarks, Algorithms, Implementation Shortfall)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG4*

### Q342 — Benchmark for the Music Plus Sell Order [QG-14]

**Context (QG4):**
Wong thinks Music Plus ($15.25) overreacted; fair value $14.20; expects reversion within the day; decides to sell when the price is $15.22.

**Question:**
The most appropriate price benchmark for the sell order of Music Plus shares is the:

A. closing price.
B. decision price.
C. time-weighted average price (TWAP).

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Closing price is a post-trade benchmark used by index managers/mutual funds.
- **B is correct.** **Decision price.** Pre-trade benchmark for short-term alpha trades; she has an exact record of the price at decision ($15.22).
- **C is wrong.** TWAP excludes outliers; she wants to sell near the day's high, which would likely be an outlier.

**Key Principle:**
> Short-term alpha trade → decision (or arrival) price benchmark.

### Q343 — Trading Strategy for the Music Plus Sell Order [QG-15]

**Context (QG4):**
Order is ~20% of expected daily volume, high urgency, submitted after the open.

**Question:**
The most appropriate trading strategy for the sell order of Music Plus shares is:

A. trading in the open market.
B. selling at the closing auction for the day.
C. passive trading over the course of the trading day.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Trade in the open market.** High urgency; the opening auction has passed.
- **B is wrong.** Closing auction suits trades needing NAV proceeds (e.g., redemptions).
- **C is wrong.** Passive trading is for low urgency; the price could revert at any time.

**Key Principle:**
> High urgency → trade now; passive/participation strategies are for low urgency.

### Q344 — Algorithm for the Large, Information-Sensitive Sell Order [QG-16]

**Context (QG4):**
Order is ~20% of expected volume; Wong fears information leakage from a public limit order.

**Question:**
The trade algorithm that Wong should consider for the sell order of Music Plus shares is:

A. a POV algorithm.
B. an arrival price algorithm.
C. a liquidity-seeking algorithm.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** POV follows a volume schedule; it isn't suited to the urgency here.
- **B is wrong.** Arrival price algorithms suit liquid securities or orders under 15% of expected volume; 20% is outsized.
- **C is correct.** **Liquidity-seeking algorithm.** For large orders to be executed quickly without substantial impact and without signaling intentions.

**Key Principle:**
> Large and urgent with leakage concern → liquidity-seeking.

### Q345 — Implementation Shortfall of the West Commerce Sell Order [QG-17]

**Context (QG4):**
Sell 10,000 West Commerce shares; decision price $27.10; limit $26.20; released at $26.90; close $26.00; commission $0.03/share. Executed: 6,000 at $26.80 and 3,000 at $26.30 (9,000 total). Index 600 at release; index VWAP 590 over the trade horizon; beta 0.9.

**Question:**
The implementation shortfall, in basis points (bps), for the sell order of West Commerce shares is closest to:

A. 139.
B. 198.
C. 206.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 139 bp doesn't match.
- **B is wrong.** 198 bp doesn't match.
- **C is correct.** **206 bp.** Execution cost = (−6,000 × 26.80 − 3,000 × 26.30) − (−9,000 × 27.10) = $4,200. Opportunity cost = (−10,000 + 9,000)(26.00 − 27.10) = $1,100. Fees = 9,000 × 0.03 = $270. IS = $5,570; ÷ (10,000 × 27.10) = 205.5 ≈ 206 bp.

**Flag:** The key's bp formula image didn't paste. $5,570 is in the key; the bp conversion (÷ 10,000 × $27.10 = 205.5 bp) is my reconstruction, which matches the key's 206.

**Key Principle:**
> IS = execution cost + opportunity cost + fees; ÷ (order shares × decision price).

### Q346 — Delay Cost of the West Commerce Sell Order [QG-18]

**Context (QG4):**
Sell 10,000 West Commerce shares; decision price $27.10; limit $26.20; released at $26.90; close $26.00; commission $0.03/share. Executed: 6,000 at $26.80 and 3,000 at $26.30 (9,000 total). Index 600 at release; index VWAP 590 over the trade horizon; beta 0.9.

**Question:**
The delay cost in dollars for the sell order of West Commerce shares is:

A. $1,800.
B. $2,000.
C. $2,700.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **$1,800.** Delay cost = side × shares executed × (arrival price − decision price) = −1 × 9,000 × (26.90 − 27.10) = +$1,800.
- **B is wrong.** $2,000 uses 10,000 shares (the whole order) rather than shares executed.
- **C is wrong.** $2,700 doesn't match.

**Flag:** The key's formula image didn't paste; $1,800 matches 9,000 × $0.20. My explanation of the $2,000 option is an inference (10,000 × $0.20).

**Key Principle:**
> Delay cost = side × Σs_j × (p_arrival − p_decision).

### Q347 — Market-Adjusted Cost of the West Commerce Sell Order [QG-19]

**Context (QG4):**
Sell 10,000 West Commerce shares; decision price $27.10; limit $26.20; released at $26.90; close $26.00; commission $0.03/share. Executed: 6,000 at $26.80 and 3,000 at $26.30 (9,000 total). Index 600 at release; index VWAP 590 over the trade horizon; beta 0.9.

**Question:**
The market-adjusted cost in basis points for the sell order of West Commerce shares is closest to a:

A. cost of 249 bps.
B. savings of 50 bps.
C. savings of 68 bps.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 249 bp doesn't match.
- **B is correct.** **Savings of ~50 bp.** Arrival cost (sell) = −(26.633 − 26.90)/26.90 × 10,000 ≈ +99 bp cost. Market move = 590/600 − 1 = −166.7 bp; beta-adjusted = 0.9 × −166.7 = −150 bp. For a sell, falling markets help, so the market-adjusted cost = 99 − 150 ≈ −51 bp, i.e., a savings of ~50 bp.
- **C is wrong.** 68 bp doesn't match.

**Flag:** The key's formula and inputs didn't paste. I recomputed −50.9 bp from the stated data; it matches the key's ~50 bp savings.

**Key Principle:**
> Market-adjusted cost = arrival cost − β × (side-adjusted market move from arrival).

### Q348 — SWM's Trading Policies [QG-20]

**Context (QG4):**
P1: only pre-approved brokers and venues, reviewed and updated regularly. P2: pooling when appropriate, with executed orders allocated pro-rata.

**Question:**
Which of SWM’s trading policies identified by the client are consistent with good trade governance?

A. Only Policy 1
B. Only Policy 2
C. Both Policy 1 and Policy 2

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Policy 2 is also consistent.
- **B is wrong.** Policy 1 is also consistent.
- **C is correct.** **Both policies are consistent with good governance.** Approved broker lists should be maintained (ideally by a best execution committee); partially executed pooled orders should be allocated pro-rata.

**Key Principle:**
> Governance: approved broker list, regular review, fair pro-rata allocation.

# QG5 — North Circle / LIM (Benchmarks, Delay Cost, Market-Adjusted Cost)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG5*

### Q349 — Price Benchmark for the LIM Trade [QG-21] (Essay)

**Context (QG5):**
LIM: fair value $28; prior close $30.05; pre-market $20.34; open $22.15; 10:00 $23.01 (decision confirmed); 10:05 order at $23.09, limit $28; filled 25,000 at an average of $23.45 by 10:22.

**Question:**
Identify the likely appropriate price benchmark type (pre-trade, intraday, post-trade, price target) and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**Pre-trade benchmark** (decision price or arrival price). The decision was based on valuation before the trade; waiting for intraday or post-trade benchmark inputs would let the opportunity expire. Arrival price is typically specified when trading on alpha from a market mispricing.

**Key Principle:**
> Alpha/mispricing trades → pre-trade benchmark (decision/arrival price).

### Q350 — Delay Cost of the LIM Order [QG-22] (Essay)

**Context (QG5):**
Decision confirmed at 10:00 with LIM at $23.01; order instruction and submission at $23.09; 25,000 shares bought.

**Question:**
Calculate the delay cost incurred in trading the LIM order.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Delay cost = shares × (arrival price − decision price) = 25,000 × ($23.09 − $23.01) = **$2,000**.

**Flag:** The key's calculation image didn't paste. $2,000 is my computation using $23.01 as the decision price and $23.09 as arrival; I could not verify it against the key, and the timeline doesn't separate instruction time from release time.

**Key Principle:**
> Delay cost = side × shares × (arrival − decision price).

### Q351 — Market-Adjusted Cost of the LIM Trade [QG-23] (Essay)

**Context (QG5):**
Arrival price $23.09; average fill $23.45; index $2,150 at order entry, index VWAP $2,184; LIM beta 0.95.

**Question:**
Calculate the market-adjusted cost and discuss.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

Arrival cost = (23.45 − 23.09)/23.09 × 10,000 ≈ 155.9 bp.
Market move = (2,184 − 2,150)/2,150 × 10,000 ≈ 158.1 bp; beta-adjusted = 0.95 × 158.1 ≈ 150.2 bp.
Market-adjusted cost ≈ 155.9 − 150.2 ≈ **5.7 bp**.

This is far lower than the arrival cost: most of the cost of buying LIM came from buying in a rising market rather than from the order's own price pressure.

**Flag:** The key's number didn't paste (only the interpretation, 'significantly lower than total arrival cost'). 5.7 bp is my computation.

**Key Principle:**
> Market-adjusted cost = arrival cost − β × market return over the horizon.

# QG6 — Minchow / EEP (Algorithms, Dark Pools)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG6*

### Q352 — Algorithm for the Dynopax Sell Order [QG-24] (Essay)

**Context (QG6):**
Lightly traded shares; no adverse price move expected; scheduled algorithm; minimize impact but priority is complete execution within one day. Options: POV, VWAP, TWAP.

**Question:**
Determine which algorithm is likely and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

**TWAP.** It sends the same number of shares in each time period, helping ensure the order is completed in the specified period. VWAP follows a volume schedule and may not complete when volume is low; POV follows real-time volume and may not complete within the period.

**Key Principle:**
> Completion priority in thin stocks → TWAP.

### Q353 — Dark Pool Comments [QG-25] (Essay)

**Context (QG6):**
C1: a feature of a dark pool is that transactions and quantities won't be reported. C2: a dark pool offers anonymity but less certainty of execution.

**Question:**
Determine the veracity of each comment and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

- **Comment 1: Incorrect.** Transactions and quantities are always reported, regardless of venue.
- **Comment 2: Correct.** No pre-trade transparency gives anonymity; execution is less certain than on an exchange (lit market).

**Key Principle:**
> Dark pools: no pre-trade transparency, trades still reported, execution less certain.

# QG7 — Swanson / Russell (Trade Policy)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG7*

### Q354 — Inappropriate Trade Policy Themes [QG-26] (Essay)

**Context (QG7):**
T1: one optimal execution approach for all asset classes. T2: partial executions allocated pro rata in aggregated trades. T3: same broker-selection principles across asset classes. T4: disclose all trade errors to clients.

**Question:**
Identify two inappropriate themes and justify.

**Model Answer / Explanation:**

- **Theme 1:** optimal execution differs by asset class, liquidity and market mechanism (order-driven, quote-driven, brokered); the policy should describe factors for choosing an approach.
- **Theme 4:** errors and any gains/losses are disclosed to compliance and logged; the priority is resolving them without adverse impact on clients, not disclosing everything to clients.

**Key Principle:**
> Execution approach varies by asset class; trade errors go to compliance and a log.

# QG8 — Morales / Bahk (Trading Risks, Delay and Arrival Cost, Implementation Shortfall)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG8*

### Q355 — Bahk's Statements About Trading Risks [QG-27]

**Context (QG8):**
S1: lower execution risk when a wide-spread security is executed over a longer period. S2: more market impact for trades with higher rates of alpha decay. S3: to minimize information leakage, trade faster on exchanges with market orders.

**Question:**
Which of Bahk’s statements is most likely correct?

A. Statement 1
B. Statement 2
C. Statement 3

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Longer execution means higher execution risk.
- **B is correct.** **Statement 2.** With high alpha decay, trade faster, which raises market impact cost.
- **C is wrong.** To limit information leakage, trade less on exchanges and use dark pools with a mix of market and limit orders.

**Key Principle:**
> Faster trading: ↓ execution risk, ↑ market impact.

### Q356 — Delay Cost of the SRM Order [QG-28]

**Context (QG8):**
SRM: opened $245.00; decision at 10:00 a.m. at $245.10 to buy 50,000; released at 11:00 at $246.50; 46,000 filled at an average of $249.75 (fee $46 total); close $253.00; 4,000 cancelled.

**Question:**
The cost of delaying the submission of the SRM order is closest to:

A. $64,400.
B. $69,000.
C. $149,500.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **$64,400.** 46,000 × ($246.50 − $245.10).
- **B is wrong.** $69,000 doesn't match.
- **C is wrong.** $149,500 doesn't match.

**Key Principle:**
> Delay cost = shares executed × (arrival − decision price).

### Q357 — Arrival Cost of the SRM Order [QG-29]

**Context (QG8):**
SRM: opened $245.00; decision at 10:00 a.m. at $245.10 to buy 50,000; released at 11:00 at $246.50; 46,000 filled at an average of $249.75 (fee $46 total); close $253.00; 4,000 cancelled.

**Question:**
The arrival cost of the SRM order is closest to:

A. 57 bps.
B. 61 bps.
C. 132 bps.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 57 bp doesn't match.
- **B is wrong.** 61 bp doesn't match.
- **C is correct.** **132 bp.** (249.75 − 246.50)/246.50 × 10,000 = 131.85 bp.

**Key Principle:**
> Arrival cost = side × (avg price − arrival)/arrival × 10,000.

### Q358 — Implementation Shortfall of the Trade Stop (TS) Sell Order [QG-30]

**Context (QG8):**
Sell 20,000 TS at decision price $122.60; price $121.90 when received; close $118.00; no fees.
| Trade | Shares | Price |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 11,500 | $121.80 |
| 2 | 2,500 | $120.50 |
| 3 | 3,000 | $120.20 |
| Total | 17,000 | |

**Question:**
The implementation shortfall for the TS order is closest to:

A. –$21,650.
B. $21,650.
C. $35,450.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** −$21,650 has the wrong sign and omits opportunity cost.
- **B is wrong.** $21,650 is only the execution cost.
- **C is correct.** **$35,450.** Execution cost = 2,062,550 vs paper 17,000 × 122.60 = 2,084,200 → $21,650. Opportunity cost = 3,000 × (122.60 − 118.00) = $13,800. Fees $0. IS = $35,450.

**Key Principle:**
> IS = execution cost + opportunity cost + fees.

# QG9 — Miller / BBIM (Advanced Trading, Expanded Implementation Shortfall, Governance)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG9*

### Q359 — Least Correct Statement on Advanced Trading Strategies [QG-31]

**Context (QG9):**
S1: arrival price strategy to avoid adverse moves and capture alpha. S2: dark aggregator algorithms to avoid leakage on large orders. S3: smart order routers to send large market orders for best execution without impact.

**Question:**
Which of Farina’s statements about advanced trading strategies is least likely correct?

A. Statement 1
B. Statement 2
C. Statement 3

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Statement 1 is correct: arrival price algorithms trade aggressively near market to capture alpha.
- **B is wrong.** Statement 2 is correct: dark aggregators suit large orders where leakage is a concern.
- **C is correct.** **Statement 3 is incorrect (keyed).** Smart order routers are for small orders; large market orders would move prices significantly.

**Key Principle:**
> SOR = small orders, best venue; dark aggregators = large orders, leakage concern.

### Q360 — Trading Cost Component of Expanded Implementation Shortfall [QG-32]

**Context (QG9):**
Sell 100,000 SM; arrival price $70.75; close $67.25; limit $68.25. Fills: 24,000 @ $70.60; 31,000 @ $69.60; 20,000 @ $69.40; 25,000 @ $68.80 (average $69.60).

**Question:**
The trading cost component of the expanded implementation shortfall for the sale of 100,000 SM shares is closest to:

A. $102,000.
B. $115,000.
C. $140,000.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** $102,000 doesn't match.
- **B is correct.** **$115,000.** Trading cost = Σ s_j p_j − (Σ s_j) p₀ = −6,960,000 − (−7,075,000) = $115,000.
- **C is wrong.** $140,000 doesn't match.

**Key Principle:**
> Expanded IS = delay cost + trading cost + opportunity cost + fees.

### Q361 — Arrival Cost of the SM Sale [QG-33]

**Context (QG9):**
Sell 100,000 SM; arrival price $70.75; close $67.25; limit $68.25. Fills: 24,000 @ $70.60; 31,000 @ $69.60; 20,000 @ $69.40; 25,000 @ $68.80 (average $69.60).

**Question:**
The arrival cost for the sale of 100,000 SM shares is closest to:

A. 35 bps.
B. 163 bps.
C. 197 bps.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 35 bp doesn't match.
- **B is correct.** **163 bp.** −1 × (69.60 − 70.75)/70.75 × 10,000 = 162.54 bp.
- **C is wrong.** 197 bp doesn't match.

**Key Principle:**
> Arrival cost = side × (avg − arrival)/arrival × 10,000 (side = −1 for sells).

### Q362 — Miller's Governance Recommendations [QG-34]

**Context (QG9):**
R1: fairly allocate partial fills on a pre- and post-trade basis to clients with similar goals. R2: use the same broker and venues for all asset classes.

**Question:**
Are both of Miller’s recommendations most likely consistent with good trade governance?

A. Yes
B. No, only Recommendation 1 is consistent with good trade governance
C. No, only Recommendation 2 is consistent with good trade governance

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Recommendation 2 is not consistent.
- **B is correct.** **Only Recommendation 1.** Partial fills should be allocated fairly (pro-rata) so no account is disadvantaged. One broker/venue won't give best execution across asset classes.
- **C is wrong.** Recommendation 1 is consistent; 2 is not.

**Key Principle:**
> Best execution varies by asset class and venue.

# QG10 — Mulaney / Inflection (Algorithms, Implementation Shortfall, Benchmarks)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG10*

### Q363 — Algorithm for the NGEN Short Sale [QG-35]

**Context (QG10):**
Largent thinks the market underreacted to bad news; sell ~3% of expected volume to capture alpha.

**Question:**
Which of the following trading algorithms did Mulaney most likely use when trading NGEN?

A. Arrival price
B. Dark aggregator
C. Time-weighted average price (TWAP)

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Arrival price.** Adverse price movement expected; wants to trade aggressively; liquid and order under 15% of volume.
- **B is wrong.** Dark aggregators suit orders large relative to the market where leakage is the concern.
- **C is wrong.** TWAP suits no expected adverse moves and tolerance for longer execution.

**Key Principle:**
> Expected adverse move → arrival price algorithm (order < 15% of volume).

### Q364 — Implementation Shortfall of the ACME Trade [QG-36]

**Context (QG10):**
Decision price $79.90 (Tuesday close). Sell 50,000 (Wednesday limit $80.00: unfilled). Thursday: sold 30,000 at $79.50, fees $0.02/share; Thursday close $79.40; 20,000 cancelled.

**Question:**
The implementation shortfall for the ACME trade is closest to:

A. $8,600.
B. $12,600.
C. $22,600.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** $8,600 doesn't match.
- **B is wrong.** $12,600 is execution cost plus fees only (omits opportunity cost).
- **C is correct.** **$22,600.** Execution cost = 30,000 × (79.90 − 79.50) = $12,000. Opportunity cost = 20,000 × (79.90 − 79.40) = $10,000. Fees = 30,000 × 0.02 = $600.

**Flag:** The key's explanations for A and B don't add up: it says $8,600 is 'execution cost plus fixed cost' and $12,600 is 'opportunity cost plus fixed cost', but execution + fees = $12,600 and opportunity + fees = $10,600.

**Key Principle:**
> IS = execution + opportunity + fees (sell: use decision price − price).

### Q365 — Benchmark Against Which the TEXX Trade Looks Best [QG-37]

**Context (QG10):**
Buy TEXX: price at order $50.45; average fill $50.55; VWAP $50.52; TWAP $50.57; close $50.60. Index arrival 1,010, VWAP 1,015; beta 1.30.

**Question:**
The TEXX trade most likely looks best relative to which trading benchmark?

A. Market-adjusted cost
B. Time-weighted average price (TWAP)
C. Volume-weighted average price (VWAP)

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Market-adjusted cost.** Arrival cost = (50.55 − 50.45)/50.45 = 19.8 bp; index cost = (1,015 − 1,010)/1,010 = 49.5 bp × 1.30 = 64.4 bp; market-adjusted ≈ −44.5 bp. vs TWAP −4.0 bp and VWAP +5.9 bp.
- **B is wrong.** TWAP: (50.55 − 50.57)/50.57 = −4.0 bp.
- **C is wrong.** VWAP: (50.55 − 50.52)/50.52 = +5.9 bp.

**Flag:** The key's step formulas didn't paste; it states −44.6 bp, while unrounded I get −44.5 bp (19.82 − 64.36). The TWAP and VWAP figures match.

**Key Principle:**
> Rising market flatters a buy's market-adjusted cost.

### Q366 — Accurate Comments at the Strategy Meeting [QG-38]

**Context (QG10):**
Morrison: average trade sizes rising; EM dark pool volume similar to lit. Shui: exchange-traded derivatives transparency similar to equities; algorithms more evolved in futures than options. Mulaney: OTC liquidity has increased and trade size is small.

**Question:**
Whose comments at the weekend strategy meeting are most likely accurate?

A. Shui
B. Mulaney
C. Morrison

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Shui.** ETD transparency resembles exchange equities; algorithms are more evolved in futures than in options.
- **B is wrong.** Mulaney is wrong: OTC liquidity has decreased, and trade sizes are relatively large.
- **C is wrong.** Morrison is wrong: average trade sizes have decreased and EM dark pool volume is minimal compared with lit exchanges.

**Key Principle:**
> ETD: transparent, algos in futures. OTC: lower liquidity, larger trades.

# QG11 — Holt / Quagmire (Expanded Implementation Shortfall, Trading Options)

*Full vignette: Appendix → QG11*

### Q367 — Motive for Selling New Technology Holdings [QG-39]

**Context (QG11):**
Sector exceeds the prospectus limit; Holt reduces the holdings over two months.

**Question:**
The primary reason for the planned trades of shares in the new technology sector of the UPX Fund over the next few months is most likely to:

A. manage risk.
B. realize a profit.
C. meet cash flow needs.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Manage risk.** Prospectus limits prevent unintended concentration risk.
- **B is wrong.** Profit may result, but risk management is the primary motive.
- **C is wrong.** No indication of cash flow needs.

**Key Principle:**
> Compliance-driven trades are risk management trades with low urgency.

### Q368 — Trading Option for the UPX Technology Sale [QG-40]

**Context (QG11):**
Sell ~20% of daily volume over eight weeks. O1: broker principal for the whole amount; O2: scheduled algorithm over eight weeks; O3: dark pool.

**Question:**
Which trading option is most appropriate for selling securities from the new technology sector of the UPX Fund?

A. Option 1
B. Option 2
C. Option 3

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Broker principal is unnecessarily expensive without urgency.
- **B is correct.** **Option 2.** Per-day sale ≈ 20% ÷ (5 × 8) = 0.5% of daily volume, so minimal impact and no urgency.
- **C is wrong.** A dark pool suits information-sensitive trades; execution isn't guaranteed.

**Key Principle:**
> Low urgency, ample time → scheduled algorithm.

### Q369 — Most Favorable Component of Expanded IS [QG-41]

**Context (QG11):**
UPX sale values: decision price $5,124,300; arrival price $5,175,800; actual execution $5,545,600. All shares sold.

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 1, the component of the expanded implementation shortfall for the trading of the securities from the UPX Fund that is most favorable to Quagmire Funds is the:

A. delay cost.
B. trading cost.
C. opportunity cost.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Delay cost (−$51,500) is smaller in effect.
- **B is correct.** **Trading cost.** Delay cost = −5,175,800 − (−5,124,300) = −$51,500; trading cost = −5,545,600 − (−5,175,800) = −$369,800; opportunity cost = 0. Trading cost reduces expanded IS the most.
- **C is wrong.** All shares were sold, so opportunity cost is zero.

**Key Principle:**
> Expanded IS = delay + trading + opportunity + fees.

### Q370 — Market-Adjusted Cost of ZB's Execution [QG-42]

**Context (QG11):**
| | XTV | Index |
|---|---|---|
| Decision price | 25.45 | 12,750 |
| Arrival price | 25.50 | 12,740 |
| Average trade price | 25.75 | 12,775 |
| Beta | 0.85 | 1.00 |

**Question:**
Based on Exhibit 2, the market-adjusted cost of trade execution by ZB is closest to:

A. 70.57 bps.
B. 74.69 bps.
C. 101.21 bps.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 70.57 is not beta-adjusted (98.04 − 27.47).
- **B is correct.** **74.69 bp.** Arrival cost = (25.75 − 25.50)/25.50 = 98.04 bp; index cost = (12,775 − 12,740)/12,740 = 27.47 bp. 98.04 − 0.85 × 27.47 = 74.69.
- **C is wrong.** 101.21 uses the decision price instead of the arrival price.

**Key Principle:**
> Market-adjusted cost = arrival cost − β × index cost.

# Standalone — Trading and Implementation

### Q371 — Benchmark for a Manager Minimizing Trading Cost [QG-43]

**Question:**
A portfolio manager looking to minimize trading cost is most likely to specify a price benchmark based on:

A. arrival price.
B. closing price.
C. volume-weighted average price.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Arrival price.** A pre-trade benchmark for managers minimizing trading cost.
- **B is wrong.** Closing price is for valuation and tracking error.
- **C is wrong.** VWAP lets managers participate with volume patterns and suits rebalancing.

**Key Principle:**
> Min trading cost → arrival price.

### Q372 — Benchmark for Intraday Rebalancing [QG-44]

**Context:**
Rebalancing with buys and sells over the day; tracking error and short-term alpha are not a concern.

**Question:**
An active portfolio manager is rebalancing a portfolio over the day and has to place both buy and sell orders. If tracking error and short-term alpha are not a concern, which of the following price benchmarks is most appropriate?

A. Closing price
B. Price target benchmark
C. Volume-weighted average price

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Closing price suits index managers and funds managing tracking error.
- **B is wrong.** Price target benchmarks suit short-term alpha seekers.
- **C is correct.** **VWAP.** Intraday benchmark that structures executions so sell proceeds fund buys.

**Key Principle:**
> Rebalancing over the day → VWAP.

### Q373 — Algorithm Ahead of Expected Buying Pressure [QG-45]

**Context:**
Large-cap stock expected to rise sharply; large buy orders expected into the close, ahead of earnings.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager wants to place a buy order on a large-cap stock that is expected to rise sharply in the near term. The portfolio manager expects large buy orders from other market participants into the market close, ahead of the company’s earnings announcement later in the day. The most appropriate algorithm for trading this order is:

A. arrival price algorithm.
B. time-weighted average price algorithm.
C. volume-weighted average price algorithm.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Arrival price algorithm.** Adverse price expectations; trade aggressively before it rises.
- **B is wrong.** TWAP is a scheduled algorithm for orders with no adverse price expectation.
- **C is wrong.** VWAP is a scheduled algorithm for orders with no adverse price expectation.

**Key Principle:**
> Expected adverse price move → arrival price algorithm.

### Q374 — Delay Cost in Expanded Implementation Shortfall [QG-46]

**Context:**
Buy 1,000 shares; price $20.50 at decision, $20.70 at release; total cost $20,750; latest price $20.80.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager places a buy order of 1,000 shares of a stock when the price is $20.50 per share. The price increased to $20.70 when the order was released. The total cost before fee for purchasing 1,000 shares is $20,750. The latest price of the stock is $20.80 per share. What is the delay cost based on the expanded implementation shortfall?

A. $50
B. $200
C. $300

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** $50 is execution cost: 1,000 × (20.75 − 20.70).
- **B is correct.** **$200.** 1,000 × (20.70 − 20.50).
- **C is wrong.** $300 is the paper return: 1,000 × (20.80 − 20.50).

**Key Principle:**
> Delay cost = shares × (arrival − decision).

### Q375 — Costs When No Shares Are Executed [QG-47]

**Context:**
Buy 10,000; system error: nothing executed; price has been rising.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager places a buy order of 10,000 shares of a stock. Due to a system error, no shares are executed. The price of the stock has been rising since the buy order was placed. Which of the following is most likely correct?

A. The execution cost is zero
B. The opportunity cost is zero
C. The implementation shortfall is zero

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Execution cost is zero.** It applies only to executed shares.
- **B is wrong.** Opportunity cost is positive: unexecuted buy in a rising market.
- **C is wrong.** Implementation shortfall is positive.

**Key Principle:**
> Execution cost ↔ filled shares; opportunity cost ↔ unfilled shares.

### Q376 — Market-Adjusted Cost (6 bp Example) [QG-48]

**Context:**
Buy at an average of $53.40; arrival $53.30; index $100 at entry, index VWAP $100.15; beta 0.85.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager executes a buy order at an average price of $53.40. The arrival price at the time the order was submitted to the market was $53.30. The index price at the time of order entry was $100, and market index volume weighted average price over the trade horizon was $100.15. If the stock has a beta to the index of 0.85, the market-adjusted cost is closest to:

A. 6 bps.
B. 15 bps.
C. 19 bps.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **6 bp.** Arrival cost = 0.10/53.30 = 18.8 bp; index cost = 0.15/100 = 15 bp; 18.8 − 0.85 × 15 ≈ 6.
- **B is wrong.** 15 bp is the index cost.
- **C is wrong.** 19 bp is the arrival cost.

**Key Principle:**
> Market-adjusted cost = arrival cost − β × index cost.

### Q377 — Trading Motive Where Alpha Decay Matters [QG-49]

**Question:**
With respect to motivations to trade, alpha decay is an important trading consideration for:

A. profit seeking.
B. meeting margin calls.
C. meeting cash flow needs.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Profit seeking.** Alpha decay matters for active managers maximizing net returns.
- **B is wrong.** Margin calls drive urgency; alpha decay isn't the consideration.
- **C is wrong.** For cash flow needs, liquidity is the key consideration.

**Key Principle:**
> Alpha decay → profit-seeking trades.

### Q378 — Trading Larger Orders More Quickly [QG-50]

**Question:**
All else being equal, trading larger order sizes more quickly will:

A. decrease both market impact cost and execution risk.
B. increase both market impact cost and execution risk.
C. increase market impact cost and decrease execution risk.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Market impact rises.
- **B is wrong.** Execution risk falls with urgency.
- **C is correct.** **Increases market impact cost and decreases execution risk.**

**Key Principle:**
> Faster/larger → ↑ impact, ↓ execution risk.

### Q379 — Strategy for a Short-Term Alpha-Driven Buy [QG-51]

**Question:**
A trader plans to purchase a stock based on a short-term alpha-driven motivation. Which of the following is the most suitable trading strategy?

A. Using dark pool venues to hide the order and minimize the impact
B. Participating in the opening auction and trading any residual using an arrival price strategy
C. Trading the order passively using volume-weighted average price participation strategy during the day

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Dark pools risk unfilled executions.
- **B is correct.** **Trade part at the opening auction, then the residual with an arrival price strategy.**
- **C is wrong.** Passive VWAP trading isn't appropriate given urgency.

**Key Principle:**
> Short-term alpha → urgency: open auction + arrival price strategy.

### Q380 — Futures Implementation for a Large Index Mandate With No Closing Auction [QG-52]

**Context:**
Track a benchmark (5% tracking error); futures have no closing auction; fully invest quickly using derivatives.

**Question:**
An investment manager has received a large mandate to track a market benchmark with a tracking error of 5%. The futures market on this benchmark does not have a closing auction. The client is open to using derivatives and requests that the mandate be fully invested as quickly as possible. The most appropriate trading strategy is to have the manager:

A. send a market order for the futures contract at the market close.
B. trade futures using a volume-weighted average price algorithm into the market close.
C. skip the equitization-via-futures step and instead invest directly in the underlying securities.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** A single market order at the close would have large price impact for a large mandate.
- **B is correct.** **Trade futures using a VWAP algorithm into the market close.**
- **C is wrong.** For large mandates, equitizing via futures first usually reduces tracking error.

**Key Principle:**
> Large futures trades with no closing auction: VWAP/TWAP into the close.

### Q381 — When Scheduled Algorithms Are Appropriate [QG-53]

**Question:**
Scheduled algorithms are appropriate for executing trades when the portfolio managers:

A. are concerned with minimizing market impact.
B. have low risk tolerance for longer execution time periods.
C. have expectations of adverse price movement during the trade horizon.

**Correct Answer:** A

**Explanation:**

- **A is correct.** **Concerned with minimizing market impact.**
- **B is wrong.** Scheduled algorithms are for those with greater, not lower, risk tolerance for longer execution.
- **C is wrong.** They're for no expected adverse price movement.

**Key Principle:**
> Scheduled algorithms: low impact, no adverse view, tolerate longer execution.

### Q382 — Market Commonly Using RFQs for Large Urgent Trades [QG-54]

**Question:**
Which of the following markets more commonly utilize request for quotes (RFQs) for large and urgent trades?

A. Equities
B. Fixed income
C. Over-the-counter derivatives

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Large urgent small-cap equity trades are high-touch broker risk trades.
- **B is correct.** **Fixed income.** Broker risk trades via RFQs (broker as counterparty) are common for small and large urgent trades.
- **C is wrong.** Large OTC derivative trades are generally high-touch agency trades per the key.

**Flag:** The key's explanation for A and C is not parallel to B (it says large urgent trades in OTC derivatives are agency trades, which conflicts with calling them 'urgent' in the stem). I've repeated it as given.

**Key Principle:**
> FI: RFQs/broker risk trades. Large non-urgent: high-touch agency.

### Q383 — Opportunity Cost of a Partial Fill [QG-55]

**Context:**
Buy 90,000 at decision price $29.95; 60,000 executed; close $30.75.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager decides to buy 90,000 shares of a stock, when the price is $29.95. At the end of the day, 60,000 shares are executed and the stock closes at $30.75. If no further attempt is made to buy the stock, the opportunity cost for this trade is:

A. $0.
B. $24,000.
C. $48,000.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Rising price makes the opportunity cost positive.
- **B is correct.** **$24,000.** (90,000 − 60,000) × (30.75 − 29.95).
- **C is wrong.** $48,000 uses the executed shares.

**Key Principle:**
> Opportunity cost = (S − Σs_j)(p_n − p_d).

### Q384 — VWAP Cost in Basis Points [QG-56]

**Context:**
Buy at average $59.80; VWAP $59.40.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager executes a buy order at an average price of $59.80. The volume-weighted average price (VWAP) over the trading horizon is $59.40. The VWAP cost benchmark (in bps) is closest to:

A. 66.9.
B. 67.1.
C. 67.3.

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 66.9 divides by the execution price.
- **B is wrong.** 67.1 divides by the average of price and VWAP.
- **C is correct.** **67.3 bp.** (59.80 − 59.40)/59.40 × 10,000.

**Key Principle:**
> VWAP cost = side × (P − VWAP)/VWAP × 10,000.

### Q385 — Market-Adjusted Cost (17.6 bp Example) [QG-57]

**Context:**
Buy at $50.70; arrival $50.00; index 4,900 at entry, VWAP 4,950; beta 1.2.

**Question:**
A portfolio manager executes a buy order at an average price of $50.70. The arrival price at the time the order was entered into the market was $50.00. The selected index level at the time of order entry was 4,900, and market index volume-weighted average price (VWAP) over the trade horizon was 4,950. If the stock has a beta to the index of 1.2, the market-adjusted cost (in bps) is closest to:

A. 15.6.
B. 17.6.
C. 18.8.

**Correct Answer:** B

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** 15.6 uses the execution price as the arrival cost denominator.
- **B is correct.** **17.6 bp.** Arrival cost = 0.70/50.00 × 10,000 = 140 bp; index cost = 50/4,900 × 10,000 = 102.04 bp. 140 − 1.2 × 102.04 = 17.55.
- **C is wrong.** 18.8 uses index VWAP as the index cost denominator.

**Key Principle:**
> Denominators: arrival price and index arrival level.

### Q386 — Best Execution as Good Trade Governance [QG-58]

**Context:**
Short-term returns; concerned about impact costs.

**Question:**
An asset manager is seeking to generate returns in the short term. If the manager is concerned about impact costs, with respect to best order execution, which of the following is the most appropriate example of good trade governance?

A. Maximizing the likelihood of execution and settlement
B. Obtaining the best price at the lowest possible trading cost
C. Identifying the most appropriate trade-off between nature of trade and speed of execution

**Correct Answer:** C

**Explanation:**

- **A is wrong.** Focusing only on execution and settlement may increase impact and give unfavorable prices.
- **B is wrong.** Best execution isn't simply lowest cost; patient trading can be suboptimal when alpha decays quickly.
- **C is correct.** **Identify the most appropriate trade-off between the nature of the trade and speed of execution.** Best execution weighs price, costs, speed, likelihood of execution and settlement, size and nature of trade.

**Key Principle:**
> Best execution = trade-off among price, cost, speed, likelihood, size.

## Formulas Used in This Bank

**Terms:** w_p, w_b = portfolio and benchmark weights · TC = transfer coefficient · IC = information coefficient · BR = breadth (number of independent bets per year) · σ_A = active risk (tracking error) · MD = modified duration · BPV = basis point value = MD × MV × 0.0001 · KRD = key rate duration · ΔY = yield change · ΔSpread = spread change · POD = probability of default · LGD = loss given default = 1 − recovery rate · RR = recovery rate · ADV = average daily volume · β = beta · bp = basis point (0.01%).

**Index concentration and tracking**
> HHI = Σ wᵢ² (sum of squared index weights). Effective number of stocks = 1 / HHI. Equal weights across N stocks give HHI = 1/N.
> Sector attribution contribution = sector return × sector weight (compare portfolio vs benchmark weights)
> Excess return from active factor weighting = Σ (w_p,f − w_b,f) × factor return

**Equitizing cash with futures**
> N_f = (target exposure) / (futures price × multiplier), where target exposure = policy weight × cash to be invested. Buy when the exposure is wanted now.

**Fundamental law of active management**
> E(R_A) = TC × IC × √BR × σ_A
> Rearranged for breadth: BR = [E(R_A) / (TC × IC × σ_A)]²
> TC = correlation between the active weights the manager wants and the active weights actually held (constraints lower it). IC = correlation between forecast and actual returns.

**Active share and active weights**
> Active share = ½ Σ |w_p,i − w_b,i|. Range 0 (index) to 1 (no overlap). High active share does not by itself mean high active risk.
> Active return = Σ (w_p,i − w_b,i) × R_i, or portfolio return − benchmark return.

**Factor models and variance contributions**
> Return explained by a factor = β_f × factor return. Unexplained (security-specific) return = excess return − Σ β_f × factor return.
> Contribution of factor f to portfolio variance = β_f × Σⱼ βⱼ Cov(f, j); share of variance = contribution / portfolio variance.
> Contribution of asset i to portfolio variance = wᵢ × Σⱼ wⱼ Cov(i, j); share = contribution / portfolio variance.

**Position-size constraints**
> Max position by liquidity = (allowed % of ADV) × ADV in dollars, where dollar ADV = market cap × % of cap traded per day.
> Max position by allocation = (% of AUM limit) × AUM. Max position by index weight = (multiple) × index weight × AUM.
> The binding constraint is the smallest of these. Days to build a position = position size / (daily purchase limit).

**Duration, convexity and price change**
> %ΔPV ≈ −MD × ΔY + ½ × Convexity × (ΔY)². Add the second term for the convexity effect. Higher convexity benefits more in both rate moves.
> Portfolio duration and convexity are market-value weighted averages of the bonds' values.
> Duration-neutral barbell vs bullet: the barbell has higher convexity and lower yield.

**Yield curve and key rate duration**
> Butterfly spread = −(short yield) + 2 × (medium yield) − (long yield). A positive butterfly view expects this spread to fall (the belly yield falls relative to wings).
> Value change from a curve shift ≈ −Σ (KRD_k × ΔY_k) (apply per-tenor yield changes). Active KRD minus index KRD shows curve exposure.
> Position KRD = MD × position / portfolio value (per tenor).

**Rolldown return and total expected return**
> Rolldown = (expected price in 1y at unchanged curve − price today) / price today.
> E(R) ≈ rolldown + E(%ΔPrice from yield view) + E(%ΔPrice from currency). Convert with (1 + R_FC)(1 + R_FX) − 1 where required.

**Currency return**
> R_DC = (1 + R_FC)(1 + R_FX) − 1, where R_FC = foreign-currency return, R_FX = percent change of the foreign currency in domestic terms.

**Immunization and liability hedging**
> Single liability: asset PV ≥ liability PV and asset Macaulay duration = the liability horizon (or asset BPV = liability BPV). Among portfolios meeting these, the one with the lowest convexity (least dispersion of cash flows around the horizon, so a bullet beats a laddered portfolio, which beats a barbell) has the lowest structural risk.
> Multiple liabilities: asset PV ≥ liability PV; asset BPV (money duration) = liability BPV; the range of asset durations must be at least as wide as the range of liability durations (asset convexity ≥ liability convexity). Beyond that minimum, more asset cash-flow dispersion means more structural risk.
> Structural risk = risk from non-parallel yield curve shifts and twists.
> Money duration = MD × market value. BPV = money duration × 0.0001.

**Futures overlay for duration or BPV gaps**
> N_f = (BPV_L − BPV_A) / BPV_f. Positive = buy, negative = sell. BPV_f = BPV_CTD / CF (CF = conversion factor).
> Expecting rates to fall: hold asset BPV above liability BPV (buy more than the full-hedge number or sell less).
> Surplus = assets / liabilities − 1.

**Credit spread measures**
> G-spread = bond yield − interpolated government yield at the same maturity.
> I-spread = bond yield − interpolated swap rate at the same maturity.
> Asset swap spread (ASW) = the spread over the swap curve equating a bond's price to par on an asset swap package.
> Z-spread = constant spread added to each point on the benchmark spot curve to match price. OAS = Z-spread − value of the embedded option (OAS removes option effects).
> Credit spread ≈ POD × LGD (approximately; lower recovery raises LGD and the spread).

**Excess return and excess spread**
> E[ExcessSpread] ≈ Spread₀ × t − SpreadDur × ΔSpread − POD × LGD (t = holding period in years)
> Price change from a spread move ≈ −SpreadDur × ΔSpread. Excess return = excess spread of the position relative to the benchmark.
> Empirical duration = regression of price returns on yield changes; effective duration uses a valuation model.

**CDS and CDX**
> Upfront (percentage of notional) ≈ EffSpreadDur × (CDS spread − fixed coupon). Protection buyer pays upfront when the spread exceeds the coupon; receives when below.
> MTM on bought protection ≈ EffSpreadDur × (new spread − old spread) × notional (gain if the spread widens).
> Curve steepening trade (CDS): sell short-tenor protection, buy long-tenor protection (size by spread duration).

**Value at risk (VaR)**
> Parametric bond VaR = position value × MD × (yield volatility × z × √T). 99% one-tailed z ≈ 2.33.
> Conditional VaR (CVaR) = expected loss in the tail beyond the VaR level.

**Trade implementation and cost measurement**
> Implementation shortfall (IS) = execution cost + opportunity cost + fees (total relative to paper portfolio at decision price). Express in bp: IS ÷ (shares ordered × decision price) × 10⁴.
> Execution cost = Σ (shares executed × price paid) − shares executed × decision price (buy); reverse the sign for a sell.
> Opportunity cost (unexecuted shares) = unexecuted shares × (closing price − decision price) (buy); reverse for a sell.
> Expanded IS = delay cost + trading cost + opportunity cost + fees. Delay cost = shares × (arrival price − decision price) with sign for side. Trading cost = shares executed × (average execution price − arrival price) with sign.
> Arrival cost (bp) = side × (average execution price − arrival price) / arrival price × 10⁴, where side = +1 for buys and −1 for sells.
> VWAP cost (bp) = side × (average price − VWAP) / VWAP × 10⁴.
> Market-adjusted cost = arrival cost − β × index cost, where index cost = side × (index VWAP − index at arrival) / index at arrival.

---

# Appendix — Full Vignettes

## QA1 — Winthrop / Tong (Index Benchmarks, Replication, Equitizing With Futures, Securities Lending)

Evan Winthrop, a senior officer of a US-based corporation, meets with Rebecca Tong, a portfolio manager at Cobalt Wealth Management. Winthrop recently moved his investments to Cobalt in response to his previous manager’s benchmark-relative underperformance and high expenses.

Winthrop resides in Canada and plans to retire there. His annual salary covers his current spending needs, and his vested defined benefit pension plan is sufficient to meet retirement income goals. Winthrop prefers exposure to global equity markets with a focus on low management costs and minimal tracking error to any index benchmarks. The fixed-income portion of the portfolio may consist of laddered maturities with a home-country bias.

Tong proposes using an equity index as a basis for an investment strategy and reviews the most important requirements for an appropriate benchmark. With regard to investable indexes, Tong tells Winthrop the following:

Statement 1:
A free-float adjustment to a market-capitalization weighted index lowers its liquidity.

Statement 2:
An index provider that incorporates a buffering policy makes the index more investable.

Winthrop asks Tong to select a benchmark for the domestic stock allocation that holds all sectors of the Canadian equity market and to focus the portfolio on highly liquid, well-known companies. In addition, Winthrop specifies that any stock purchased should have a relatively low beta, a high dividend yield, a low P/E, and a low price-to-book ratio (P/B).

Winthrop and Tong agree that only the existing equity investments need to be liquidated. Tong suggests that, as an alternative to direct equity investments, the new equity portfolio be composed of the exchange-traded funds (ETFs) shown in Exhibit 1.

**Exhibit 1**
Available Equity ETFs

| Equity Benchmark | ETF Ticker | Number of Constituents | P/B | P/E | Fund Expense Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S&P/TSX 60 | XIU | 60 | 2.02 | 17.44 | 0.18% |
| S&P 500 | SPY | 506 | 1.88 | 15.65 | 0.10% |
| MSCI EAFE | EFA | 933 | 2.13 | 18.12 | 0.33% |

Winthrop asks Tong about the techniques wealth managers and fund companies use to create index-tracking equity portfolios that minimize tracking error and costs. In response, Tong outlines two frequently used methods:

Method 1:
One process requires that all index constituents are available for trading and liquidity, but significant brokerage commissions can occur when the index is large.

Method 2:
When tracking an index with a large number of constituents and/or managing a relatively low level of assets, a relatively straightforward and technically unsophisticated method can be used to build an index-based portfolio that requires fewer individual securities than the index and reduces brokerage commission costs.

Tong adds that portfolio stocks may be used to generate incremental revenue, thereby partially offsetting administrative costs but potentially creating undesirable counterparty and collateral risks.

After determining Winthrop’s objectives and constraints, the CAD147 million portfolio’s new strategic policy is to target long-term market returns while being fully invested at all times. Tong recommends quarterly rebalancing, currency hedging, and a composite benchmark composed of equity and fixed-income indexes. Currently the US dollar is worth CAD1.2930, and this exchange rate is expected to remain stable during the next month. Exhibit 2 presents the strategic asset allocation and benchmark weights.

**Exhibit 2**
Composite Benchmark and Policy Weights

| Asset Class | Benchmark Index | Policy Weight |
|---|---|---|
| Canadian equity | S&P/TSX 60 | 40.0% |
| US equity | S&P 500 | 15.0% |
| International developed markets equity | MSCI EAFE | 15.0% |
| Canadian bonds | DEX Universe | 30.0% |
| Total portfolio |  | 100.0% |

In one month, Winthrop will receive a performance bonus of USD5,750,000. He believes that the US equity market is likely to increase during this timeframe. To take advantage of Winthrop’s market outlook, he instructs Tong to immediately initiate an equity transaction using the S&P 500 futures contract with a current price of 2,464.29 while respecting the policy weights in Exhibit 2. The S&P 500 futures contract multiplier is 250, and the S&P 500 E-mini multiplier is 50.

Tong cautions Winthrop that there is a potential pitfall with the proposed request when it comes time to analyze performance. She discloses to Winthrop that equity index futures returns can differ from the underlying index, primarily because of corporate actions such as the declaration of dividends and stock splits.

## QA2 — Mackenzie Foundation / McMahon (Factor Strategies, HHI, Sector Attribution, Tracking Error)

The Mackenzie Education Foundation funds educational projects in a four-state region of the United States. Because of the investment portfolio’s poor benchmark-relative returns, the foundation’s board of directors hired a consultant, Stacy McMahon, to analyze performance and provide recommendations.

McMahon meets with Autumn Laubach, the foundation’s executive director, to review the existing asset allocation strategy. Laubach believes the portfolio’s underperformance is attributable to the equity holdings, which are allocated 55% to a US large-capitalization index fund, 30% to an actively managed US small-cap fund, and 15% to an actively managed developed international fund.

Laubach states that that the board is interested in following an index-based approach for some or all of the equity allocation. In addition, the board is open to approaches that could generate returns in excess of the benchmark for part of the equity allocation. McMahon suggests that the board consider following a factor-based momentum strategy for the allocation to international stocks.

## QA3 — Camme / Parker (Factor Approaches, Replication Managers, Rebalancing)

Arthur Camme, founder of Camme Consulting, Inc., advises endowment funds and private foundations regarding equity manager selection. Because of the poor relative performance and high fees of active management over the past several years, Camme has been fielding an increasing number of inquiries from clients about the best ways to use passive solutions for equity exposure.

Camme meets with client Sylvia Parker, who seeks guidance in establishing passive exposure for her $400 million family foundation. For a portion of equities, Parker wants to use an index approach augmented by factor-based solutions. She comments, “I hear that factor-based approaches can be used when pursuing risk-adjusted returns superior to that of a comparable market cap-weighted index. In addition,

fundamental weighting can enhance return, but when compared with intrinsic values, it has the disadvantages of overweighting overpriced stocks and underweighting underpriced stocks;

value factor funds seek to lower downside risk; and

relative to cap weighting, single-factor funds tend to concentrate risk exposure.”

Camme suggests to Parker that the first priority in moving to a passive solution is to consider the choices available for index exposure. Owing to the large size of the fund, Camme recommends choosing a replication manager in order to minimize total costs. He explains the differences between full replication, stratified sampling, and optimization when constructing the portfolio. Parker likes the idea of blending stratified sampling with optimization and asks Camme to identify the appropriate manager based on the data in Exhibit 1.

**Exhibit 1 Equity Replication Managers**

|  | Manager A | Manager B | Manager C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Benchmark | S&P 500 | MSCI EAFE | Russell 3000 |
| Number of holdings: Fund/index | 498/500 | 800/928 | 1,200/3,000 |
| Usage of futures | For cash drag | For currency overlay | No |
| Accounts for covariances | No | No | Yes |
| Tracking error | 0.06% | 1.97% | 0.34% |
| Excess return | 0.10% | 2.10% | –0.15% |

In reviewing the relative performance of Manager B from Exhibit 1, Parker makes the following statements:

He faced more volatile markets than the others did, based on the tracking errors.

He used currency overlays to lever the returns of securities held in foreign currency.

His excess return looks like it is more a matter of luck than skill.

Another client is Ken Dashe, the new chairman of the investment committee of the Daventown Arts Endowment. He wants Camme to help him understand the primary investment strategy being followed by the MultiFAK fund, which uses several factors to structure and maintain its large-cap active portfolio. The fund uses benchmark segments of four mutually exclusive sub-categories as shown in Exhibit 2.

- **Active investment philosophy:** Seek excess returns through the use of active equity and fixed-income managers.
- **Allocation guideline:** 60% equity (± 5%), 40% fixed income (± 5%). Cash maximum of 2%.Fixed income is to consist of 50% US Treasuries and 50% investment-grade corporate bonds.
- **Derivative usage:** 1. Cash drag can be addressed through derivatives.2. Allocation guidelines can be achieved with derivatives.3. Notional amount of derivatives shall be limited to 10% of total assets.

Several months later, Dashe and Camme meet again. Dashe needs advice on how to handle a new pressing issue. A significant market correction has resulted in a current asset allocation of 51% equities, 2% cash, and 47% fixed income. These values are now inconsistent with the investment policy statement guidelines. In addition, the tracking error with equity benchmarks has increased because of a disproportional decline in values of some of the actively managed funds. Camme recommends a cost-efficient strategy to address the situation that will put the portfolio back in line with allocation guidelines and reduce the tracking error.

## QA4 — Serinch / PGQ (Top-50 and Top-10 Index Products, Tracking Without Derivatives)

Rick Serinch, a product development director at Pickering Asset Management (PAM), is reviewing the PAM Mega-Cap 50 ETF (PGQ), which tracks the S&P 500 Top 50 Index using a replication approach. This index consists of the 50 largest S&P 500 constituents, is rebalanced quarterly with a buffer, and is weighted by float-adjusted market capitalization. With limited quantitative analysis resources, PAM relies primarily on its trading desk head and external partners in managing the ETF. Serinch has observed positive excess returns for some quarters in which there were no changes to the index constituents, and plans to discuss this with the head trader.

A PAM client is interested in investing in a more concentrated mega-cap strategy. Serinch finds that the S&P 500 Top 10 Index is a suitable benchmark and notes that no US ETFs, mutual funds, or listed derivatives track this index. Selected fund and index characteristics for the PGQ ETF and the S&P 500 Top 10 Index are summarized in Exhibit 1. For reference, characteristics of the S&P 500 Index are also provided. However, the client’s planned $12 million investment is not large enough to justify creating a new fund product. Serinch decides to propose a new “Ultra-Cap 10” separately managed account for the client that tracks the S&P 500 Top 10 Index. In selecting a portfolio management approach for the account, Serinch knows he cannot use full replication, sampling, or optimization to build the portfolio. In addition to these constraints, he also wants to utilize an approach that would not involve constructing the portfolio using securities that match the constituents of the index.

## QA6 — Marketdex (Low-Volatility Europe ETF vs Mutual Fund)

Marketdex, Inc. publishes various equity index products including the Marketdex Low Volatility Europe Index, which tracks 205 stocks from 13 European countries. Stocks from the UK, France, Switzerland, and Germany make up about 75% of the index. Index returns are based on daily closing prices from each constituent stock’s primary exchange (e.g., London Stock Exchange, Euronext Paris) converted to USD as of the primary market close. Currently, there are no listed futures for the Marketdex Low Volatility Europe Index, although the MSCI Minimum Volatility regional indices do have futures available in the US.

Centerpoint Management and Research Company (CMR) manages and markets investment products in the US such as the Low Volatility Europe ETF (LVEU) and the Low Volatility Europe Institutional Shares mutual fund (LVEUX). Both funds are designed to track the Marketdex Low Volatility Europe Index and are managed by the same team, resulting in similar portfolio compositions. Equity futures are used to manage cash flows for both funds, and trades are executed through major brokerage firms using market-on-close (MOC) orders.

For performance reporting, CMR applies the same pricing practices as Marketdex for the LVEUX mutual fund but follows pricing practices required for exchange-traded stocks for the LVEU ETF. Both CMR funds engage in securities lending and use local brokers to trade foreign stocks, with European brokers often offering commission-free MOC trades to attract passive investors. Typical commissions for market purchases average around 0.25%. Key performance data for the two CMR funds is summarized in Exhibit 1.

**Exhibit 1**

| Fund Name | Low Volatility Europe ETF (LVEU) | Low Volatility Europe Institutional Shares Mutual Fund (LVEUX) |
|---|---|---|
| Number of holdings | 98 | 99 |
| Purchase cost (load) | N/A | 0.25% |
| Annual management fee | 0.22% | 0.24% |
| 3-year annualized tracking error | 1.15% | 0.67% |
| 3-year annualized excess return | 0.05% | –0.03% |

## QA7 — Cullen / Stapleton (Funds vs ETFs, Factor Strategies, Subgroup Sampling)

Edward Cullen advises the board of directors of the Sapphire Bay Foundation (Sapphire) regarding all aspects of the investment portfolio of Sapphire’s endowment fund. Traditionally, Cullen drove the selection of active investment managers for the various asset classes. Despite historically ranking well among peers, several of the managers have performed below the level of their respective benchmarks in the past few years. Cullen’s colleague Paige Stapleton recommends that some passive management should be introduced into Sapphire’s investment mix using pooled investments. They agree to introduce the idea to Sapphire’s board at its next meeting.

At the next board meeting, Cullen begins by introducing passive investing to Sapphire’s board. He states that open-end mutual funds and exchange-traded funds (ETFs) are appropriate approaches. Both alternatives are readily available, offer a broad spectrum of investment choices, and are easy to buy and sell. He makes the following comments comparing the two alternatives.

Both mutual funds and ETFs can be purchased on margin.

Investors can take short positions in ETFs but not in mutual funds.

Both mutual funds and ETFs have the same degree of liquidity.

Stapleton then begins a description of factor-based strategies. These include common equity factors, such as value, size, and quality, and they can be used either in place of or to complement market-cap-weighted indexing. She points out that relative to market-cap weighting, factor-based strategies tend to diversify risk exposures; are transparent in terms of factor selection, weighting, and rebalancing; but can be copied by other investors, which can reduce the advantages of a strategy.

Cullen provides Sapphire’s board with an example comparing the performance of the River Valley Fund, a factor-based fund, with its benchmark portfolio (Exhibit 1). The fund uses benchmark segments of four mutually exclusive sub-categories. Cullen calculates the percentage of River Valley’s excess return that resulted from active factor-weighting decisions.

| Factor | Weight | % Return | # of Stocks | Weight | % Return | # of Stocks |

| Value | 0.19 | 5.2 | 27 | 0.19 | 5.2 | 27 |
| Quality | 0.29 | 6.7 | 20 | 0.26 | 6.7 | 20 |
| Momentum | 0.30 | 3.9 | 24 | 0.30 | 4.5 | 30 |
| Total | 1.00 | 5.84 | 94 | 1.00 | 6.06 | 100 |

For the large-cap US equity portion of Sapphire’s investment portfolio, Cullen believes that there are some existing passive indexed-based funds that track the S&P 500 Index that the foundation should consider. Cullen presents Exhibit 2 to Sapphire’s board.

For the international portion of the investment portfolio, Stapleton suggests that Sapphire invest in an MSCI EAFE index portfolio specifically tailored for the foundation rather than investing in an existing index fund. Anne Rowland, Sapphire’s board chair, asks her how this could be accomplished, given that the initial allocation is only $15 million. Stapleton suggests that Sapphire hire a manager to purchase a portfolio of securities that are a mutually exclusive yet comprehensive subgroup of the index designed to track the index return and risk characteristics.

## QB1 — Furlings / Asgard / Tokra (Manager Styles, Value Traps, Activism)

James Leonard is a fund-of-funds manager with Future Generation, a large sovereign fund. He is considering whether to pursue more in-depth due diligence processes with three large-cap long-only funds proposed by his analysts. Although the funds emphasize different financial metrics and use different implementation methodologies, they operate in the same market segment and are evaluated against the same benchmark. The analysts prepared a short description of each fund, presented in Exhibit 1.

**Exhibit 1**
Description of Each Candidate Fund

| Fund | Description |
|---|---|
| Furlings | Furlings Investment Partners combines sector views and security selection. The firm’s head manager uses several industry and economic indicators identified from his own experience during the last two decades, as well as his personal views on market flow dynamics, to determine how to position the fund on a sector basis. Sector deviations from the benchmark of 10% or more are common and are usually maintained for 12 to 24 months. At the same time, sector managers at Furlings use their expertise in dissecting financial statements and their understanding of the corporate branding and competitive landscape within sectors to build equally weighted baskets of securities within sectors. Each basket contains their 7 to 10 highest-conviction securities, favoring firms that have good governance, strong growth potential, competitive advantages such as branding, and attractive relative valuations. The Furlings master fund holds approximately 90 securities. |
| Asgard | Asgard Investment Partners is a very large asset manager. It believes in investing in firms that have a strong business model and governance, reasonable valuations, solid capital structures with limited financial leverage, and above-average expected earnings growth for the next three years. Although the Asgard master fund invests in fewer than 125 securities, each sector analyst builds financial models that track as many as 50 firms. To support them in their task, analysts benefit from software developed by the Asgard research and technology group that provides access to detailed market and accounting information on 5,000 global firms, allowing for the calculation of many valuation and growth metrics and precise modeling of sources of cash-flow strengths and weaknesses within each business. Asgard analysts can also use the application to back-test strategies and build their own models to rank securities’ attractiveness according to their preferred characteristics. Security allocation is determined by a management team but depends heavily on a quantitative risk model developed by Asgard. Asgard has a low portfolio turnover. |
| Tokra | Tokra Capital uses a factor-based strategy to rank securities from most attractive to least attractive. Each security is scored based on three metrics: price to book value (P/B), 12-month increase in stock price, and return on assets. Tokra’s managers have a strong risk management background. Their objective is to maximize their exposure to the most attractive securities using a total scoring approach subject to limiting single-security concentration below 2%, sector deviations below 3%, active risk below 4%, and annual turnover less than 40%, while having a market beta close to 1. The master fund holds approximately 400 positions out of a possible universe of more than 2,000 securities evaluated. |

When Leonard’s analysts met with Asgard, they inquired whether its managers engage in activist investing because Asgard’s portfolio frequently holds significant positions, because of their large asset size, and because of their emphasis on strong governance and their ability to model sources of cash-flow strengths and weaknesses within each business. The manager indicated that Asgard engages with companies from a long-term shareholder’s perspective, which is consistent with the firm’s low portfolio turnover, and uses its voice, and its vote, on matters that can influence companies’ long-term value.

Leonard wants to confirm that each manager’s portfolios are consistent with its declared style. To this end, Exhibit 2 presents key financial information associated with each manager’s portfolio and also with the index that all three managers use.
